AMERICAN FRIENDS BOARD OF MISSIONS

AUTHORIZATION 

Early in 1944, Friends began to plan for an appropriate observance of the fiftieth anniversary of the formation of their board of missions. One of the first factors of such a plan was the decision to produce a new historical survey of all the missionary enterprises of American Friends-but, in greater detail, those sponsored by Friends of the Five Years Meeting. 

Accordingly, Christina H. Jones was persuaded to undertake the necessary exhaustive research for materials and the writing of such a history. A small committee was appointed to collaborate with Christina Jones by way of editorial comment and suggestion.

The following chapters convey to Friends and all readers the thrilling story of Friendly adventure in the world mission of Christianity.

AMERICAN FRIENDS BOARD OF MISSIONS

FOREWORD 

The rounding out of fifty years of service in the mission field by the American Friends Board of Missions is a fitting time to give to the world a written story of the background, the incentives, the programs and the problems of Friends in America, who having caught the world vision of the Master Missionary, have seriously attempted to give it concrete reality in as much of the world as Friends could reach since the Mission Board was organized August 19, 1894. 

It seems more like a special providence than a mere coincidence that Christina Jones came to this portion of the Kingdom at such a time as this to undertake and to carry through such a beautiful task as writing about those faithful souls who caught the vision of world service, and who went out consecrated to carry in word and life the reincarnation of the Master's spirit wherever doors were open. 

In producing this volume she brings to her work a Quaker training and years of service in the foreign field and in the home field as well. She has gathered the essential facts, and with keen insight on a background of practical experience she makes them live for the reader, until as the pages turn there is unfolded before him vivid panoramas of spiritual landscapes, where sunshine and shadow mingle over consecrated lives carrying out the Great Commission. 

Her introductory sketch of the world-wide outlook and activities of early Friends gives a kind of stage background before which all the activities this book portrays carry into concrete and lasting programs the heroic endeavors of those who caught the vision of world responsibility in the seventeenth century. 

Charles M. Woodman

ACKNOWLEDGMENT 

The foreword has stated the purpose and plan of this history so well that we shall not repeat it here. However, we could not send the book out without some words of appreciation to some of those who have helped in the project. 

When I was asked to undertake the task of writing the history of the American Friends Board of Missions as part of its fiftieth anniversary observance, I felt that it might be possible if I had a committee to help me.

The board, therefore, appointed Faith A. Terrell of Wilmington Yearly Meeting, a Friend of wide interests; Milo S. 

Hinckle, pastor of the Friends Meeting at Plainfield, Indiana, and former missionary to Jamaica; and Charles M. 

Woodman of Richmond, Indiana, whose missionary interests began in New England more than forty years ago and who has served the board for twenty-seven years. They have read the entire manuscript, and their counsel and encouragement all along the way have been of inestimable value to me personally, and to the book as a whole. 

Thanks are due also to Charles E. Cosand, former professor of English in Earlham College, for helpful suggestions; to Merle L. Davis, administrative secretary of the American

Friends Board of Missions, for checking the manuscript for historical accuracy; to Martha K. Tubesing, office secretary whose infinite patience and knowledge of where to find source material in the files saved me hours of work; and to 

Ruth Robbins who typed the manuscript, giving it the benefit of more than just mechanical efficiency. Special thanks are also due to my husband, A. Willard Jones, with whom I have had the privilege of sharing in missionary work, and from whom, though we have been separated by vast distances, I have had continued inspiration to keep on with my task.

We have tried to write a history worthy of all those who have helped make it. To them, wherever they have served, at home or abroad, we express our deepest respect and sincere thanks for their inspiration-this is their book. 

Christina H. Jones

CHAPTER I EARLY FRIENDS AND THE WORLD VISION 

A sense of mission permeated the life and thought of early 

Quakerism. From the moment George Fox heard the voice say, "There is one, even Christ Jesus who can speak to thy condition," until his death more than forty years later, he never ceased to share the great truth with others. His followers, likewise, were filled with a burning desire to tell it to all men. The discovery of the power of the love of Jesus to transform souls and lead them from darkness to light was glad tidings of great joy to be told to all people everywhere. 

Like Isaiah of old, it was as if Fox's lips had been touched with a live coal from off the altar and he, too, had heard the voice say, "Whom shall I send and who will go for us?" He said of his call, "I was glad that I was commanded to turn people to that inward light, spirit, and grace by which all might know their salvation and their way to God." 

This great experience came to George Fox in 1646 when he was twenty-two years old. For some time he joyously told it to the people with whom he lived and worked while he continued his trade. However, in 1647, he decided that the time had come for him to give up his work and devote himself entirely to the service of his Master. "I saw the harvest white and the seed of God lying thick on the ground as ever did wheat that was sown outwardly and none to gather it." 

Again, "There was a great people to be gathered." 

The England to which George Fox came was a confused and troubled one. Changes in the monarchy during the previous hundred years had brought about marked changes in the church. These had tended to rob the church of stability and security and religion of much of its meaning. The

people of the country felt a great need of some higher source of authority than their unsettled State Church, and there were many groups of Seekers looking for a religion vital and intimate that could speak to them in a personal way. George 

Fox came to them as one who had sought and found. He radiated in his person and speech the light that burned so brightly within. Here was a leader sent from God with a personality around which they could unite, a teacher who could help them clarify their own thinking, and lead them to the truth.

The first response to the message of George Fox came from a remarkable woman, Elizabeth Hooton. She was then forty-seven years old and the mother of seven children. For some years she had been an earnest Seeker but had not found that inward religion that could satisfy her longings. 

The message of this young man was what she had prayed for and she quickly accepted it. She was the first of the great company of women who were to give the movement its unique character with its recognition from the very beginning of the equality of women. She suffered imprisonment and many hardships for the cause but she never relaxed her zeal. Soon others, men and women in great numbers, were attracted by the message. The next decade witnessed such an ingathering as to be almost spectacular and shows how truly prophetic was Fox's vision of the fields ready for the harvest.

By the middle of the year 1653, twenty-four itinerant ministers came out of Westmoreland, Furness, and North Lancashire, and by 1654, Fox was able to write, “A matter of seventy ministers did the Lord raise up and send abroad out of the North Countries." It seems as if the spirit of the Lord had been laid upon many and that "all the Lord's people were prophets."

The movement spread quickly from the north to the south

of England, then to Wales, Ireland, and Scotland. George 

Fox himself visited every county in Wales in June and July, 1657. Small groups of Quakers were to be found going about everywhere in the British Isles, and everywhere there were hearts ready for their message.

However, this was no insular movement and no limited vision was to keep it within the British Isles. The essential truth of Quakerism that "there is that of God in all men" impelled them to find it in every part of the world and in every race and condition of men. Across the North Sea and the English Channel lay Europe with its kings and its peasants. There were Jews in Palestine, Turks in Constantinople, Ethiopians in Africa, Negroes and Indians in the West Indies, "savages" in the New World, masses of people in China, all to be gathered. In all was the divine potentiality for truth, "that of God," and on these earnest Quakers was placed the divine responsibility of proclaiming it to them. 

Before the end of the first decade Friends had been in Holland which had given a haven to the Pilgrims of an earlier day, France had been visited, and nearly every Catholic country on the continent had heard of this new teaching of the Inner Light. 

From the New World which had seemed to promise religious liberty, came disturbing reports of intolerance. 

Could it be that there was a service to render there in Massachusetts as well as to the Indians? 

In May 1655, Mary Fisher and Ann Austin landed in the

Barbados and remained there for six months. This island was a prosperous English colony but it had a large slave class. The two women found many who desired to know the way and they had a profitable ministry. Barbados became a center where Friends first landed from England and from which they went to other parts of the Americas, as 

Jamaica, Nevis, and Bermuda. From there, Mary Fisher

and Ann Austin went to Boston, only to be thrown into prison and finally returned to Barbados.

Thus ten years had seen the Quaker movement grow from the sincere seeking of a young man to a fairly large group that was to know no limits of persons, places, or opportunities. The British Isles had rung with the message, the Continent of Europe had felt its stirrings, and the New World was within its grasp. Much persecution was to be endured before freedom of religion was to be achieved in the Colonies, but Quaker missionaries felt the call to struggle for it-laid especially upon them.

At the beginning of the second decade the vision was brighter and even more impelling. Friends were making ready now to go to the uttermost parts of the earth. Mary 

Fisher back in England felt a call to carry a message to the young sultan of Turkey. She joined a party of brave men and women who were starting for Rome, Turkey, and Jerusalem. This journey was marked chiefly by hardships and frustrations. Miraculously led on the lonely and dangerous journey to give her message from the "Most high 

God," Mary Fisher completed her mission to the sultan.

The year 1660 marked a special height of Quaker missionary zeal. A minute from the epistle of Skipton General 

Meeting of that year, just fourteen years after George Fox had his personal experience of the living Jesus, shows us how world-minded the first Seekers had become ... 

We have received certain information from some Friends of London of the great work and service of the Lord beyond the seas, in several parts and regions, as 

Germany, America, Virginia, and many other places, as

Florence, Mantua, Palatine, Tuscany, Italy, Rome, Turkey, Jerusalem, France, Geneva, Norway, Barbados, Bermuda, Antigua, Jamaica, Surinam, Newfoundland, through all which Friends have passed in the service of the Lord, and divers other places, countries, islands and

nations, and over and among many nations of the Indians, in which they have had service for the Lord and have published His name and declared the everlasting Gospel of peace unto them that have been afar off, that they might be brought nigh unto God.

They may not have been very clear on geography or understanding of other places, but their hearts were filled with the love of Christ and no hardship was too great for them to bear if God would just let them carry His message. 

The next year, four Friends, David Baker, John Stubbs,

Henry Fell, and Richard Sansthorp felt called to go "towards China and Prestor John's country." (The latter was probably Ethiopia as Prestor John has been identified as the Christian king of that country.) They carried letters from George 

Fox, mostly in English and Latin to "the King of Spain, the King of France, the magistrates of Malta, the Turk, the Emperor of China, Prestor John," and one addressed "to all nations under the whole heavens." What a proud message they had, this glorious message of the love of God, one for kings as well as every humble citizen. One can but marvel at such a sense of responsibility and vision. 

We appreciate the sense of urgency the early Quakers felt when we realize that by 1660, Friends had visited all the existing new colonies in America, excepting Connecticut. Three Friends, William Robinson, Marmaduke

Stephenson, and Mary Dyer had given their lives in the struggle for religious freedom, and the brief but sad story of intolerance was almost over in the New World. The first New England monthly meeting was established in 

Sandwich, Massachusetts, in 1660, and New England Yearly Meeting held its first session in the friendly State of 

Rhode Island in 1661.

We can hardly estimate how great a debt we owe to the intrepid missionaries who challenged the cruel laws passed

195 

 against them by the Puritans in Massachusetts. To the names above must be added the name of William Leddra who was put to death in 1661. It was tragic to find the intolerance the colonists had been trying to escape implanted in the new soil, and it was a great service made at great sacrifice to have the issue faced and resolved so early in its history. 

Quakers lured by stories of the wonders of the New World, had been settling in small groups in Maryland and Virginia and other places in America, but the first Quakers came chiefly as missionaries. Even in a period of religious persecution in England, Friends did not flee to the New World to escape it. Rather were they urged to remain in England and face it. The greater immigration of Quakers was to come with the settlements of Pennsylvania and the Jerseys.

George Fox felt a definite call to visit the Quaker groups in the colonies in 1671. He had already travelled much in the British Isles and Europe between periods of imprisonment but the isolated followers in America called to him. A number of Friends accompanied him including seventy-two-year-old Elizabeth Hooton who felt that she could help Fox by "doing the best that is required for him."

The party arrived first in the Barbados where they had much service. Seven weeks in Jamaica followed where "there was a great convincement and many received the Truth." He "settled several Meetings and brought Friends into pretty good order." The visit to Jamaica was saddened by the death of Elizabeth Hooton, although George 

Fox wrote of her going "in peace like a lamb, bearing testimony to truth at her departure.' 99 The journey from Jamaica to Maryland took six or seven weeks, due to rough seas. Fox found a splendid opening there. A large meeting had been called at West River of all Friends to take leave of a Friend who was returning

to England. Many notable people were there to whom 

George Fox was able to speak. This was in 1672 and that meeting marks the beginning of Baltimore Yearly Meeting. 

From there the party travelled to New England which was a hard, difficult journey over rough roads, across creeks and rivers, through unfriendly forests, but they were marvelously preserved. Fox especially welcomed openings to speak with Indians with whom he seemed to find communion easy. 

He visited Oyster Bay and Flushing, Long Island, and went as far as Rhode Island. He returned through the Jerseys and Delaware to Maryland, holding meetings wherever he went and helping with the discipline. He was then in his prime, forty-eight years old and physically fit. He needed all his energy for he estimated that he had travelled 16,149 miles on this journey. When he reached home he was able to say, "Friends in those parts are well established in the Truth."

Rufus M. Jones in Quakers in the American Colonies says that as a result of this missionary journey George Fox left in America "a strong group of Friends stretching with some breaks from the coast of New Hampshire to Albemarle Sound in the Carolinas and having accomplished a piece of colonial missionary labor which, as far as I know, no visitor to America in colonial times paralleled." The visit of 

Fox and William Edmondson to North Carolina "was the first organized effort of any kind to carry the religion of 

Christ into North Carolina." Fox is credited by Robinson in his History of Modern Missions with being among the first of the Protestants to grasp the idea of foreign missions.

The missionary journeys of Friends to Europe and the Near East did not succeed so well, resulting mostly in costly expenditure of the ones who undertook them. When we realize how difficult it was to travel, how little Friends un-

derstood about the geography, language, and climate of all these countries, we marvel that anything was accomplished at all. But the selflessness and the heroism of these missionaries and the utter joyousness of those bearers of the Truth in their gospel is a precious heritage and we are humbled before it.

Through all this period, Swarthmore Hall was a sort of 

Friends Center. George Fox visited it in 1652 and was received warmly. Margaret Fell was the mother of a large family, a woman of great influence in the countryside, and of great gifts. In his History of Quakerism, Elbert Russell says of it: "Swarthmore Hall became the unofficial center of the Quaker Movement. Margaret Fell and her daughters kept in touch with the leaders by means of the travelling ministers and by correspondence. They wrote her of their needs, persecutions, imprisonments, successes, and prospects, suggested fields to be occupied or opened up places where the work should be strengthened. Funds were raised at Swarthmore and at Kendal to pay the expenses of travelling ministers and care for their families. In these earliest years the easiest way to find or communicate with these 

Quaker apostles often was to communicate with Swarthmore Hall. In this way Margaret Fell became the 'mother of Quakerism.""

It would be pleasant and inspiring to pause here to read more in detail the stories of the first Quaker missionaries. 

They have been told in many ways by different writers and are wonderful tales. It is, however, our purpose to understand if we can what was their secret, or the impulse that sent them out risking all for their Savior, and wherein lay their success.

In the first place they had a message. This message was incredibly simple but unbelievably wonderful. To the troubled soul, they could say, "There is one, even Christ

Jesus, who can speak to thy condition." What comfort and assurance and love lay in these words! In every one, humble or exalted, criminals or oppressors, in every race and condition of men "there was that of God." In that discovery lay the divine possibilities for good and truth and righteousness. It held the hope of the brotherhood of man. If it was true, and these men held it so, it would do away with ugliness and sin, with war and slavery, for brother cannot fight brother, nor can brother enslave brother. A new world would be born when men realized this. Therefore, it was an urgent message and the first Friends felt that deep in their hearts. It gave the movement a radiance and an energy that could "move mountains" as the followers sought to show others that ocean of love that lay above the ocean of darkness that covered men's lives. 

This message was a living one. They had cast off the forms of religion that had almost stifled its life-blood and had found at its core the living Jesus. He dwelt in their hearts, changing their lives, bringing peace, giving them tremendous power. In the harshest periods of persecution, they seemed almost oblivious to physical suffering, so powerful was the reality of the spiritual resources within them. It was a wonderful privilege to tell men of the nearness of God, of his intimateness, and his great love.

Another important feature of this movement was its youthfulness. George Fox was a comparatively young man when he found an answer to his seeking, and his message appealed to young men and women. They gave the movement all the vigor and enthusiasm of their youth. It spoke also to people of maturer years who gave it that fine balance and stability so essential to the success of any adventure. But one feels especially the spontaneity and freshness of young people in the records. 

Much of the success of the first phase of Quakerism no

doubt lay in its leadership. It is remarkable that George 

Fox never lost the regard of his followers through the fortyfive years of his ministry. The Quaker movement attracted many men in his own lifetime with more education and higher intellectual attainment, and men and women from different social backgrounds than his own, but he remained their leader to the end. Singularly enough, when he died the Society of Friends was so organized that there was no disintegration. Like the sincere, humble, wise person he was, he had helped organize the large group of Friends that had gathered in such a short time into a society where no one person held all the power. It was the essence of democracy in form and spirit with loving concern for the individual at its heart, a combination basic for any religious society. He gave an example of living that was above criticism and suspicion.

That, in brief, is part of the picture of seventeenth cen tury Quakerism. The story we shall tell in the following pages had its foundations there, although a long time was to elapse before the second period of missionary zeal was to be re-awakened in the soul of Quakerism. The second period will take a different form. We may be too near it to survey it with the right perspective, but we shall try to see how the basic or eternal truths of Quakerism found expression in a changing world. As it unfolds, we pray that we may see. how this movement grew so that, perhaps, out of a study of the past, may come a vision of the future.

CHAPTER II THE RE-AWAKENING 

If seventeenth and eighteenth century America will be remembered as the age of colonization, expansion, and consolidation, nineteenth century America will be remembered, in part, as a century of great spiritual awakening and outreach.

At the turn of the century, life in the United States was beginning to take form and show some unity and cohesion. 

There were dark days ahead when wars would engage the nation, and there was land to be gained, but there were people in this country whose roots had been in the soil for almost two hundred years. Their desires for political independence from Great Britain had culminated in the War for Independence. Unity within the States had taken form in the new government they had but recently established "with liberty and justice for all," based on the rights of man "to life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness." The doors of the new country were open wide to the oppressed, the adventurous, and the idealists of the old world with its kings and potentates. There was a spirit of youthful idealism and enterprise in the very air they breathed and an assurance that they had found the ideal government. The democratic ideals of the new government were just what the world needed and should be shared. This became a call to preach the gospel of liberty to all mankind.

The sense of independence and individualism which was a characteristic of the settlers had changed their outlook on religion. There had been a decided let-down in religious interest as men had turned away from the harsher aspects of Puritanism or the forms of the older churches, with noth-

22 ing to take its place. With a more settled life and stable government, however, men and women began to feel the need of the church. The mood of the country was changing and early in the century the religious revival was felt in the villages and the cities and colleges of America. This was to gain momentum and reach its greatest height under the evangelism of Moody and Sankey and others about 1870, but the country had been growing steadily toward it through the preceding years.

The missionary impulse of the century gained impetus and took form as a result of the famous haystack meeting at Williams College in New England in 1806. Five young college students who were deeply religious were in the habit of meeting in some secluded place off the college campus for prayer, meditation, and discussion. One hot 

August afternoon while they were in one of those sessions, a thunderstorm came upon them. They sought shelter in a haystack, and as the thunder roared and the lightning flashed, they continued their meeting. After the storm subsided, four of these young men returned to the campus completely dedicated to the cause of foreign missions. One wonders if after the thunder and the lightning, the still small voice of God spoke to them as he had to one centuries before on a mountain in Sinai. There was no question of the sincerity of their convictions, for four years later they applied to the Congregational Church for service in the foreign field. 

That was in 1810, and as a result, the first board of foreign missions in the United States was organized to care for them. Within fifteen years, every major denomination in the country had a Mission Board. Religion now had a respected place on the campuses of America, and the country as a whole became increasingly religious and missions conscious.

Along with the religious revival, a new interest in educa-

The Re-awakening 

23 tion developed. All over the country denominational colleges sprang up, each one bearing its denominational stamp clearly and proudly. Religious emphasis predominated in the curriculum, and the religious organizations, such as the 

Young Men's and Young Women's Christian Associations and the Student Volunteer Bands, attracted large numbers of student leaders. Literally thousands of young men and women from these colleges volunteered for service abroad. 

They went from the settled homes of the New England states and the pioneer homes of the Middle West, motivated alike by the spirit of high adventure for Christ under the conviction that His was the power that could save the world as it transformed the souls of men. 

By the middle of the century, the evangelical movement with its missionary emphasis had attained great power. The 

Society of Friends felt the stirrings of the movement, too. 

Quaker communities were in the heart of many centers where revival meetings were held, and Friends were brought under the influence of the movement. They were further affected by the travelling ministry of Friends which had become more common as travel facilities were made more easily available. There was a continuous exchange of visits among Friends in America and between English and American Friends. Some of the English Quakers had come under the influence of the evangelical movement in the British Isles and were preachers of great power. They came to this country under a great urge to give expression to their convictions. 

However, the earliest Quaker evangelists were influenced largely by leaders of other denominations. For example, Sybil Jones, who with her husband, Eli Jones, made a profound impression on American Quakerism in the middle years of the century, always acknowledged a great debt to a Methodist minister of Augusta, Maine. Elbert Russell, in his

History of Quakerism, says that only two of the influential American Friends of that period, Allen Jay and Luke Woodard, were converted wholly by influences at work within the Society of Friends. Many new Quaker leaders were drawn from other denominations, adding new life and bringing about a quickening within the Society itself. 

One unfortunate aspect of the evangelical movement, however, was that it brought about a number of separations which exist to this day, and which overshadowed a conservative element in Quakerism which was needed because of its link with its past history, literature, and principles. These conservative Friends looked askance at the extreme radical elements which were beginning to dominate the local meetings. Indeed, they seemed far removed from Quaker thought and practice, especially the latter.

On the other hand, young people were impatient with the inertia which had settled on the Quakers, and while they were proud of the high esteem in which the Society of Friends was held, they wanted to be more active in service. 

They realized that Friends had not been without witnesses of their fundamental beliefs and took great satisfaction in the work of those who had tried to express them by attempting to correct the political, economic, and social evils of their times. The present called to them to follow their example. 

William Penn in the seventeenth century had sought to set up the perfect government in Pennsylvania, calling his own city "The City of Brotherly Love." His relations with the Indians had given a new pattern for dealing with them and set his seal on all friendly future relations with the "savages" as the people of the times called them. 

John Woolman, through the middle years of the eighteenth century, had stirred the conscience of American Friends on the issue of slavery. He himself lived a life of such integrity and saintliness that the fragrance lived on.

The Re-awakening 

25 Only recently Elizabeth Fry had died after having done as much to correct the terrible conditions of the prisons of England and the Continent as any other person. Her fame as a prison reformer was as well known in America as in her native England and her memory is cherished. 

Friends were glad for these and other great souls. They faced issues that appealed to them and gave practical form to their religious principles. No, they had not been without witnesses in the past two hundred years, but the first, fine exultant mood of the first fifty years of Quakerism had settled into a calm, quiet state of security, serenity, and impeccable respectability, and Friends as a whole had entered into what the historians of the Society have called a period of 

Quietism. 

The spirit of the new era was of life and adventure. The threat of war made Friends re-examine their peace testimonies; the growth of alcoholism was a challenge to their practices of sobriety and temperance in all things, and foreign missions began to have a strong claim on them. 

Quakers' earliest venture in the field of foreign missions was so closely associated with similar interests of English Friends that we must pause to see what was happening in England a little less than one hundred years ago, also. 

Nineteenth century England was empire conscious, and as the deplorable living conditions of some of the people brought under the flag became known, there was a sense of responsibility for them among church people. Kipling's famous "white man's burden" phrase aptly expresses the feelings of large numbers of the British people of that century. The Church of England felt this, also, and early organized great missionary societies. 

English Friends, too, felt a concern for the acts of their government affecting the millions of underprivileged people who were now part of the empire. As early as 1833, Daniel

Wheeler made a voyage to the South Sea Islands, visiting mission stations from Tasmania to the Sandwich Islands. 

Even before this, James Backhouse went to Australia and the adjoining islands because of a concern for the convicts of the penal colonies. He remained with them for ten years. 

English Friends generally felt a deep concern for the thousands of English men and women who were sent to the distant lonely country on convict ships. A number of Friends went there through the years on individual concerns. They took a very active part in the movement for the abolition of slavery throughout the empire. They were also greatly disturbed over the stories of the Sepoy Rebellion in India. 

The result of the travels of these concerned Friends was a sincere appreciation of the work of missionaries with which they came into contact everywhere they went, and they came to feel that Friends should carry their share. 

This was brought to English Quakers at a conference in Ackworth in 1860 and again in 1861 and after much discussion was approved. Four years later, in 1865, a Provisional 

Committee on Foreign Gospel Service was formed. In 1870 this became the Friends Foreign Mission Association. 

Rachel Metcalfe was sent to India in 1866. Although she was supported by Friends, she served under the London Missionary Society in Benares until a separate work was possible.

In 1869, English Quakers were invited to help with mission work in Madagascar, where they still maintain a vital mission station. We shall see now how their work influenced the missionary effort of Friends in the United States.

While English Friends were reaching out, individuals in America were seeking opportunities for service beyond their borders, also, but there were no organizations to help them. 

Sybil Jones seems to have been the first of the American

The Re-awakening 

27 Friends to respond to a call to go on a foreign missionary journey. This call was to go to Liberia, the Negro republic in West Africa. This place was of special interest to Americans who thought it might be the answer to the vexed question of slavery which was even then (1850) hanging so ominously over our nation. A great deal of missionary activity was going on there, and as the call deepened in insistence and Sybil Jones felt she must answer it even at great personal sacrifice, Eli Jones informed their meeting that he felt that it was his duty to accompany her on the very difficult mission. They sailed from Baltimore in July, 1851. 

Much of their service was to give their message of love to the people of the young republic and to encourage and inspire the missionaries at work among them. This journey and visitation took about six months. 

The next record we have is of a missionary journey of Joel and Hannah Bean of Iowa, then a part of Indiana Yearly Meeting. They asked to be liberated according to the manner of Friends "to visit in the love of the Gospel the inhabitants of the Sandwich Islands, and to reside for some time among them." They sailed from New York in June 

1861, and reached Honolulu two months later. They received a warm welcome from missionaries of the American Board who opened the way for them to speak to large numbers of natives. They were urged to remain and open a school for girls, but these Friends did not feel the call to that service. They returned home after nine months, and their stories and trophies did much to widen the horizon of Indiana Yearly Meeting.

In 1867, we find Sybil Jones again under a concern for religious service abroad. Between 1851 and 1867, she had gone on many journeys at home and to the British Isles and the Continent. Now the call was especially to go to Palestine and Syria. Though in frail health, she undertook the long,

trying journey, her husband at her side as usual, as the mission promised to be difficult. In Syria, as in other countries, they found a vigorous missionary work going on, and on their first visit they spent their time bringing encouragement and inspiration to every mission and mission school in the country. They had to return to England after a few months because of Sybil Jones' health, but as her strength came back and she did not feel released from the call, they made a second journey to the Near East early in 1869. On this visit they left money with their interpreter, Joseph Hishmeh, to open a small school for girls in the village of Ramallah. This was the beginning of the mission work of 

Friends in Bible Lands. It was on this visit, also, that they met Theophilus Waldemeier, who started a very excellent work for Friends in Syria in 1873. 

It was fortunate indeed that English Friends had a committee for foreign gospel service in 1869 as they were prepared to assume responsibility for the school in Ramallah when Sybil Jones told them about it. They enlarged the work and maintained it until 1888 when American Friends took it over.

In 1867, two other American Friends came forward with a concern for foreign mission service. They were Louis and 

Sarah Street of Indiana and were the first American Friends to consider serving for a long time in a special field. The yearly meeting was not prepared to care for them, so they appealed to the Provisional Committee of Foreign Gospel Service in England and were accepted for service in Madagascar. The opportunities on the island were unusual in scope and timeliness. Soon after their arrival in 1869, the new queen became a professed Christian and made Christianity the religion of Madagascar. The London Missionary 

Society was already at work and welcomed the assistance of Friends gratefully. At first they worked in the same field,

The Re-awakening 

29 but as the load increased they very sensibly divided the work, and Friends were assigned to a special area. They established two large schools in Tananarivo, the capital. Soon there radiated from these two schools eighty-five smaller ones, and eventually one hundred and eight congregations. 

Rarely has any mission group been faced with such an opening, and Friends labored hard. This mission is still maintained by English Friends, and the Boys' School in Tananarivo is the largest Friends School in the world today. 

In 1869, two other Indiana Friends offered themselves to the English Committee for service in India. They were 

Elkanah and Irene Beard. They were accepted and joined

Rachel Metcalfe in India. Their appointment made it possible for English Friends to establish their own mission stations at Hoshangabad and Bhopal. The Beards had to give up the work after three years on account of their health, but the work continued.

Splendid as these efforts were, they were at best sporadic and unorganized. While many Friends in America were in complete sympathy with the concerns of these Friends, there had been no effort to give them continuity or to set up permanent institutions. They had not come to the place where they realized that the element of time and long periods of living with people are essentials to really constructive effort. A few did know it and preach it, but for several reasons Quakers to a large extent were not ready. 

The Quietist tradition still persisted among Friends beyond the middle of the nineteenth century. As a whole they distrusted "creaturely" activity. Again, Quakers as a body were not converted to a "hireling" ministry, and evangelical Friends were moving quickly that way. The missionary movement was a step in the same direction, as missionaries would have to be supported. They also feared proselytism, which seemed to them basic in the missionary enterprise!

These fears were very real to a Society which had come to pride itself on just the opposite principles for so long. They did not know that they had drifted into a passive state where they did not even publish the truth that was in them. They had a precious message for the world, but they kept it to themselves fearing any change. A religion which has no outlet is a dying one. The Society of Friends was living but certainly not growing. There were the additions by birthright membership but that was all. It seems hard for us to understand how some Friends came to regard Quakerism as a closed interpretation of Christianity entrusted to the descendants of that first group of the seventeenth century but unconsciously that attitude developed. Thus it was, that while the rest of the Christian Church was reaching out to the ends of the earth, Friends were looking on with mixed feelings, some treasuring their traditions-others eager to find avenues of service.

However, the current was strong and it was impossible for Friends to keep out of the stream that was influencing the rest of the nation. Travelling ministers were aware of the weaknesses of the Society as they saw the joy of others in Christian service and the benefits to foreign lands of the schools, hospitals, and churches missionaries established. 

They appreciated the unselfish devotion of men and women of all faiths. Naturally, they urged Friends to enter into the task of bringing in the Kingdom by reaching out with their message. Were there not still Seekers to whom the 

Quakers could speak with conviction? There were millions living in want and ignorance and disease. Would it be un- 

Christian to bring hope and light and healing to those millions? The first Quaker missionaries had gone out in the joy of the truth which they had found which was for the healing of the nations. They had gone unprepared for the dangers and difficulties of travel, knowing only that they had a

The Re-awakening 

31 call. Were their descendants less brave, less consecrated?

The evangelical movement had its greatest response in the Middle West and since the missionary enterprise was an expression of it to a large extent, it was natural that the first Quaker missionary organization was established there. In 

1870 a Foreign Missionary Society was organized among Friends of Indiana Yearly Meeting. Four of their members had been compelled to go to England to have their concerns recognized and openings made. If others were going to offer themselves for missionary service, this yearly meeting at least would be ready to consider their appeals. They were sure of Divine leading in this matter when they learned of the call of Samuel A. Purdie to work in Mexico soon afterwards. Samuel Purdie had been preparing himself for this service for some time—this organization was God's answer to his prayers. The inspiring story of his work in Mexico will be told in a later chapter. 

In addition to the "missionary education" activities of the travelling ministers, the journeys of Eli and Sybil Jones, Joel and Hannah Bean, and the missionary work of the Streets and the Beards under the English committee, the evangelical movement and religious revivals, the influence of the other denominations and the infusion of new blood into the lifestream of the Society of Friends, two other factors entered into the awakening of the Quakers in the second half of the century.

The question of slavery had split the nation and for four years a bitter war was fought around it. This event had been a severe test of Quaker principles and Friends had been divided, not on the issue of the evil of slavery on which they were fully united, but on the manner of abolishing it. Many 

Quakers joined the Union forces and fought. Others suffered great personal loss and persecution. For a long time 

Friends engaged in the activities of the Underground Rail-

way along with other denominations. The Society had been involved in one way or another all through the war. When it ended, their sense of responsibility for the Negroes brought them into association with societies for their care and into schools for their education. They were not alone in their care for the Negroes and it was good for them to meet with people from other churches and work with them. 

Friends' traditional friendship for Indians had unique recognition by President Grant in 1867. The bitter conflict from which the nation was beginning to recover had made men and women realize that in the new country so idealistically founded there were grave evils. Consciences were becoming sensitive and tender. Slavery was abolished, but there was an Indian problem. Heretofore, the government had pursued a harsh policy in dealing with the Indian and there were those who even advocated extermination. By 1867, the citizens of the New World were tired of this policy and asked for something better. Men close to the president were urging that milder methods might produce better results, that the war policy be replaced by a peace policy. 

From the days of William Penn, Quakers had maintained a consistent peace testimony in their relations with the Indians. Against a policy of pillage, massacre, war, and duplicity, their peaceful, honest dealings, and friendly attitude glowed like a pure, clear light in a dark night. The missionary and philanthropic efforts of various yearly meetings throughout the country, and the outstanding service of individual Friends travelling in the ministry of the gospel among the Indians, had earned for Quakers the right to speak out on their behalf.

The year 1867 was almost a crisis year because of the increase of massacres and bloodshed and the despair of the military leaders themselves of subduing the Indians by military might.

The Re-awakening 

333 At a Friends Conference on Indian Concerns held in Baltimore in the fall of 1867, representatives of six yearly meetings expressed their willingness to enter unselfishly and without compensation on any service the government might require of them. At the same time a joint committee of 

Iowa, Indiana, Western, and Ohio Yearly Meetings on Indian

Concerns was formed. They co-operated with the other committee and presented a united appeal to the president on behalf of the Indians, in 1869. Philadelphia Friends, also, were exercised over the situation among the Indians, and sent a committee to wait on the president-elect. 

President Grant received them all cordially and responded by asking Friends to send him a list of names of Friends to serve as Indian agents, assuring them of his complete support in any attempts they might make to Christianize the Indian. As a result the Associated Executive Committee of 

Friends on Indian Affairs was organized by Orthodox Friends to meet the challenge of the new and important peace policy of the administration. 

These two issues, Negroes and Indians, helped Friends to unite on common and deep interests, and thrust them conspicuously into the stream of American life.

By the end of the century, Friends in America were serving in nine foreign mission fields in addition to the work at home. The story of the development must come in later chapters; we merely record it here. The awakening of foreign missionary interests came later than in most of the other churches but it proceeded with speed and vigor between 1850 and 1900, covering almost the same years of the nineteenth century that the birth of the movement covered in the seventeenth. In each case, Friends were influenced by the state of society and the church. We have seen how the revival was due to many factors and was inevitable. Some of the factors were the evangelical movement which swept

across the entire nation; renewed contacts with English 

Friends who, too, were influenced by a similar movement in the British Isles; enlarged vision of world responsibility due in large measure to a number of concerned Friends who had travelled in various fields of missionary endeavor and had been inspired by the work of missionaries; the increase of interest in the travelling ministry; the influence of other denominations on local groups and individuals; and the infusion of new blood into the body of Quakerism. In addition, the crisis of the abolition of slavery and the Civil War, and the traditional friendship of the Society of Friends for the Indians culminating in helping to work out General Grant's peace policy, all brought about the quickening of the Spirit and opened the mind and soul of Quakerism to the world's suffering and great needs.

Through all the work that developed was the heart of 

Quaker womanhood. Women early heard the call to serve their generation and responded gladly. Their history of complete equality in the educational and spiritual life of the Society made them peculiarly ready for their new tasks. 

They led out fearlessly and courageously. It was the women who first saw the value of unity and they were able to call a great meeting in Indianapolis as early as 1888. Twelve yearly meetings were represented and the report of the addresses given and business conducted makes stimulating reading, today. The work of the Woman's Missionary Union will have a more complete presentation, but we cannot close a chapter on the growth of missionary interest within the Society of Friends in the nineteenth century without a reverent, though brief, mention of their work here. 

The missionary movement of the nineteenth century was also a youth movement. The Christian Endeavor Movement, another manifestation of the growth of religious interest in the United States, played its great part. The inter-

The Re-awakening 

335 denominational aspect of the organization appealed to Friends and they entered into the movement wholeheartedly. They were responsible for the opening of some mission work and the support of missionaries, and assisted with many special projects on the fields. 

By the end of the century, American Quakerism was awake. The awakening had not been altogether quiet and peaceful but there was a renewed interest in Quaker testimonies and history, an awareness of social, economic and religious needs at home, and a care for people of other lands. 

Friends could no longer quietly cherish and pass on a beautiful tradition; the new age called to them to enter into the world's needs and suffering. They had a message, living and glowing within them to be shared, and they were ready.

CHAPTER III THE BOARD OF MISSIONS 

Missions were an absorbing topic of discussion whenever two or three Friends were gathered together during the latter years of the nineteenth century. This was particularly true of women Friends who were so well organized that they could hold a great conference in Indianapolis in 1888. Nor were the men far behind, although they did not feel the need for organization quite as early. There were some who were opposed to foreign missions, but they, though very vocal, were not many. Letters from Madagascar, China, India, Japan, Palestine, Syria, Mexico, Jamaica, and Alaska were read and shared. Comparatively large sums of money were being collected and sent to build schools, hospitals, and churches in all these lands and committees met to discuss the details of administering them. Young people through the 

Christian Endeavor societies were giving money for scholarships for children in the mission schools or for hospital beds. 

In countless ways Friends were entering almost intimately into the lives of people of distant lands. The beautiful thing about it, as about all mission work, was unselfish giving for a cause higher than any earthly one-to bring people into the Kingdom of God. Friends were now uniting with all 

Christians in building bridges of good will and love to unite the races of mankind. Jesus of Nazareth was to be lifted high. He was the Light and the Hope of the World. His message of the Fatherhood of God and the brotherhood of man was to be preached again. It was a happy service. 

Thus it was that at the great General Conference of American Yearly Meetings held in Richmond, Indiana, in 1887, missions had a prominent place on the program. This con-

The Re-awakening 

37 ference, called to study the state of the Society of Friends, brought representatives from all the American Yearly Meetings, London, and Dublin. The awakening of the Society had been accompanied by innovations in worship and interpretations of the scriptures, and ultra-progressive tendencies that called for Friends to "reason together." Migration from the East, especially Pennsylvania and the southern states, particularly North Carolina, before the Civil War, had brought about a decided change in the Quaker population of America—more than one half of which was now in the Middle West.

Indiana Yearly Meeting called the conference, expressing its concern in the following minute: "The importance of our growth and strength in the maintenance of the common faith is such that this meeting unites in believing that it might be right to hold a conference of committees of the American Yearly Meetings to consider such matters appertaining to our welfare, either in faith and practice, as shall be in their judgment demanded, and that no conference shall be held unless six yearly Meetings unite in the concern and appoint committees to that service." Another minute expressed the hope that the holding of such a conference "would strengthen the bonds of Christian fellowship among us, and tend to promote unity in important matters of faith and practice in the different bodies into which Friends in America are divided."

The time apparently was ripe for such a gathering for there was ready response from all the Yearly Meetings and across the sea. Almost every aspect of Christian doctrine was discussed as well as matters concerning the conduct of the meetings for worship and business. Very great issues were faced and important decisions made. 

When the question of the mission of the Society of Friends was introduced, the great commission, "Go ye into all the

world to preach the gospel," was reiterated again and again. 

And as they surveyed their progress in missions within the past sixteen years alone, there was reason to feel that 

Friends were obeying the command.

Indiana Yearly Meeting reported on the mission in Mexico, established in 1871 by Samuel Purdie and his wife. Iowa 

Yearly Meeting had taken over the work begun by Evi Sharpless in Jamaica two years before in 1883; the Women's 

Society of Philadelphia Yearly Meeting had opened a Mission in Tokyo, Japan, in 1885, and just that year, 1887, Kansas Yearly Meeting started work in Douglas, Alaska. While 

New England Yearly Meeting had not assumed official responsibility for any field, they had a foreign missions committee as early as 1873 which had made possible the work of Friends in Brumanna, Syria, and to a certain extent helped the London Committee in Ramallah. They were also interested in Jamaica. They were anticipating the official adoption of one of the fields in the Near East in the very near future. The presence of English Friends at the conference made the discussion of this possible and plans were made for the conclusion of the matter in 1888. J. Walter Malone of 

Ohio reported that his yearly meeting had already sent Esther Butler to China to assist a physician of the Methodist Board until such time as it was advisable to start work for her own board, and that within a month, Delia A. Fistler and 

Esther Baird would leave for India. Matilda W. Anderson of 

Iowa told the Conference that she knew of thirty student volunteers within the limits of Iowa Yearly Meeting. 

Nor was interest in missions limited to the yearly meetings having established work. Friends in Baltimore, New 

York, North Carolina, and Western were giving generously to the work of the various boards. 

Friends from overseas also had a story to tell. They told of work in India begun in 1866, in Madagascar in 1867, and

in Ramallah, Palestine in 1869, fields close also to American Friends, and of the Monthly Meeting in Syria set up in conjunction with New England in 1876. They had the advantage of having had the earliest organization for foreign mission work among Friends even though the board was not an official part of London Yearly Meeting. 

Behind all these reports were stories of schools, hospitals, printing-presses and churches ministering to thousands of needy people in the name of Jesus. The accounts of progress in all fields encouraged them and stimulated them to greater effort.

The words of the historic conference and the historic hour were evangelism, missions, and union. Friends were again zealous to share the message burning within them. If the first missionary work of Friends was concerned chiefly with a harvest in-gathering, this revival was characterized by an awareness of the necessity of plowing and sowing and cultivating the ground for the sun of the Spirit to bring the seed to fruition.

The conference revealed that Friends were clearly united in their belief in missions and under a great sense of responsibility for the cause. However, it was evident that the efforts lacked unity and that much of the strength that could come from united action was dissipated by this very aspect of their efforts. It was not surprising, therefore, to find the following question asked, "Is it desirable that there should be a union of American Yearly Meetings in Foreign Missions?"

The question was not a new one to Middle-western Friends. 

It had been suggested to them by Stanley Pumphrey of England at a conference of Indiana and Ohio Yearly Meetings in 1877. He was a man with a rich spiritual background and ready sympathy, who seemed to enter into the problems of Mid-western Quakers with peculiar tenderness. His knowl-

edge of missionary work was broad. He urged Friends in 

America to enter into a united foreign work and proposed a board for this purpose. A report on the conference says: "Stanley Pumphrey was present and under his leadership the decided sentiment of the conference was favorable to such an organization." Nothing specific, however, was accomplished but the seed of union was planted in favorable soil. 

Ten years had witnessed a change in sentiment in many lines in American Quakerism and the separatist tendencies were giving way to a real concern for Friends to have a united testimony. There were still hindrances to it, but the matter of missions offered one issue upon which they could work together. After much discussion on the question the answer was in the affirmative; "and it was concluded that it is desirable to have one central board of missions whose actions would unify the efforts now put forth by the different yearly meetings and enable them to work more wisely and efficiently." 

Since this conference had no legislative power, its conclusions could be useful only in so far as the membership of the Society concurred. The matter was taken back to all the yearly meetings by the delegates. Obviously there was general interest for at the Quinquennial Conference held in Indianapolis, Indiana, Tenth Month 18-21, 1892, one of the special subjects for consideration was "One Board of Foreign 

Missions for all the American Yearly Meetings."

Five years had seen further development in missions. 

New England Yearly Meeting had assumed official responsibility for the Ramallah, Palestine Mission and had opened a boarding school for girls in 1889; Western Yearly Meeting after having assisted Indiana with the work in Mexico for some years had opened its own station there in 1889; Esther 

Butler started the China Mission of Ohio Yearly Meeting in 1890. There were now seven American Friends Mission sta-

tions throughout the world cared for by seven independent boards.

Much of the burden of mission work was borne by the women's organizations. They had held a conference in Indianapolis in 1888 and the association and inspiration had convinced them of the need of unity. It was in the tradition of the Society for their work to be brought to the men and the Society as a whole, and they were completely united on the issue of one board.

The discussion was opened by Mahalah Jay. She emphasized the need of such a board to give the work direction, stability, counsel, and advice. This could be done, she thought, without usurping the authority of the yearly meeting boards. The associated Executive Committee of Friends on 

Indian Affairs offered a model on which to base the union. 

The discussion continued for two hours. Some few felt that a central board was not necessary as the various boards were doing very well and this would only make additional work for them; others, that it would take away the interest from the yearly meetings; still others, that it would limit the existing boards and interfere with their work. However, most Friends were of the opinion that a central board could serve a real purpose as a bureau to gather information, keep statistics, keep files of candidates, and be informed on the conditions and needs of the various fields. 

During all the discussion, the fact remained that Friends were committed to foreign missions. The problem was how to carry their message most effectively and best care for their fields of labor and those whom God called to this service. The matter was left to a committee to report on later in the conference. This was done and the following report was made (in part): "The committee to which was referred the subject of a Central Board of Foreign Missions, would pro-

pose that the Conference recommend to our several Yearly. 

Meetings, for their adoption, the following plan for the organization of an American Friends' Board of Foreign 

Missions: 

"The Board shall consist of a Secretary, an Advisory committee of three members, and, from each Yearly Meeting, two Corresponding Members. "It shall be the duty of the Secretary to collect information respecting the condition and needs of foreign mission fields, and to learn, as far as possible, the best means of supplying those needs; to obtain from the Corresponding Members full reports of all the foreign mission work carried on by the several Yearly Meetings, or by the members of these meetings; to ascertain the qualifications and preferences of those offering themselves as missionaries; and, in general, to obtain and impart such information from within and without the church as may enhance the success of Friends' foreign mission work. When new or difficult questions arise in the discharge of his duties, he shall consult the Advisory Committee. "The Yearly Meetings shall not be financially responsible for the means to carry out the objects which the formation of this Board contemplates, but the methods of securing the necessary funds for its use shall be left to the Board itself." 

The report required that the matter be presented to the various yearly meetings and that the several persons so chosen constitute a committee to which the further care of the subject should be intrusted this committee to meet as soon as four yearly meetings should adopt the proposition. "The following appointments were then officially reported to introduce the subject of the Foreign Missions Board to the several yearly meetings: Calvin W. Pritchard of Western

Yearly Meeting; Mahalah Jay of Indiana; Hannah E. Sleeper, of Kansas; Anna B. Thomas, of Baltimore; Deborah P. Atherton, of New England; Albert Peelle, of North Carolina; Augustus Taber, of New York; Elizabeth Larkin, of Wilmington; 

Jacob Baker, of Ohio; Lydia Maria Deane, of Iowa; Elias Jessup of Oregon."

Two years passed before the committee could meet and they had been busy ones. The matter had to be brought to each yearly meeting, fully discussed, and the arguments pro and con carefully weighed. Meantime, those who had the question close at heart waited eagerly the action of each yearly meeting. The following extract from a letter written by Mary A. J. Ballard, daughter of Mahalah Jay, for the fiftieth anniversary meeting of the board in Richmond, Indiana, April 1944, will give some idea of the mood of the period: 

1892 has always seemed to me an important date, for then the Five Years Meeting took the definite action that there should be an American Friends Board of Foreign Missions, if the yearly meetings as they came into session took steps individually to become a part of it. From that date on, in our home, my mother's chief concern was to be sure that everything was in readiness for the subject to be well presented in each yearly meeting in turn, and the exciting news of what action that yearly meeting took was awaited most eagerly. 

Then came the great day in 1894 when those appointed to be members came together in the first meeting the culmination of years of dreams, of planning and of work; and yet at the same time just the beginning of the Board's actual service to God, the Society of Friends, and the mission fields established and yet to be established. 

The daughter of that first Executive Secretary of the

Board considers it one of the great privileges of her life to have lived through those beginning days and to have followed the growth through the years;-the beginning of the work in Cuba, when the need was pressing, but no

yearly meeting free to undertake it alone; the gradual transfer of the responsibility of mission fields to the Board; the various changes in Executive Secretaries; the opportunity of being a member of the board; etc. I cannot express how even living over these things in memory, still thrills me. *Nine yearly meetings appointed delegates to meet at Wilmington, Ohio, Eighth Month 20, 1894, to form the American

Friends Board of Foreign Missions. Indiana, Western, Wil mington, and Oregon were represented by delegates; Baltimore, Iowa, and Kansas by letter. John Henry and Miriam 

C. Douglas of Oregon being present at Wilmington Yearly

Meeting represented Oregon Yearly Meeting in the absence of their regular delegates. The other yearly meetings, North 

Carolina and Ohio were not represented although in sympathy with the plan of organization. 

Elwood O. Ellis of Indiana Yearly Meeting served as chair man and Ellen C. Wright of Wilmington Yearly Meeting as secretary at the first meeting. Mahalah Jay of Indiana Yearly Meeting was chosen as the first secretary of the new board and Ellen C. Wright, Wilmington, treasurer. W. Jasper 

Hadley of Iowa, Lydia Taylor Painter of Western, and Carle ton C. Pritchard of Wilmington Yearly Meetings the advis ory committee. Two minutes of the eight that recorded the session are interesting. Minute six reads: "The board shall enter at once as fully as practicable upon its functions as set forth in the proposition from the American Friends' Conference in 1892"; and Minute eight, "This newly constituted board is dependent upon the voluntary gifts of the yearly meetings for the necessary funds to carry on its work." 

The very limited powers permitted it and the unwillingness of the yearly meetings to assure it of any funds do not seem like a very auspicious beginning. Such faith is not often found-faith to carry on against great odds. 

* Minutes of the A.F.B.F.M. 1894

Within a year a modest report was printed. The secretary had answered many requests for statistics, sent information to periodicals, collected information from each board, and put it into a useful form for all. They had received $86.97 in voluntary contributions from four yearly meetings and had spent $26.16, leaving a balance of $60.81. Perhaps never in the history of any other board was there this unusual proportion of receipts and expenditures. No reports were printed for the next three years nor did the board meet. *"The work of the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions being chiefly a bureau of information, as intended, carried on by correspondence for the next several years without the board being called together, and annual reports were made to the Yearly Meetings united in it.” 

Certainly they were limited in scope during the first years. 

Each board guarded its work carefully and watched the new board to see that it did not usurp any of its powers. There were no funds to defray travelling expenses of members for meetings; so upon the secretary lay the heavy burden of carrying on by correspondence. She was increasingly called on to furnish information to them and to other denominational boards.

By 1898 a great event in the history of our nation was to affect America and in a peculiar way the modest American 

Friends Board of Foreign Missions. The United States had engaged in the war against Spain for the liberation of Cuba. 

The war was brief but it ended by the United States defeating Spain and assuming a paternalistic protectorate over Cuba. Friends had been at work in Jamaica since 1881, so were not unaware of the West Indies, but there had been no very great concern for them generally in America. Now the 

Christian forces of America felt the challenge of the Islands. 

*Minutes of the A.F.B.F.M. 1898

A conference was called of missions boards of the United States and Canada to consider the situation in Cuba. Friends were invited to share in this. Their attention had already been called to Cuba by Captain L. D. Baker of the United 

Fruit Company of Boston who was eager to have Friends enter the Cuban field. He promised to assist them as much as possible. The company was starting extensive plantations on the island at the time and Captain Baker was familiar with the needs of the people. 

Friends were interested in Cuba but there was no yearly meeting at this time ready to assume full care of a new mission. Then it was that they began to think in terms of the five-year-old American Friends Board of Foreign Missions. 

But how could a board which was constituted as only a bureau of information and advice, with no budget, undertake such a project? It was evident that Friends needed a board with wider scope and some authority. One marvels at the faith of the first group. If this was the only way to get work started in Cuba, they must work out a plan whereby they could do it. Several yearly meetings promised to support the new mission although they could not promise an annual income. The members of the board were well aware of the possibilities of the Cuba mission as a project in which Friends could carry on a united work.

So it came about that the meetings of the board in Ninth Month, 1899, were momentous. Two important items were before them, the appointing of eight delegates to the Ecumenical Conference to be held in New York at Easter time 1900, and a consideration of work in Cuba. 

A letter from Benjamin F. Trueblood of Boston was read in regard to the Cuba situation. He favored immediate action to secure foothold in Cuba and was himself organizing a committee to act if the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions did not get ready to act speedily. The opportunity

was too good to pass up for want of an organization ready to go to work. Captain Baker of the United Fruit Company was contemplating purchasing a valuable property overlooking Santiago Bay which he would hold for the Mission headquarters. to There were wise heads on the board. Eager as they were go ahead speedily, they realized they must proceed slowly in order to avoid mistakes in their first adventure. They must know if Captain Baker would be disappointed with a small beginning, upon what terms they could have the use of the property overlooking Santiago Bay, what attitude the Catholic manager of the United Fruit Company in Cuba would take to their work, what were the health conditions, whether or not the United Fruit Company would operate in Puerto Rico, an island belonging to our government, and how much money would be required to begin with. Could 

Benjamin F. Trueblood answer these questions? New England had not yet appointed any members to the board. They suggested that Benjamin F. Trueblood and a woman Friend be appointed as honorary members until their yearly meeting was ready to make regular appointments.

The matter of Cuba had to be presented fully to all the yearly meetings as the board could not operate without their full consent. It was brought before Indiana Yearly Meeting then in session, Ninth Month, 1899, and was "fully endorsed by it and its pledge of support thereof was given, the members of the board being present." Some yearly meetings expressed complete sympathy with the proposal but felt that they could not give financial assistance on account of their own obligations.

At the closing meeting of this important session, they recorded the following decisions: "Encouraged by the general sympathy and having now the authority and direction of four yearly meetings-viz: Wilmington, Iowa, Western, and

Indiana, to co-operate in the West Indian work proposedyearly meetings which embrace more than one-half the membership of Friends in America-the Board decided that the authority was sufficient, and that the time had come for it to take definite steps toward organizing for opening and carrying on a united work of the yearly meetings in the West Indies. It was the judgment of the Board, however, that responsibility for the work would not attach to any yearly meeting till by its action it had united in it. "The secretary was directed to inform all the members of the Board of this decision; and ask them for suggestions as to the fuller organization of the Board, and to give their voice as to whether a meeting of the Board should be called soon or not, and, if called, on the time and place of meeting; the purpose of the meeting being to organize more perfectly, and to appoint officers for the next term of service, and to decide upon the work to be undertaken, the method to be adopted for raising funds, etc." 

The minutes are expressed with simplicity and restraint, yet they record a tremendous decision, the result of long hours of prayer, and abundant faith. As the century drew to a close, Friends were facing together a united work in the Kingdom-Cuba was not only a very real opportunity for service, it was a symbol of the spirit of growing unity in the 

Society of Friends.

THE NEW CENTURY 

Friends had been slow to venture into the field of foreign missions. Sometimes their efforts seemed very small in comparison with the work of other bodies, but they were doing their part now and were eager to unite with all Christians in the work. 

The year 1900 opened auspiciously for the Christian

Church throughout the world. With the experience of al-

most a century of foreign missions on which to build, the new century offered limitless opportunity. The United 

States was one of the largest areas of the Protestant Church in the world. The population was approximately 76,000,000 with the Protestant groups dominating the religious life of the nation.

The country was growing prosperous and more vigorous. 

Here in America were wealth, science, industry, education, and the church; an abundance of everything to be shared with all people everywhere. Christian people stood on the threshold of the century and saw the world speedily won for Christ.

The great Ecumenical Conference was called to meet in 

New York on the first Easter season of the century. Months of planning and millions of prayers were behind it. The call to prayer went out, "If this Conference is to aid the church in going forward into the new century in the fullness of the blessings of the gospel of Christ, it must itself be lifted and carried through on a flood-tide of prayer."* Missionary experience had taught the Christian Church that the world needed the message of Jesus and to give it was not a problem peculiar to a denomination but to a united Christendom. 

Friends gladly accepted the invitation to participate in this gathering. Two Friends were members of the committee on arrangements, Micajah M. Binford of New York, and James Carey, Jr., of Baltimore, and these were exofficio members of the Conference. Friends were entitled to send eight delegates on the basis of their contribution to foreign missions. They were careful to insure full attendance at this meeting and actually had more than that number when the Conference met.

The mission board met again at Earlham College, Rich- 

* Ecumenical Missionary Conference, Vol. 1, p. 12.

mond, Indiana, Second Month, Fifth, 1900, to proceed with plans for the work in Cuba. There were present James Carey, Jr., of Baltimore Yearly Meeting, Thomas C. Brown and

Lydia Taylor Painter of Western Yearly Meeting, Charles E. 

Carey and Mahalah Jay of Indiana Yearly Meeting, and 

Zenas L. Martin from Iowa Yearly Meeting. Others who were unable to attend sent letters.

They were ready now to make definite plans for the work in Cuba. Before they could go ahead, however, the question of incorporation must be taken up. This could be done under the laws of the State of Indiana, they were advised. They secured legal advice on this matter and proceeded to apply for incorporation. 

Minute eight of their meeting expressed the heart of the gathering and the first big step of the new board, and must have been recorded with deep though quiet emotion: "It was decided by unanimous vote to locate its first mission in Cuba and engage in work there now, to the extent of the funds at their disposal." 

Zenas L. Martin, evangelistic superintendent of Iowa

Yearly Meeting who was present, was chosen general agent and superintendent of missions and was asked to go to Cuba to gather essential information as soon as possible. 

The question of financing the work of the board arose and it was decided to ask that the contributions come as far as possible through the existing foreign missions organizations of the yearly meetings "that they may have credit for the amount contributed by their respective members, but to take direct contributions also."

Seven candidates offered themselves for the new work. 

After much discussion the board chose Sylvester Jones, May 

Mather, and Emma Phillips, the latter a missionary in Mexico whose experience and knowledge of Spanish would be invaluable in the new mission in a Spanish-speaking coun-

try. She could take with her a Mexican girl to help get the work under way quickly. It was with regret that the board was not able to send all who applied.

An executive committee now being essential, the president of the board, the secretary, treasurer, and a member of the advisory committee were constituted to serve in that capacity. These were in this instance Thomas C. Brown, James Carey, Jr., Mahalah Jay, and Wm. Jasper Hadley.

Their problems were serious ones. However, at their first meeting immediately following the close of the board meeting, they were informed that Penn College Foreign Mission Association proposed to support Sylvester Jones for five years in whatever foreign field he may enter as a missionary. He and May Mather, seniors in Penn College at that time, were married that following summer and entered the work in the fall.

It was evident that the secretary would have to give more time to the work, so a salary of two hundred dollars a year was offered to her for that service.

In a general appeal for funds sent out in Eleventh Month, 1900, the board gave a good account of itself. Four missionaries were on the field opening work in Gibara, eight yearly meetings had made appropriations for the work, Friends in other yearly meetings had contributed, and about six thousand dollars had been contributed and subscribed. Captain 

L. D. Baker had been a generous contributor, also. Four thousand dollars was needed within thirty days for much needed buildings "to save expensive rent and convince the people of our intention to remain with them."

When application was made for incorporation they discovered that there was no law in the State of Indiana applicable to a missionary board composed of residents of different states. Suitable legislation was necessary. Consequently a bill was introduced into the Legislature, in session
