in 1901, and the enactment of a law permitting such incorporation was passed on March 11, 1901, under Senate Bill 278. Articles for incorporation were drawn up Third Month 

16, 1901.

The meetings of the board during the next few years were concerned chiefly with Cuba. They worked hard and overcame many obstacles. The work moved on so that by 1905 they had ten missionaries and teachers, and three main stations: Gibara, Holguin and Banes, with five outlying ones, some substantial buildings at Gibara and Banes and money on hand for the mission house at Holguin. They still depended on voluntary contributions for the work but providentially their needs were met. There were times when the treasurer, James Carey, Jr., had to advance money from his personal account which he never collected in full, but on the whole the income of the Board was large enough to encourage all connected with it. Mahalah Jay was given $300.00 a year eventually as executive secretary.

Meanwhile the Society of Friends was realizing how good a thing it was to meet in regular sessions and when the 

Fourth Quinquennial Conference convened in Indianapolis,

Indiana, in 1902, its first business was to organize the Five 

Years Meeting. The Uniform Discipline, a common Declara tion of Faith, constitution of Government, and rules of Discipline of the general body of Friends in America was adopted by eleven Yearly Meetings. The American Friends 

Board of Foreign Missions became the missionary board of the Five Years Meeting and a further step in its growth and development was recognized. 

Philadelphia Yearly Meeting, which had been represented on the board from the beginning, withdrew in 1903 in order to concentrate more on their work in Japan, but maintained sympathetic relations with it. The work which they had

started in Cuba was taken over by North Carolina for a brief time.

Big decisions had to be made at the annual meeting of the board in October, 1905. Although all the yearly meetings had been interested in the united project in Cuba, the burden had been borne by four, the others giving relatively small amounts, some as small as five dollars. In five years they had spent an average of four thousand dollars a year on the work. They felt that since the board was now the official board of the Five Years Meeting the burden should be more nearly shared and giving increased. There was a deficiency of $500.00 in the budget this year. Something needed to be done or the work of the promising new mission would have to be curtailed. 

In June of this same year, Willis Hotchkiss appealed to the Board to open an industrial mission in Africa to the south of the mission already under the auspices of the Friends Africa Mission Board. They could not see their way clear to do this.

Now at this meeting, Western Yearly Meeting presented the proposition that it was practically unanimous in asking the board to take over its work in Mexico. It stipulated that all Western Yearly Meeting funds be used for the Mexican work. The board could not accept that as it felt that all funds should be put together, pooled, and distributed by the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions. 

Before closing the afternoon session the following resolution was adopted: "That we open the way at the evening session to hear any and all propositions that may come before the board from the yearly meetings or from other missionary boards on the subject of transferring their work to the 

American Friends Board of Foreign Missions."

"The evening meeting was opened by prayer by Allen Jay for divine guidance in considering the important subject to come before the board, the subject of uniting the foreign mission work of the American Yearly Meetings under the board."

A letter from Iowa Yearly Meeting indicated their readiness upon the concurrence of other yearly meetings to transfer all supervision and direction in their mission field together with their financial support to the board. They wished the transfer to take place as soon as they had paid off their debts and the mission was “unencumbered." 

This was followed by the reading of a paragraph from the report of Indiana Yearly Meeting Foreign Missions Board to the effect that in view of Iowa Yearly Meeting's step toward placing their work under the board, and Western Yearly Meeting's favorable consideration of the same one, they, too, should anticipate such an arrangement.

With almost dramatic force came an appeal from the Friends Africa Industrial Mission for the board to consider taking over their work. The board had been spending $6,- 

000.00 a year but Edgar Stranahan, who presented the petition felt that $3,500.00 would be adequate for the future. 

Four boards had gone on record as favoring centralizing the work of the mission board. Allen Jay, seeing the trend, urged, "Don't put off what ought to be done soon."

This was a big thing for the board and needed prayerful consideration. They recorded humbly, "Seeing the unmistakable trend of things toward the union of the foreign mission work of Friends in America, the board took the subject under advisement and resolved 'that the board is united in judgment that it should take the subject under prayerful consideration and as early as practicable formulate some general working plan for accomplishing the end desired.""

The board turned to the above propositions the next morning. Two ideas were presented: one, that their work be confined to Spanish-speaking countries; the other "that we have a plan broad enough for all the work of American 

Friends everywhere." Before the vision and faith of these

Friends, we must pay all honor and respect for the latter proposition was "more generally favored."

Obviously the question was too big to be decided by a board even as representative and trusted as this one. There must be a general missionary conference. This was eventually called to be held in Richmond, Indiana, Tenth Month, 1906. Committees were appointed to plan a basis of union. 

The First Missionary Conference of Friends in America was duly held in Richmond, Indiana, Tenth Month 24-26, 1906. This was a representative gathering of all the Friends 

Yearly Meeting Missionary Boards, and Philadelphia Foreign Missionary Association. One of the Philadelphia dele gates, Mary Morton Haines, served as secretary of the conference. Rufus M. Jones of Haverford was on the program with a paper on the "Place and Scope of Foreign Missionary Work Contemplated by the Uniform Discipline." 

The plan of union was fully discussed and the delegates instructed to place it before their yearly meetings in time for the report to be made to the next session of the Five Years 

Meeting in 1907.

The old Board of the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions met for the last time in October, 1907. There were in attendance some of those who had pioneered and carried the load since 1894, Thomas C. Brown of Carmel, Indiana, faithful president, James Carey, Jr., of Baltimore, trusted and generous treasurer, and Mahalah Jay, devoted secretary. 

The latter would be eighty in two months. Her body was frail, but her keen mind, tirelessness, and great faith had been stamped on every act of the board for thirteen years.

These Friends, and Ellen C. Wright of Wilmington, Wm. 

Jasper Hadley of Iowa, Lydia T. Painter of Western, Carolena Wood of New York, William C. Taber of New York, Carleton C. Pritchard of Wilmington and Charles E. Carey of Indiana, and others were a great band and the Society of 

Friends owes them a great debt.

But the new day had new needs. The united work needed a bigger program and a young man to give full time to the work, and the old board realized it and worked toward that end.

The choice of secretary fell on Charles E. Tebbetts, a 

Friend of California Yearly Meeting. Elbert Russell in his

History of Quakerism says this of him: "In 1907 Charles E. 

Tebbetts became secretary of the American Friends Board of 

Foreign Missions. He attempted to organize and systematize the mission work of American Friends on the lines marked out by the best experience of other denominations and especially by the Edinburgh Missionary Conference of 1910. 

During the... of his secretaryship, he made the missionary work the best organized and best supported united work of American Orthodox Quakerism." Actually he was appointed in 1907, but began work on January 1, 1908. 

At the time of the Five Years Meeting in 1907, the missionary work of Kansas, Indiana, and Western Yearly Meetings was placed under the care of the board. Between then and the 1912 Five Years Meeting, Iowa, Oregon, New York, Wilmington and North Carolina Yearly Meetings and the Friends Africa Industrial Mission Board transferred their work to the new board, also. 

The trend toward centralization and union had started slowly but had moved with increasing momentum and confidence. No one could have foreseen the tremendous changes in the world scene to be brought about by two wars before the century, opened with such optimism and dedication, was

half-spent; but such unity as Friends had achieved, and such world vision as had come to them through their missionary work were to be their greatest blessing, and God's evident leading a witness to the spiritual life of many who had learned to wait upon the Lord in expectant silence. 

Charles Tebbetts resigned in 1916 to return to California.

Fortunately for the work its continued success was assured by the appointment of Ross A. Hadley to the position. He had served for two years as assistant secretary and so was thoroughly familiar with the four fields, Africa, Cuba, Jamaica, and Mexico. His background of Y.M.C.A. work in the 

United States and India also fitted him well for his new responsibilities.

With the close of the World War in 1918, the fifth foreign field of the Five Years Meeting was placed under the Amer ican Friends Board of Foreign Missions. New England 

Yearly Meeting had maintained the mission in Ramallah, Palestine, since 1888. The first World War had made it necessary to close the schools and to recall the American workers to America. With the coming of the Armistice and the possibility of reopening, New England Yearly Meeting decided to place the mission under the care of the board. A 

Palestine Field Committee of people familiar with Ramallah was appointed to assist the board in the new work. This added a very interesting field, one in which all Friends had a peculiar interest because it took them into one of the most hallowed places in the world, and there was great need in 

Palestine for the Christian message.

The chastening influence of the World War caused Friends to turn their thoughts to the home fields and they saw needs there, too. Consequently, a Home Mission Board was formed for service in most of the areas of America which were without churches. This had been recommended at the 

Five Years Meeting in 1917 and three existing boards, the

Evangelistic and Church Extension Board, the Social Service Board and the Board on Welfare and Conditions of the Negro were merged into the new Board of Home Missions. 

Ruthanna M. Simms became Executive Secretary of this board in 1920.

The story of its work belongs to another chapter. However, its connection with the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions must be recorded here. For the four years, 1920, 1921, 1922, 1923, it published its own reports, but in 1924 was published the first joint annual report of the Home and Foreign Missions Board. Friends were celebrating that year the three hundredth anniversary of the birth of George Fox.

It was a time for large vision, deepening of the Spirit, and adventurous living. There was no longer a home and a foreign field; humanity and human needs were universal. *"Whenever they have found human need, be that need physical, mental, social or spiritual, they (Friends) have unhesitatingly cast their lot and told their story by simple word and kindly ministration." In order that Friends might have the vision of the oneness of the human family, the boards would publish their reports jointly. This they did until 1928-1929, when another beginning was made. 

At the 1927 Five Years Meeting in America in Richmond,

Indiana, the two boards, Home and Foreign, "were directed to close as rapidly as was consistent with the best interests of all concerned, the work of their respective boards in order that the two boards might become one Board of Missions at the annual meeting in May 1929, such board to have direct supervision of all mission work both at home and abroad, the name to be American Friends Board of Missions."

This was a difficult and perplexing assignment but it was accomplished by May 1929, as directed. Eleven yearly meetings were represented and appointees of the Women's Mis- 

* First Joint Annual Report, 1923-1924, p. 3.

sionary Union of Friends in America were included as members to serve on the new board. Charles M. Woodman was chosen temporary chairman and Alta L. Jewell, temporary secretary. The following officers were elected: Chairman, Howard W. Cope; Vice-Chairman, Charles M. Woodman; Administrative-Secretary, B. Willis Beede; Acting Administrative-Secretary, Charles O. Whitely; Assistant Secretary- 

Treasurer, Alta L. Jewell; Treasurer, Dickinson Trust Company. A statement of the policy and purposes of the board as prepared by the special committee appointed for the purpose was presented and adopted as follows: 

"The Board of Missions of the Five Years Meeting of the 

Society of Friends recognizing the very grave responsibility thrust upon it by the work of the two boards that hitherto have labored separately within the geographical limitations of the United States, and in foreign countries, and recognizing that in the thought and purpose of our Heavenly Father there are no such things as national boundaries, racial limitations, and class distinctions, and recognizing that not only the man himself but the place and conditions under which man lives lie within the purpose of the divine redemption, solemnly dedicates itself to the following program: "First, to discover and to cultivate within the lives of Friends everywhere in a much deeper and more vital way than hitherto, those resources of spiritual power and living which have been so clearly revealed to us in Jesus Christ. "Second, to carry this spiritual experience of the indwelling Christ out into every area of human relationships everywhere. "Third, to select carefully people for this supremely important task whose lives will abundantly radiate this spirit of the living Christ abiding in their hearts. "And fourth, that this program be carried out in harmony

with the statements of belief set forth by the Five Years Meeting."

As in every step of the growing board, there had not actually been as great a change as one might think. Most of the people serving on the new board were familiar with the work of both the old ones and there was close sympathy and understanding for all fields.

The new secretary, B. Willis Beede, had served the Foreign Mission Board since 1917, first as education secretary, then from 1923 as general secretary. He brought to the new work his rich experience and consecration. 

The budget was big. They asked for $115,000.84 plus $3,500 for Nebraska Central College but the treasurer was instructed to make payments on the basis of only $101,876.84 which was slightly less than the average of the receipts of the two boards for the two previous years. Considering the history of the next fifteen years, that sum marks a high spot in such benevolences and seems stupendous today. 

Errol T. Elliott of Kansas Yearly Meeting became Admin istrative Secretary of the board in 1932 when B. Willis Beede had to resign because of his health. The new secretary had been assistant secretary for one year so entered his new duties with some knowledge of his work. Hazel Lincoln of 

Iowa Yearly Meeting was appointed assistant secretary. Errol T. Elliott resigned in June 1934 to serve as Promotional 

Secretary for the Five Years Meeting, and Hazel Lincoln carried on until the appointment of Merle L. Davis as acting secretary in 1934 when she, too, resigned.

Merle L. Davis, former missionary to Cuba, was appointed 

Administrative Secretary of the Mission Board and Martha 

C. Kendall, Office Secretary, in 1935. They have served since then, giving the work a continuity so important in any effort. Merle Davis has visited all the fields, as had most of

his predecessors, so is familiar with the widely scattered areas of Friendly concern, and his missionary background helps him to interpret events on the fields, readily. Martha 

Kendall Tubesing has carried her share of the work efficiently and understandingly.

The American Friends Board of Missions celebrated its fiftieth anniversary August 20, 1944. Its growth from a small organization of concerned Friends which had to win its way, step by step, overcome prejudices, win confidence, and justify its existence by the quality of its work, is a great story. Its courageous adventuring in foreign missions when the challenge of Cuba was laid before it, was in the true Friendly tradition. When the forces of unity began to leaven the Society of Friends, the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions was one of them, a great one. Its greatest task, however, was to centralize and strengthen the work of several small boards striving to carry on work in far-flung corners of the world. The need for such a board was manifest in 1906 when more burdens were laid on the existing one than it could well carry. It came into being at the right time; its growth at first was slow but in the right direction; its experience in administering one field was the right kind of beginning, getting foundations laid that were sure. The coming together of Friends into a Five 

Years Meeting group helped the board and the large interests of the board helped it. As the story grows, it bears the radiance of a Divine unfolding and shows how we are inextricably a part of our time. There is a holy naturalness about it. There is the unmistakable evidence of the Spirit of God at work in human lives. 

In the 1928-1929 report the American Friends Board of Missions defined its function in the world. It was repeated in 1944 as it entered the next phase of its development.

CHAPTER IV THE HOME MISSION BOARD 

The big event of 1915 in the Society of Friends was a conference of men Friends held in Richmond, Indiana, in October of that year. Although originally planned as a national conference, when the time came it had attained international proportions as every yearly meeting in the world except 

Dublin was represented.

The idea of a conference for the men of the Society of Friends was rather unusual. The very nature of the organization of the Society in which women from the very beginning had been accorded an equal place in its councils, had seemed to preclude separate organizations. However, women Friends as early as 1865, had seen the need of united effort in their particular concerns and had accepted the practical as well as inspirational aspects of the situation. They were at this time well organized for their program of home and foreign missions. 

At the great Ecumenical Conference in April 1900, the place of the Christian laymen in the foreign missions program was strongly emphasized. Out of this grew the Laymen's Missionary Movement. The Church eagerly enlisted consecrated business and professional men in its great missionary program and their support gave the movement tremendous impetus. Various denominations began to hold men's conferences and the action of the men of the Society of Friends was in keeping with the times. 

At a meeting held early in February to plan for the conference, "There was a pronounced belief expressed that the time had come to move forward along some practical lines

that would insure a larger interest on the part of Quaker men.”

Friends responded enthusiastically to the call. In August an item in the American Friend said that it seemed sure that every monthly meeting in America would be represented. 

Actually, five hundred and eighty men registered. Women were welcome to attend, apparently, for two hundred and eleven women registered also. The conference opened with the expectancy of great achievement.

This was a critical period in the world's history and Friends met under a great sense of grave responsibility. The brave new century begun in such a hopeful spirit was darkened by the war then raging in Europe. English Friends were eager for Friends in America to maintain a strong peace testimony. In its splendid isolation of three thousand miles from the scene of the conflict, and in its material sufficiency, the United States felt secure. There seemed little danger in 1915 that this country would be involved in the war. Although the historical peace testimony was emphasized and much thought given to it, the burden of the conference was for the home scene, and the practical application of Christianity to life in the United States was the center of their deliberations.

Walter Rauschenbusch's great book, Christianizing the Social Order, published in 1912, had aroused the Christian Church in the United States in a remarkable way. Its influence was far-reaching, forming a background for discussions on Christianity for many years. Its indictment of 

Christians was of such honesty and conviction that it could not be ignored. People began to study life in America and were made uncomfortably aware that all was not well. Here in the United States were areas of great human need. Poverty, ignorance, disease, and superstition existed in this country as well as in foreign lands. The social order at home

was clearly a denial of the fundamental teachings of Jesus. 

These and many other evils were facts to be faced by Christians of all denominations. Quakers saw in the needs of the hour a challenge to the very essence of their faith.

Again, some very practical questions had to be faced in connection with the Society of Friends. These were implicit in the call.

The first of these was the fact that the Society of Friends was not growing. That was partly due to the practice of disowning Friends for marrying out of meeting. More recently, however, it was due to the changes that were taking place in American life, among which was the movement from rural to urban life. Many rural communities which once had strong meetings were sadly depleted of young people. Often these young people moved to urban centers where there were no meetings. Many of them associated themselves with other churches, which was good, but as a result, the already small Quaker group in America lost much of the strength it so much needed. Many of these 

Friends were eager to continue Friendly contacts and voice their common testimonies but were too small in number in most areas to support a meeting. For years, additions to the Society of Friends had come chiefly through birthright membership. If Friends were to press forward, they needed a stronger Society in order to hold together these small, widely scattered groups.

Their concern was recorded in a minute calling for "an intelligent and carefully thought out system of church extension which would establish new Friends meetings in the newer settlements of the West, in larger cities, in irrigation centers, and other communities where Friends were locating and no meetings exist." This was carried to the Five 

Years Meeting in 1917 and as a result a Home Mission Board of twenty persons was set up. The existing Evangelistic and

Church Extension Board, The Social Service Board, and the 

Board on Welfare and Condition of Negroes were merged in the new one. Ellison R. Purdy was first chairman of the 

Home Mission Board and Edgar H. Stranahan, secretary. 

Charles O. Whitely was appointed chairman of Evangelism and Church Extension, Carolena M. Wood, chairman of the 

Department on the Welfare and Condition of the Negro, later succeeded by Earle J. Harold and Albert J. Shepard of the

Department of Social Service. 

The entry of the United States into the war followed by great changes and emphases in our American life for the next two decades may have made some of the dreams and hopes of the conference fade, but much good of permanent value did come of it at home.

The new board, the result of their concern, was incor porated in 1919. *The following year Indiana Yearly Meet ing transferred to it the property and administration of 

Southland Institute, a school for Negro children near Helena, Arkansas. About the same time, the Associated Executive Committee of Friends on Indian Affairs asked for closer co-operation and sharing with them of the responsibility of its work with the Indians, and that year held their annual meeting in Richmond. 

Alexander C. Purdy served as executive secretary for more than a year but could give only part time to the work. 

In 1920, Ruthanna Simms became the executive secretary of the Home Mission Board. 

The Forward Movement of the Five Years Meeting, grow ing out of the conference, stimulated Friends, bringing a great spiritual awakening and increased responsibility for evangelism and home missions. The Home Mission Board became the agent through which much of its work was accomplished. In November 1920, $63,110 was pledged to the 

* Board of Home Missions Report, 1920-1921, p. 4.

Forward Movement budget by the yearly meetings for the home field alone, although only $46,316.36 was finally raised. Incidentally, the budget for foreign mission work that year was $123,080.75.

The first annual report, for 1920-21, tells of work undertaken in thirty places, from New England to Colorado, from North Carolina to Oklahoma, including meetings in Vermont, Nebraska, Indiana, Iowa, and Virginia. Attendance and interest had increased in most churches, Sunday schools had been started or built up, meetinghouses repaired, resident pastors secured for many meetings, and funds made available for travelling evangelists. Much of the hope expressed in 1915 was being realized. 

Meetings in Chicago, Detroit, Washington, D. C., Everett, Washington, and Denver, Colorado, all received assistance for some time. Some of these meetings have developed into strong centers. Others grew stronger for a little while but were not able to weather "the winds of adversity" when the period of prosperity ended and much of the initial enthusiasm of the Society as a whole was gone. However, the work of the departments of Evangelism and Church Extension and Social Service was productive of lasting good, and some of the results anticipated by the Men's Conference, realized.

Southland Institute closed its doors in 1925. It was a difficult decision to make for the school had long been an evidence of Friends' interest in Negro welfare. It was founded by Indiana Yearly Meeting in 1864 at the request of Governor Morton of Indiana. During the Civil War, many slaves sought sanctuary within the lines of the Union Army. One of the many points where this took place was at Helena, Arkansas. Their plight was serious and in the emergency 

Friends were appealed to by the governor. The Yearly 

Meeting appointed a committee "whose labors were to be di-

rected especially to the relief of the physical necessities of the Freedmen and their advancement in knowledge and religion."

For years, Southland College, as it was called, sent out to all parts of the country young men and women who became leaders in their communities. The name was changed to Southland Institute in 1917, as being more appropriate for the type of work offered at the school, and the good work continued.

Southland Institute was officially transferred by Indiana Yearly Meeting to the Home Mission Board in August, 1920. 

They hoped that the larger body would make it possible to secure more funds with which to develop and enlarge the work.

A good start was made. A survey was made by an able committee in 1921 and four general policies of development were recommended. These were to be along the lines of more emphasis on secondary education in order to train leadership for the neighbouring communities, community outreach, agricultural training, and simple iron work to enable farmers to care for their implements. 

Prospects were very bright-F. Raymond Jenkins studied 

Negro Education at Hampton Institute for a year in order to serve as principal of Southland, teachers were encouraged to study at other colleges, and a beautiful new barn was built at a cost of $2,500.

But the community was changing. It seemed increasingly difficult for the Negroes to pay fees, other day schools were being built which were more accessible to them, and it was impossible for Friends to maintain the physical plant of the school adequately.

The closing of Southland after sixty-one years of usefulness was a great disappointment to Friends. It had been a

bond of love between the races and a practical expression of mutual aspirations. There was comfort in the realization, however, that Friends in 1864 had met an urgent need splendidly and continued to maintain the institution as long as possible.

Although Southland closed as an institution in 1925, work for Negroes did not completely end. All the funds that had been expended for Southland are held by Indiana Yearly Meeting and the interest used to aid worthy Negroes in many different colleges. The Sturge Fund of some $28,500, another fund of $18,000, which includes bequests, and the money realized from the sale of the farm at Southland, are sufficient to make available approximately $5,000 a year for this purpose. From fifty-five to sixty students are helped each year with sums ranging from fifteen to two or three hundred dollars and they attend such institutions as 

Fiske University, Hampton Institute, Penn College, Earl ham College, and Pendle Hill. Most students regard the aid as loans and pay back to the fund. The handling of this fund is a responsibility which Indiana Yearly Meeting carries as a legacy from the school founded in an emergency and which claimed its sympathetic and loving care during a critical period in the development of the Negro in the United States. 

Two other fields of activity engaged the interest of the Home Mission Board. They were the work in the Tennessee 

Mountains and among the Indians in Oklahoma. The latter missions were under the Associated Executive Committee of 

Friends on Indian Affairs but the Five Years Meeting

Friends aided them. An account of the work in those two fields is given in the next chapter.

The decade following the first world war was one of the most unusual periods in recent American history. In the early 1920's there was a great wave of prosperity. Religious organizations found it easy to get money and so made big

plans for expanded programs. The two Friends Mission 

Boards thought in large sums, too. Their combined budgets seem staggering in view of the United Budget of the Five Years Meeting in 1944, which is less than the budget of the Foreign Mission Board alone at that time. In its first report, 1920-21, the Home Mission Board asked for $96,000 to enter all the doors then open to them for immediate service in home fields. In November 1920, the board "was forced to reduce the budget to $63,000."

The stability of the 1920's was reflected in all aspects of American life, particularly in the religious life. Each year the budget had to be reduced, most of the fields aided by the department on Evangelism and Church Extension had to be closed. Southland was given up in 1925, and by 1928, we find the budget of the Home Mission Board reduced to $19,633.39, the Foreign Mission Board to $88,475.10, and the curve steadily going down. The need of economizing led to a study of the situation from the standpoint of administration. 

By 1927, it seemed advisable to have all the work under one board. This was authorized at the Five Years Meeting and the matter accomplished in May, 1929. The great depression began to be felt at this time. America faced at the end of the decade an exceedingly low period. The new board began its work at this difficult time. The optimism of 1920 was replaced by the pessimism of 1930. It took courage to go on, but though hard facts had been sobering to a degree, idealism was not dead. There was a new reliance on spiritual resources and an awareness of the need of the power of the Spirit to overcome the materialism of the new age. 

From 1929 on, the spiritual message of Friends would be given by a Society united in the challenge of both in Jerusalem, and in all Judea, and in Samaria and unto the uttermost parts of the earth.

CHAPTER V WOMEN AND MISSIONS 

When Elizabeth Hooton in her mature years found in the message of George Fox the answer to years of seeking, she became almost a symbol of the part that women were to have in the new movement. From the beginning of the 

Quaker movement, women have enjoyed a large measure of spiritual freedom and have worked and suffered equally with the men. Elizabeth Hooton was one of the first Quaker ministers or propagators and Mary Dyer was one of the first 

Quaker martyrs. Quaker women were beaten and imprisoned in England and America in the struggle for religious freedom and went fearlessly about the world in the joy of their message. The Society of Friends has had a large number of able ministers among its women. George Fox never questioned the intellectual or spiritual equality of the sexes. 

Pioneer life in the United States must have given women Friends little time or energy for full participation in the meeting for worship or business. According to Rhoda M.

Coffin, a woman of energy and independence, the type that foreshadowed the feminine movement, writing in 1860, "women were not accustomed to organized efforts (among the women). The voice of a woman in church action was rarely heard in expression of a personal opinion. The usual answer was, 'I unite' or 'I am free' and never, I may say, to evolve a system of organized work which should bring out the energies of the younger portion of the members. . . 

The women were earnest but timid, some of them frightened at their own voices and fearful to take hold and step out into a new field."

Whether Rhoda M. Coffin was unduly critical or whether

this situation was true in all yearly meetings, we do not know, but it was in this setting that the first woman's organization was formed.

There was a great revival among the young people of Indiana Yearly Meeting in 1860 but all the benefits that had come from it were in danger of being lost to the Church and "the world would be deprived of this vast amount of spiritual energy if something wasn't done quickly." Rhoda Coffin was deeply concerned that the yearly meeting should find some way of letting the younger members of the meetings participate in its work.

From this concern grew the earliest organization of women Friends and it was for home mission service. The women who united in it were Rhoda M. Coffin, Harriet Stier, Sarah 

J. Smith, Mary J. Taylor, Anna M. Pugh, and Elizabeth Hopkins. They met at the home of Rhoda M. Coffin during the yearly meeting of 1866 and invited Murray Shipley, Charles 

F. Coffin, and John Henry Douglas to meet with them. They drafted a constitution and by-laws, named officers, selected Friends from all the quarterly meetings to act as correspondents and organize in all the monthly meetings. They organized themselves under the name of the Women's Home 

Mission Association of Friends. That accomplished, the problem was how to call a meeting of women unhampered by church restrictions. One woman agreed to do it but lost her courage at the last moment. "I am afraid to call the meeting without consulting the elders," she confessed. Let Rhoda 

Coffin tell the sequel in her own forthright manner: "I was much annoyed. Everything had been arranged for the meeting and I knew that consultation would kill it. "With an intense burning in my soul for the advancement of the work and all absorbed in the purpose and fearing that I was about to break the rules of the church, I knelt down by

122 

 one of the benches on the porch and taking an old envelope and pencil wrote, 'A meeting will be held this afternoon at 3 o'clock in this room for the purpose of organizing a Woman's Home Missions Association of Friends. All are invited.' 

As I was at the table as assistant clerk (and had been for several years) and the announcements were always read by me,

I read it with the other announcements. We invited J. H. 

Douglas, Murray Shipley, and C. F. Coffin to meet with us and give us some information as to needs. "We met at the appointed time and seven hundred women assembled. It was a wonderful meeting... The women were ripe for the work and took hold with much interest, joyful to have some opportunity to labor for Christ." 

Then followed a period of great activity. Cottage prayer meetings were held, Sunday schools organized, jails, prisons, and poor-houses visited, tracts distributed, and new meetings started. At the end of seven years the benefits to the 

Society of Friends were so marked, and the opportunities had increased so tremendously, that the women decided to invite the men to join with them. This was done at Indiana 

Yearly Meeting in 1873, and a committee of both men and women was appointed. Before this event of 1873, however, twelve Friends had met in 1870 and organized a voluntary association for foreign service. The two incidents are not mere coincidences but are clearly related. And Charles F. 

Coffin visiting New England Yearly Meeting in 1873 urging them to assume some missionary responsibility could speak with the conviction of experience. That year New England became interested in Brummana, Syria. 

Foreign missions was the clarion call that aroused the women of the Society of Friends to action. They gave liberally of their time and money and followed the growth of the work in many lands with genuine and sincere interest. In the seven years between 1881 and 1888 there was an astonish-

ing amount of missionary organization and activity among the women of the Society. They themselves reviewing the period in the First Union Report of the Woman's Foreign 

Missionary Societies in 1887 said: "In the recent revival of missionary zeal amongst us there sprung up an almost unaccountable demand for a Woman's Association. No one of us can trace the movement precisely to its source, or tell what caused it so rapidly to extend, but the result has been a large per cent of advance in missionary interest and effort, leading us to believe that the Woman's Foreign Missionary Societies are providentially in the church of Christ at this time and that the co-operation of men and women in the spread of the Gospel can be more effectually secured through separate organizations.' 

Certainly, many factors led to the organization of the 

Woman's Missionary Union but Eliza C. Armstrong* started the ball rolling in 1881. On one occasion when she was visiting her parents near Monrovia, Indiana, she heard that some Friends had joined a Woman's Foreign Missionary Society of the Methodist Church. Later she said, "I found myself suddenly in the grip of a beautiful vision." A vision of wonder it must have been for it led her to give herself to the missionary work of the church at home in able, guided service as president of the Union and, for twenty-five years, editor of the Friends Missionary Advocate. Perhaps in her vision she saw the women of the Society of Friends meeting regularly for study and prayer, giving to carry the message of the love of Jesus to the womanhood of the world in a peculiarly womanly way.

Back at her own home, Eliza C. Armstrong began writing letters to representative women in the quarterly meetings of Western Yearly Meeting. Her concern met with such * Eliza C. Armstrong married Joseph D. Cox of Columbus, Indiana, in 1912. 

He was her second husband.

quick sympathy and response that eight missionary societies were set up within six months. She herself organized one at Hopewell, Indiana, and Ella J. Davis, of Vermilion 

Grove Quarterly Meeting, Mary Hadley of Bloomingdale, and Sarah J. King of Hamilton County, Indiana, organized in their areas. The big moment came when the Woman's 

Foreign Missionary Society of Western Yearly Meeting of 

Friends was organized at Plainfield, Indiana, in Ninth Month, 1881, by the Yearly Meeting of Women Friends. The candle was now placed in a candle-stick to light all that were in the house.

The light glowed and was carried to all the yearly meetings. Philadelphia women formed a Woman's Foreign Missionary Association, Twelfth Month 12, 1882; Iowa and Indiana followed in 1883; New England and Ohio in 1884; Canada, North Carolina, and Kansas in 1885; and New York in 

1888. Baltimore was the only yearly meeting that did not organize in this period, but was sympathetic and joined the Union later; and as new yearly meetings were organized in 

Oregon, California, Nebraska, and Wilmington, Ohio, a

Woman's Missionary society was considered essential. 

Missionary societies were organized at Earlham College, Richmond, Indiana, and at Wilmington College, Wilmington,

Ohio in 1884 as a result of the visits of Eli Jones, who at the age of seventy-seven had only recently returned from Brummana, Syria, where he had helped dedicate a girls school. 

The work of Samuel Purdie in Mexico had also a great appeal to college men and women.

The Friends Missionary Advocate was born during this period. Although during the first five years it was "owned, edited, and published" by one individual, it was practically the organ of all the Friends Missionary Societies. For the first year or two the W. M. Societies had a column in The Christian Worker, edited by Calvin W. Pritchard

of Chicago, but in their new found powers, they felt the need of a magazine of their own. Esther Tuttle, later Pritchard, took the initiative and assumed full responsibility. The first number appeared First Month, 1885. Significantly, the first article on the first page was The World for Christ by Eliza C. 

Armstrong. In that same number were articles by Della 

Rees of Westfield, Indiana, and Dr. Levi Johnson of Oskaloosa, Iowa, who were planning to go with a party of forty missionaries, under the leadership of Bishop William Taylor, to found a mission or missions on the West Coast of Africa. 

Ohio W. M. S. had undertaken to support Dr. Johnson. Incidentally, these Friends did sail in February, 1885, but returned later in the year. Della Rees was not able to stand the climate in Africa. Dr. Johnson planned to go back and found a Friends Mission on a 2,400-acre tract of land assigned to him but apparently the time had not yet come for the venture.

For a time Friends leaned heavily on the experience of other boards as they were so new in the work, and many of the articles in the first issues were by and about other missions. Dr. James Thoburn of India and Dwight L. Moody were among the contributors of that era. Arthur H. Pierson of Philadelphia wrote a series of weighty, theological articles on The Spiritual Prospects of the Heathen. There were reports from all the Friends fields and excellent educational material. Women Friends were becoming world minded. 

Letters were coming from Mexico, Jamaica, Syria, Madagascar, India, China, Japan, and Alaska telling how people lived, what they ate and wore, marriage and funeral customs, needs of schools and hospitals, and stories of real people, men and women and boys and girls in the schools and churches. It brought them into warm and tender association with their unseen sisters in many lands. Quaker women were justly proud of their very own paper which was kept at a high level

of literary standards as well as educational and inspirational values. It became the official paper of the Woman's Missionary Union after its organization was accomplished in 1890. 

Editors of the Friends Missionary Advocate have been Esther Tuttle Pritchard, founder; Eliza C. Armstrong, who served for twenty-five years; Lenora N. Hobbs, editor for eighteen years; and Dorothy Pitman, who edited it for two and a half years and resigned to take up work in Africa in 1943. The present editor is Freda M. Hadley who took it over in 1943 and continues to maintain the best traditions of the magazine.

The seven years between 1881 and 1888 were great years in the woman's missionary movement among Friends. Women no longer were content with "I unite" or "I am free."

They knew they could do things themselves. They were conscious of their powers and abilities and dedicated them to the cause of Jesus Christ. And as they worked in separate organizations yet united in their fields of labor, there came to them a vision of a plan for union at home. The great Conference held in Richmond in 1887 may have inspired them, for in the autumn of that year, the Richmond Conference was held in Ninth Month, the Woman's Missionary Society of 

Western Yearly Meeting sent out a call for a Union Conference of the ten woman's missionary societies to meet in Indianapolis, Indiana, in March 1888. It was fitting that the call should come from Western which was the first to organize and whose president was Eliza C. Armstrong.

The conference was duly held in Indianapolis, Indiana, with a large attendance from all the yearly meetings. Among the speakers were Dr. J. M. Thoburn and Dr. Isabel Thoburn of India and Dr. George E. Post of Beirut, Syria. The two hundred thirty-seven page report of that meeting is good missionary reading today.

Out of the conference came the organization of the Wom-

en's Foreign Missionary Union of Friends in America. They held their first conference in Glen Falls, New York in 1890 with Phebe S. Aydelott of New England as president. Eliza

C. Armstrong became editor of the Friends Missionary Advocate. Mahalah Jay, who was to be so prominent in the 

American Friends Board of Foreign Missions, was appointed as Friends representative on the World's Missionary Committee of Christian Women. Ellen C. Wright of Wilmington, another who was to have a big part in the work of the A. F. 

B. F. M. was appointed treasurer. In places of responsibility and leadership were the following: Esther T. Pritchard, of 

Western Springs, Illinois; Anna M. Haines of New York 

Yearly Meeting; Margaret A. Evans, Indianapolis, Indiana; 

Mary Morton Haines, Cheltenham, Pennsylvania; Elizabeth

M. Jenkins of Mount Pleasant, Ohio; and Adeline B. Hill of 

Western Springs, Illinois. Their names stand for consecration and ability.

A few statistics will indicate the strength of the Union in 1890. There were 3,376 adult members in 224 societies, and 

2,140 children in junior and primary groups. They were supporting eighteen missionaries, eight native evangelists or 

Bible women, seven schools with 304 children enrolled, and fifty-one children in boarding or day schools.

The Woman's Missionary Union has always enlisted capable, devoted leaders. Space will not permit a record of all the names that should be written large, but it is fitting that those who have served in the highest office should be put down here.

1943Helen E. Walker of Whittier, California, is president, today. She embodies in consecration to her service the ideals that have permeated the lives of those she has succeeded. 

A few other names should be added to any list: Sybil 

Jones, Anna Hussey, Margaret Cope Kimber, and Sarah 

Swift of New England, Mary L. Peele of North Carolina, Mary L. White of Kansas, Mary T. Thomas of Iowa, Phoebe 

J. Wright of Canada, Ellen Nicholson of Baltimore, Sarah M.

Hadley and Marianna Brown of Western, Florabelle Rosen berger and Margaret Taber of California, and Carolena

Wood of New York. 

The Woman's Missionary Union today has a membership of 5,700 women in 318 societies. They gave $26,973.89 in 

1941 and 1942. Money is given for four funds dear to their hearts: the Esther Baird Love Fund; the Faith Fund which helps support a missionary in Jamaica; the Mountain Hope 

Fund for one in Tennessee; and the Eliza A. Cox Memorial 

Joy Fund for a missionary among the Indians. 

Eleven yearly meetings are represented in the Union. 

They are Baltimore, California, Indiana, Iowa, Kansas, Nebraska, New England, New York, North Carolina, Western, and Wilmington.

There are also the departments of Young Woman's Work, Junior and Primary Work, Stewardship, Education Honor Roll, and Peace and Public Morals. The Young Woman's department contributes between four and five hundred dollars a year for special projects on the mission fields and the Juniors help with the support of the children of missionaries.

Today, as in the past, the women of the Society of Friends are in large measure responsible for the missionary program of the church. The Laymen's Movement which promised so much help twenty-five years ago soon lost its vigor and as a result the cause of missions at home and abroad has suffered. 

But women folks have gone steadily on, meeting regularly for prayer and study and giving from their means, many of them sacrificially. Theirs is a great venture of faith and love. Their initiative in going ahead with organization has been a great service to the Society of Friends, and the unity they have maintained should be an inspiration to all. They have shown that Christians can be united in service in a common cause in the Kingdom of Jesus Christ.

CHAPTER VI TENNESSEE AND WESTERN INDIANS TENNESSEE 

As we review most of the mission work of the Society of Friends, we are impressed by the almost uniform design of the pattern. Individual leading followed by consecration to the call; concern shared by committees or yearly meetings, and growth of the work as others saw the needs and heard the call, seems to be the "shape of things." Work in the Tennessee Mountains has followed much this design. 

Along about 1870, a young Quaker doctor, a member of

North Carolina Yearly Meeting, located at Maryville, Blount County, Tennessee, to begin his practice. This was a fairly natural place for a Quaker of North Carolina to choose as it was in that part of Tennessee where Friends had settled. when they migrated from North Carolina in pre-war days. 

He was at home in a part of his own yearly meeting, actually, and the young doctor, Jeptha D. Garner, settled down to serve the community to the best of his ability. 

Soon, however, he was called to minister to the mountain people, and as he went about among them, their needs weighed heavily on his heart. The physical, intellectual, and spiritual poverty of their lives was indeed sad. They were Americans, who at one time were in the midst of the western migration but now that the waves of migratory people followed the northern river and railroads, they were lost to all the forces that were affecting American life. Yet, they were the descendants of sturdy pioneers who had sought these very mountains in their quest for economic, political, and religious independence long before the birth of our nation. In them was the blood of French Huguenots, Germans,

English, and Scotch-Irish, the latter predominating; they were people of intelligence, good breeding, and proud individualism. Their speech and customs, their folklore, and folk-songs reflected their heritage and their seclusion. They emerged from their mountain homes to serve the nation in times of crisis but each time, the crisis over, they returned to their loved mountains. They had contributed a great deal to the new nation, of able men and deep loyalty, but in 1870 they lived in their hidden "coves" and valleys hardly able to make a living from the soil. As the young doctor was drawn more and more into the hills, he became practically a self-appointed medical missionary yearning to open their minds to education and their spirits to a more real religion. Nor was he content to go on alone. He called on 

Friends in North Carolina, Baltimore, Philadelphia, New 

York, New England, Ohio, and Indiana yearly meetings to help him. He opened schools and urged young Friends to come there to teach. Some of the first young men who responded were Benjamin C. Coppock and James B. Bruff of 

Ohio, William P. Trueblood of Indiana, and John N. Parker, later of Baltimore Yearly Meeting. 

Southeastern Tennessee offered Friends much in the early days, a healthful climate in the midst of scenes of beauty and grandeur, better farms than they had in North Carolina, and homes away from an economic system based on slave labor, where they could rear their children in freedom and without prejudice. Friends prospered there during the first part of the nineteenth century. They established meetings, Lost Creek, the earliest quarterly meeting being already set up in 1799, opened schools, and developed their farms. 

But the Civil War brought them untold hardships, leaving them almost destitute at one time of both men and money and devastating their meetinghouses and schools. As the tides of war shifted they were now under the Union Army

and now the Confederate. They tried heroically to maintain their peace testimony even in the midst of the battles that raged around them. Some of the men joined the army, but many escaped farther west, and not a few hid in caves to escape being taken into either army. 

When the war was over, they set to work to rebuild their homes and schools but probably never again recovered their initial prosperous state. Their own plight did not blind them to the needs of others, however. There were their own white neighbors in the mountains for whom they felt a care; they set up a school in Maryville for the Negroes who had fled into Tennessee during the war; and they turned to help the remnant of the Cherokees who had fought bitterly against the government's decree to move them into eastern Oklahoma and were then living on their beautiful reservation, a neglected people. John Calvin and Ruth Jones of 

Lost Creek Meeting, and Rachel Binford of Maryville were among the Friends who took an active part in Quaker ministry in Monroe County at that time where most of the work is carried on today. 

Under the impetus of the enthusiasm and consecration of Dr. Garner and the young men who helped him, Friends in 

Tennessee carried on a significant work. Dr. Garner supervised the building of eight meetinghouses. He reported that in the first eight years of his work among the mountain peoples, three thousand were taught to read. Local leaders were raised up to help also and great advance was made in religious work.

Among the first of the local leaders was John Farr for whom Farr's Chapel was named. He was originally a Methodist but was attracted to Friends through the life and teaching of John Parker, one of the young men from Earlham College who responded to Dr. Garner's appeal in 1875. He was much loved in the community and, for a time, was allowed

the use of the Methodist Church for his services. A jealous faction caused that to be closed to him, however, but friends made available a schoolhouse. When that, too, was refused him because of the same faction, services were maintained under difficulties. At one time, one of his Methodist friends had a key made to the schoolhouse when he learned that the place was to be locked to John Farr. The solution to the problem obviously was a meetinghouse for his use. John Farr preached at this place once a month only and between visits, during one month, friends in the community built the chapel and had it ready for service on his arrival. A three mile trail led from the road to the chapel and two of his friends carried the stove over the trail to the meetinghouse, truly a labor of love. Farr's Chapel, the first Friends meetinghouse in Monroe County, Tennessee, became a center of truth and light to the people who gave so lovingly. 

Another splendid worker was David Sanders. He came into the work in 1893 largely through the influence of Calvin and Ruth Jones who had taught him to read and opened the Way of Jesus to him. He started work at Rogers Chapel in 

1893 and remained as spiritual leader among the scattered groups for twenty-seven years. He died in 1924, but had the satisfaction before his death of seeing young workers come in to carry on in his place.

Other teachers who have gone in and enriched their work by a larger service to the communities have been Pearl Redding, who taught school in Farr's Chapel at first, Sophia 

Pribbenow of Oklahoma, Gladys Morris of Iowa, Earl Clark of Kansas, Helen Hester of Illinois, and Carrie Miles of Vermont. They not only taught the three R's but they opened 

Sunday schools, directed community activities, and assisted with church work.

In the year 1920, when the Home Mission Board was formed and a survey of the needs at home was made, Friends

were divinely led to strengthen the work in Tennessee. 

Some time before this, Tennessee Quarterly Meeting had been transferred to Wilmington Yearly Meeting so that an appropriation was made to that yearly meeting for the work. 

There was need for a full-time minister to take care of the five meetings and the congregation that met monthly at Lovin Schoolhouse. At this time, Murray Kenworthy persuaded J. Edward Ransome, then serving a meeting in Vermont, to accompany him to Tennessee. What he saw and felt became a clear call to Edward Ransome to take over the work. He and his wife, Daisy K. Ransome, soon moved to 

Tennessee and started their splendid work at Oak Grove in 

September, 1920.

J. Edward Ransome possessed the qualities and abilities most needed in the new situation. He had been called to the 

Christian ministry as a young man and had been faithful. 

Happily, he was skilled in many things and exceedingly practical. He saw his church in a needy field. In addition to the worship services, he saw the need of teaching the people how to do things, the need of a shop where he might teach some of the boys the principles of three or four trades with which he was familiar, the need of wholesome recreation, better schools, and last but not least, better agricultural methods. Dr. Garner, too, had dreamed of an industrial school; the shop of Edward Ransome was in line with that dream. The Master said, "I came that ye might have life and have it abundantly." His servant facing his new task must have prayed to be a tool in the Master's hand. Daisy 

K. Ransome set up a home to which all were welcome.

When the new home was built it was a demonstration of what was possible with effort because the labor of their own hands went into it. The piano in the home and the foldingorgan in the meetinghouse brought the magic and solace of

music to a music-loving people. Later on, Daisy Ransome had home-making classes for the girls in the parsonage. 

The demonstration farm is a fairly recent venture, the result of an old concern of Edward Ransome. He has been able to enlist the interest of the men of Wilmington Yearly Meeting, of which Friendsville Quarterly Meeting is a part, and they have contributed generously from their means and experience. Clayton Terrell, chairman of the farm committee of the board, fostered the project. Through this farm, they hope to show how modern methods of agriculture can improve crops, provide more varied ones, and prevent soil erosion, the latter one of the most serious problems of that mountain country.

Eventually a manual-training shop, the demonstration farm, and a beautiful new chapel became the center for the work of Friends in Tennessee. But the load was too heavy for one family and in 1928, Fred McMillin was sent down to assist with the meetings. They built a home for him at 

Rogers Chapel, again much of the work being done by the "preachers" themselves. He continued there for ten years, retiring from the work on account of his health in 1938. 

Fred McMillin had won the love and affection of the people to a remarkable degree and he was greatly missed. 

Franklin and Lillian Chant of Indiana followed Fred Mc Millin at Rogers Chapel. They remained in the work a little over two years. 

Edward O. and Violet Linton with their two sons, John

Edward and Mark Allen, went to Tennessee in 1942. They live at Rogers Chapel but serve a larger community.

The work carried on by the Mission Board is entirely under the care of the two families. There are five meetings and the congregation that meets occasionally at Lovin 

Schoolhouse. Edward Ransome preaches at Oak Grove and

Three Points. He also oversees the farm which requires a great deal of his time. The Oak Grove Meetinghouse was built in 1927. One of the most beautiful incidents connected with it was the gift of $500 from Stanfordville Meeting of New York just laid down. The money was from the sale of the meetinghouse and passed on to Oak Grove, "that the work which they could no longer do at Stanfordville might go on there." Contributions came from many unexpected sources and a really beautiful building is the result. 

Edward O. Linton serves Rogers Chapel, Farr's Chapel, and Chestnut Valley Meetings. In the two years that he and his family have been there they have won the confidence and trust of the people. A new meetinghouse has been much needed at Rogers Chapel for a long time and this year the dream is fast becoming a reality. Again, the happiest type of cooperation is making possible this building which promises to be worthy of the task for which it will be dedicated. 

These six congregations form Oak Grove Monthly Meeting, organized by Friendsville Quarterly Meeting in 1906. 

There are four hundred and forty-seven members. They contribute to the United Budget and pay their proportion of the yearly meeting stock.

Of all the schools built by Friends in Tennessee before the Civil War, Friendsville Academy alone remains. It does not officially have any connection with the Mission Board, but its spiritual tie is a very real one, and it has many friends among American Quakers everywhere. Edward Ransome is a trustee of the Academy and has long seen how it could serve able young people of the Friends meetings under his care. Many young men and women have been helped to better Christian living and professions through its influence. 

Friendsville Academy, founded in 1857, has a long history, and some of the years have been very difficult. Only devotion and great sacrifice have carried it on.

Great changes are taking place in eastern Tennessee. With the coming of the automobile, America has discovered the surpassing beauty of the Great Smokies, and the government has set aside a large area of it for a national park which attracts thousands of visitors every year. Writers and musicians have discovered there a wealth of material in the folklore and folk-tunes of the people. The mountain people are America's rediscovered people. Better roads, improved schools, the schools and colleges of other denominations, the health and agricultural work of the Government are all influencing their life. This war will make its impress, too, on the lives of thousands of young men and women as they mingle with their compatriots in many lines of activity in our national life.

Yet, there will always be the need of the church. The needs of the people haven't changed much from the days in 1920 when the Ransomes stood on their mountain-top and saw a vision; perhaps spiritually not so much different from the needs the young doctor saw at the beginning of his career in 1870.

Through almost seventy years a Friendly ministry has been carried on by many people. Fortunately, some of them have been able to spend the long years so necessary to accomplish lasting good when one works with people. 

Behind all the activities of their work, Friends have sought to be mindful of the things that are eternal-an emphasis on the meeting still characterizes the present-day program of Friends in Tennessee.

FRIENDS AND INDIANS 

Friends had a concern for the American Indians before they came to the New World. Soon after the first Quaker settlers arrived, George Fox wrote to them to "go and discourse with some of the Heathen Kings desiring them to

gather their council and people together, that you may declare God's Everlasting Truth, and his Everlasting Way of Life and Salvation to them, knowing that Christ is the promise of God to them, a Covenant of Light to the Gentiles." 

When he made his own journey to America, he sought every opportunity of meeting with them and refers to them as "very Loveinge." 

Josiah Coale and Thomas Thurston made remarkable journeys among the Indians in the seventeenth century, and seem to have met with a ready response. One experience recorded by Josiah Coale gives us an interesting picture of the attitude of the Indians to the white brother: “I was moved to goe to a certen Island (in New England) called Martens' Vineyard (Martha's Vineyard) to visit some Indians there amongst whom I had felt desires to know the Lord, and I had a Meeting amongst them and they were very Loving to mee, and tould me they much desired to know God, and afterwards at another place near Plimouth Colony I was amongst the Indians among whom was Breathings after the Knowledge of God." Quaker history contains many such stories of individuals engaging in the travelling ministry and in each case it is clear that these Friends never questioned the ability of the Indians to understand the essentials of their message. 

When William Penn accepted the proprietorship of land in America he admonished his agents to win the Indians by "just and lenient" measures. His own just dealings and personal relations with them, so daringly different from the usual mode, places him well in the vanguard of the prophets of the brotherhood of man. He remains, today, the model for all Christian intercourse with our Indian friends. 

Friends generally seem to have taken great pains wherever they settled to protect the rights of their Indian neighbors. Practically every yearly meeting in America appointed a committee for that purpose at one time or another.

When the westward movement began for the Indians, Friendly interest and help followed them, and Friends were ever on the alert to press their claims before the government when necessary.

The policy of peace and good-will which guided Friends in all their dealings and relationships with the Indians met with much success and made them trusted both by the Indians and the colonists. There are some instances of criticism of the Quaker method but, on the whole, it was highly respected even when not adopted. The time came however, when the peace policy of the Quakers was to have political sanction and benediction.

Frequent Indian uprisings accompanied by massacres and bloody warfare were a reproach to our national life. The system of resettling the Indians on new lands, the pressure of white farmers and traders upon the old hunting-grounds, and the pushing through of the railroad combined to keep the Indians in a state of unrest and they fought hard to preserve their last hold on the old life. Between 1850 and 1869, the situation grew steadily worse. Through these years the Government of the United States sought to subdue the Indians by the war method. But the frontier wars were costly and the peace the Government sought to bring about never was realized. The year 1867 was particularly critical. General W. T. Sherman himself had despaired of subduing the Indians by military force and he was among those who advocated a new government policy. Many close to the 

President urged a new way. The Weekly Chronicle of Wash ington, D. C., September 14, 1867, in the following editorial suggested the plan of using the Quakers: "The treaties made by William Penn were always respected by both parties and the peaceful sect of which he was a distinguished member have been traditional friends of the aborigines, and always kindly regarded by them. We

have often thought that if the Society of Friends who so successfully colonized and civilized the Senecas in western New York, and with such judgment and benevolence managed their affairs with the Government, could be induced to take charge of the subject of colonizing the Indian territory, and instructing the Indians, they might prepare them for the inevitable future." 

President-elect Grant was not wholly unprepared, therefore, when a committee of Friends waited on him January 25, 1869, to plead the cause of the Indians, to ask them to carry out his proposed peace policy, later embodied in the following letter: 

Washington D. C., February 15, 1869 "Sir: General Grant, the President elect, desirous of inaugurating some policy to protect the Indians in their just rights and enforce integrity in the administration of their affairs, as well as to improve their general condition, and appreciating fully the friendship and interest which your Society has ever maintained in their behalf, directs me to request that you will send him a list of names, members of your Society, whom your Society will endorse as suitable persons for Indian agents. "Also, to assure you that any attempt which may or can be made by your Society for the improvement, education, and Christianization of the Indians under such agencies will receive from him, as President, all the encouragement and protection which the laws of the United States will warrant him in giving. "Very respectfully, your obedient servant. "(Signed) E. S. Parker 

"Brev. Brig. Gen., U. S. A. and A. D. C.

The committee of Friends which waited on President-elect Grant was truly a representative body. Realizing the tremendous responsibility laid upon them, they formed the Associated Executive Committee of Friends on Indian Affairs to insure its success. This association organized at Damascus, Ohio, June, 1869, was made up of representatives of New 

England, New York, Philadelphia, Baltimore, Ohio, Indiana, Western, Iowa, and North Carolina yearly meetings. The

Indian Aid Association of Philadelphia, organized a few months earlier, co-operated with it, also.

Enoch Hoag of Iowa, a pioneer in the Indian concerns of Iowa Yearly Meeting, was appointed head of the Central Superintendency which embraced all the area of Kansas and the Indian Territory, now Oklahoma. The five civilized tribes, Cherokees, Creeks, Chickasaws, Choctaws, and Seminoles living in the eastern part of the Indian Territory were only indirectly under the Central Superintendency, but the remainder of the Indians, 19,000 of them and mostly warlike, were divided into nine agencies with a member of the Society of Friends at the head of each. 

Liberal Friends now known as General Conference Friends, were given the Northern Superintendency which included the Indians of Nebraska.

In addition to the agents nominated by Friends, the Committee supplied teachers for the agency schools, clerks, physicians and other employees. Mahalah Pearson Jay, first seccretary of the Friends Board of Foreign Missions taught one year at the government school at Fort Sill. 

The first task for the agents was to quiet the warlike tribes so recently engaged in bitter war against the government and try to bring about a spirit of cooperation. There were innumerable details of their lives that needed attention and many perplexing problems to solve if they were to be helped to fit into the new civilization of the dominant white popula-

tion. The agents set up schools partly with government money and partly with money contributed by Friends, for although this was a government project, Friends felt the unique trust laid upon them and gave generously to supplement the funds set aside by the government. Yearly meetings sponsored some of the schools, often supplying them with books, paper, and money. The Women's Aid Committee of Philadelphia reported sending clothing, shoes, blankets, toys, books, and pictures to the value of $1,500, and paying the salary of a teacher at the Wyandotte school for four months, in 1876. Orthodox Friends gave about $6,500 in one year. Friends ministers made religious visits among the 

Indians to aid in the "Christianization" program so much desired by the President, but primarily carrying out the traditional interest of Friends in their spiritual welfare. 

Ten years of the hardest yet most consecrated of service was given by the Society of Friends, years which saw progress in education, moral uplift, religion, and last but not least, peace. It was a new thing in Indian affairs for agents to conduct religious services among their charges and to conduct their affairs with utmost integrity. As one waggish newspaper man reported it, these new agents did not prey on Indians but prayed for them.

But a new administration was not sympathetic to Friends and it became increasingly difficult for them to carry on as they felt they should. As a result, they asked to be released from official responsibility for the Indians in 1879. Undoubtedly, mistakes were made in handling a problem so big and for which there was no precedent, but the mistakes were never of the heart; some of the agents may not have been as well prepared for their work as they should have been, but there were probably few people who had the required training or the conviction necessary for the tasks. In the main the services rendered by the agents and the Society of

Friends were above reproach. The fact that some agents were recalled at the request of the Indians is evidence that they were trusted. Some Friends continued in government posts for a few years, but by 1885 their connections had practically ceased.

Severing official connections with the Indians, however, did not end Friendly interest in them; rather, it opened new doors. Friends had learned a great deal about their needs in ten years and felt called to continue a ministry of love and service for them. The entire membership of the Society of 

Friends had been aroused and the Executive Committee saw in their concern an opportunity for missionary service. The work carried on in Oklahoma today really had its beginnings in the days of the agents.

The Executive Committee of Friends on Indian Affairs appointed Elkanah and Irene Beard as their first missionaries to the Indians in 1878. The Beards had spent three years in 

India so were experienced in this type of work. They were the first of a long line of devoted Friends who have worked in Oklahoma.

Friends were slow to set up meetings among the Indians, yet by 1881, four preparative meetings were established, Ottawa, Wyandotte, Modoc, and Seneca. These constituted

Grand River Monthly Meeting with one hundred and thirtythree members.

Rayner Kelsey in his excellent study, Friends and the Indians, divides the decades 1885-1914 into three distinct periods of effort. The Committee adopted the plan of appointing a general superintendent for all the work in Oklahoma, to reside there. This work was mainly in the northeastern part of the state among the Senecas and Wyandottes, Ottawas, Quapaws and Modocs, and in the central district among the Kickapoos and Shawnees. Shawnee became the headquarters of all the missionary work.

Dr. Charles Kirk and his wife, Rachel Kirk, took charge of the work at Shawnee in 1885. He was appointed first superintendent in 1888 and served until his death in 1893. Rachel 

Kirk served as superintendent for one year after his death.

This was a period of growth and expansion. Friends had ready access to the Indians from the agency days, and Indian Territory was still largely Indian. In other words, Indians could be reached as groups. The enthusiasm of the entire 

Society of Friends must have been stimulating to the missionaries also. There were twenty-three Friends missionaries in Indian Territory in 1898. In 1892, thirteen preparative meetings, twenty-three other meetings, and four monthly meetings were reported with a total membership of four hundred and twenty-six Indians and five hundred and sixtyseven white people. New meetinghouses were built for the 

Indian groups, and, in many instances at the request of the Indians, the government made grants of land for this purpose. White people coming into the new country were attracted to Friends. This seemed a very hopeful sign at first but eventually may have been a cause of the decline of the work as they tended to be the dominant group. Schools also were established. Rayner Kelsey in Friends and the Indians describes one as follows: 

"Perhaps the most successful school carried on or aided by Friends in this period was the one at Skiatook (later called Hillside) taught by Eva Watson, assisted part of the time by Olive Chamberlain. Eva Watson had had a notable success in the Modoc School before going to Skiatook. At the latter place she soon built up a splendid school, which in 1890 had an enrollment of sixty-nine. Many pupils boarded in the families near the school, some of them in the mission which was maintained at this time by Eva Watson's parents, John M. and Eliza Watson. In 1890 the Associated Committee said of this school: 'It appears to be the best school within the

radius of twenty-five miles and the pupils crowd the houses around it so as to be near the school. The classes range from those in the alphabet chart to those studying higher arithmetic. All are taught some Bible truths daily; they have learned the ten commandments, the Lord's Prayer, the 1st and 23rd psalms, and the golden texts for the year. The school is a powerful agency for good.""

George N. and L. Ella Hartley headed up the work during the next ten years, 1894-1904. This period was marked by the opening of two new mission stations, one among the Otoes and one among the Big Jim Band of Shawnees. Women Friends of Indiana Yearly Meeting were largely responsible for the work among the Otoes, and the Maine Branch of the Women's National Indian Association, the Big Jim Mis sion. This was turned over to Friends in 1898. 

The Otoes seem to have welcomed the mission and the Christian message, making it easy for the workers to go ahead with buildings in which to do their work. On the other hand, Chief Big Jim and other prominent men in his band of the Shawnee tribe were hostile to Christianity and hindered the work considerably. As Rayner Kelsey says, "It was a time of seed sowing and long waiting for the harvest." 

Other work proceeded slowly and some progress was made. During this period, however, there appeared the first signs of the factors which were to cause the decline in mission work. White settlers were coming in ever-increasing numbers making life and religion more complex for the Indians and them less responsive to the evangelizing message of Friends. Government and public schools became numerous and efficient so that Friends schools were no longer so necessary. The opening of Indian Territory to all and sundry brought "tens of thousands in number, of almost every nationality on the face of the earth,” into Indian life. Superintendent Hartley, from whose report the above was taken,

wrote two years later, 1914; "On first coming into this locality the nearest railroad station was fifty miles distant and these Indians were comparatively isolated; but now the whistle of the trains may be heard on every hand and white people side by side with Indians are transforming the broad prairies into fertile fields of grain and orchards of fruit. The huts and cabins are being rapidly exchanged for commodious dwellings, and the promise of financial prosperity seems evident."

The third decade saw still further developments in Indian
