Territory and Indian life. William P. and Abigail C. Haworth were in charge of the work at this time. 

One big event of this epoch was the coming of statehood in 1907. Union in dignified association with the rest of the country was to make Oklahomans more self-conscious, take pride in their state, and develop a more settled, law-abiding citizenry. Public schools were set up for all children, Indian and white, better schools than religious bodies could support were built and so the last of the Friends schools closed. These church supported schools had served their generation well and while many wished that the religious influences of the church schools could have carried over into the public schools they had to admit the superiority of the equipment of the new buildings and the ambitious program of colleges and universities being set up.

One encouraging feature of these years was the beginning of work among the Osages. A mission was established for them at Hominy, Oklahoma in charge of Daniel A. and Hattie E. Williams in 1908.

But as work was growing in one area, it was decreasing in others. Kansas Yearly Meeting took over some meetings entirely and many stations had to be closed. When Clark and Elma T. Brown became superintendents in 1914 the work

was greatly reduced. The conditions first stated by George 

Hartley were intensified and work was limited to seven stations. This was a difficult period but good work was maintained at the stations.

Lawrence Lindley was the last of the superintendents. He and his wife, Amelia R. Lindley, went to Oklahoma in 1920 and made the Shawnee station their headquarters. There were then seven stations, Wyandotte, Otoe, Iowa, Big Jim, Shawnee, Osage and Kickapoo, each with its different problems and successes.

There are four mission stations in Oklahoma today which are described briefly.

Seneca Mission 

Friends' interest in the Seneca Indians began when the tribe was still in New York State. The Tunesassa Indian 

School was built on Seneca lands by Philadelphia Friends near the present city of Buffalo, New York, and was maintained almost continuously until recently when a program of community work was substituted. This interest followed the Senecas who moved to the West when they settled in the lovely hill country of northeastern Oklahoma, almost on the Missouri line. They came under the care of Hiram 

W. Jones, Quaker agent of the Quapaw Agency. Jeremiah

Hubbard visited them frequently as a minister and under his leadership a preparative meeting was set up which became a part of the Grand River Monthly Meeting in 1881. 

Seneca meetinghouse was built in 1884.

Arthur and Westine Shufelt have directed the work at 

Seneca Council House since 1937. For many years Harvey and Elizabeth Wallace had lived in the mission property and maintained a semi-official oversight over the meeting and the workers at Wyandotte had assisted them from time to time. When the Shufelts moved from the Kickapoo mis-

sion, they built a house and settled down to active mission work in this needy community. 

The membership of the meeting is about fifty, equally divided between whites and Indian. They take care of all their local expenses including yearly meeting assessments and send five dollars a month to the Indian Committee to apply on the salary of the missionaries. 

The usual program of meeting work is carried on: meeting for worship, Sunday School, Ladies Aid, and monthly meeting, but much of the work of the directors is in the community in many lines of activity, particularly in personal service. The following passage from a board report describes it, as well as most of our mission work: "The 

Friends Seneca Mission endeavors to carry Christian standards into all phases of community life and interests itself, not only in the spiritual life of the people, but also in their social, economic, and educational program." 

One of the big projects of the Ladies Aid which they have carried on for some years is making money for the building fund. The original meetinghouse, built in 1884, is no longer really safe. The meeting hopes to build a new one as soon as the war is over. When the new building is done, one thing will be central in it. A few years ago, a young Indian artist painted a large copy of Christ in Gethsemane. This hangs on the wall behind the pulpit. It is a remarkably beautiful painting and gives an atmosphere of reverence to the room and inspiration to quiet worship. 

That picture will be a fitting legacy to the new church. 

Wyandotte Mission 

Wyandotte is situated in northeastern Oklahoma, about fourteen miles from Seneca and has long been the center of our work in that district. The village is very small but is the center of a big school population. There is a large con-

solidated school there, and a government Indian boardingschool with more than two hundred children enrolled. 

Friendship with the Wyandottes goes back to 1799 when they were still in Ohio. The former Chief Tarhe of the tribe invited Friends from Baltimore to visit him at Upper Sandusky, Ohio, and be present at a Grand Council Fire. 

Three years later, the Indians returned the visit and had conference with the Indian Committee of Baltimore Yearly Meeting. 

Following the appointment of Hiram W. Jones as agent of the Quapaw Agency, Friends established the Wyandotte school. It was taken over by the government in 1883, but 

Friends were encouraged to continue their religious program in the school, which they have done ever since. 

The Associated Executive Committee decided to develop a more thorough program of religious education in Wyandotte School in 1928 under the leadership of Westine Lietzman and Dorothy Pitman. A large meetinghouse was built in the village, the most imposing of any in our Indian stations, and the large Sunday School was carried on there rather than at the Indian School on the hill. The meetinghouse also serves the community in many ways. The present directors, Ermin C. and Ruth C. Perisho go to the Indian 

School on Thursday evening for Bible study and recreation and on Sunday evening for vesper services.

The membership of the meeting is very small indeed, but like Seneca, the chief work of the pastor is his community outreach such as helping with chapel talks and Bible study at the consolidated school. 

Today, Wyandotte faces an uncertain future due to the development of Grand River Dam which has raised the level of the lake so that it threatens to inundate the town. How this will affect the community and the work of the mission is not

entirely certain but obviously some changes will be necessary; and the issue of wardship, one of the most pressing questions in regard to Indians today, when decided may affect the government school.

Kickapoo Mission 

Work with the Kickapoos began in 1883 by John Clinton and his wife. These were called Mexican Kickapoos and were located in the central part of the district near the present site of Oklahoma City. Work there was exceedingly difficult and discouraging and it took years before anything could be accomplished.

Elizabeth Test of Indiana began her great work with the Kickapoos in 1886 after several years as a teacher in a government Indian School. She lived in a bark hut in the early days and sought in every way to live close to the Indians. 

But they feared the white man's way and the white man's God. Their hearts seemed to be closed tightly against this courageous woman. After a time, by the exercise of Christian love and patience on her part they were at last opened wide. The Kickapoos even gave her land upon which to build a mission house to be used as a school also. She used one thousand dollars of her own money for the house and through it with the aid of other devoted women, she did a beautiful work for many years. Her memory is still very dear to the Indians, among whom she is remembered as

Teacher Test. 

There are not many Kickapoos in the country about Mc- Cloud, Oklahoma, where the mission center is. They are scattered along the river for about thirty miles. However, the directors of the work, William and Marian Byerly perform much service to the community. They keep up the farm which is partly a demonstration one, enter into community affairs, conduct the services at the meetinghouse

and are responsible for religious services at the Shawnee Sanatarium, at Shawnee, Oklahoma, thus keeping up Friends interest in that tribe. Whites and Indians live in congenial relations in all parts of Oklahoma. This is especially true at Kickapoo.

Recently, in 1943, the meetinghouse was painted and the interior decorated entirely by the membership who gave time and money. This has created a new interest in the 

Friends Center and an enlarged community program is visioned.

Osage Mission 

The center for work among the Osages is at Hominy, in the once rich oil fields of Oklahoma.

The mission was established in 1908 and, like the rest of our work stems from the Agency days. Laban J. Miles who had been agent to the Osages until 1885 was recalled at the request of the Indians in 1890. At this time, Thomas H. 

Stanley of Kansas, long-time friend of the Indians, came among the Osages frequently for religious services. He was very successful in his work. Then in 1907, Isaac T. 

Gibson, former agent of the Osages, told Friends of the open door for missionary work among these Indians. He and 

Edward M. Wistar, and William P. Haworth visited Hominy in 1908 and were so impressed by the need and the opportunity that within a few months they had a mission station established in charge of Daniel A. and Hattie E. Williams.

Few episodes in American history have been combined with so much drama as the unexpected fortune which gushed out of the harsh dry soil of Oklahoma at the time the automobile was making its first uncertain, noisy excursion into American life. The Osages were suddenly very rich with no background of education or understand-

ing of our type of civilization to help them meet the problems of handling so much money. The drama has had rather more pain than happiness in it. The Osages have been exploited mercilessly by their white neighbors, and the demoralizing influence of sudden wealth has been very great. Gambling, immorality, and intemperance have taken their toll. The peyote cult has grown up among them with elaborate houses for the practice of their worship. 

The curious mingling of the symbols of Christianity and peyote, the latter with its dependence on a drug, has resulted in a religion which seems a reversion to paganism. 

This background of great wealth and its attendant evils and peyote worship has made the work at Hominy very difficult, indeed. Young members of the tribe see the evils that have come upon them but find it hard to combat them. 

Some strong members, however, have built up a small meeting.

William and Adah Wagner developed a strong Sunday School while they were there. This was the foundation for the meeting.

When Arthur and Nettie Hadley went to Hominy in 1923, they immediately began to build a church. This was dedicated on January 27, 1924. The Osages took great delight in the building and attendance at services was good. They gave to the support of the church and missions and showed general interest. Their interest in the church has been fitful however and the work has been most discouraging at times.

Since the Hadleys have retired from active work, there have been several changes in personnel. At present Charles 

O. and Olive Whitely are in charge. Charles O. Whitely assisted with the dedication of the church in 1924 and his interest in Indians extends over a much longer period than that.

This long-time interest is most valuable to him and he has been able to win the confidence of the Osages in a remarkable way.

Big Jim Mission 

Although the Big Jim Mission was laid down in 1939, it deserves some mention as it had a fairly long history. Big Jim was the chief of the Absentee Shawnees who located at Shawnee not far from the present site of Norman, Oklahoma. 

The work was started in 1897 by the Main Branch of the Women's National Indian Association and put under the care of Friends the following year.

It was most difficult in the early days as Chief Big Jim and many of his band were hostile to Christianity. The chief emphasis was on community work and the development of the big farm. Every effort was made to win the friendship of the Indians and much was achieved but due to the changing location of the population, the work was finally closed after forty-three years of service.

The work in Oklahoma has been carried on in a period of great change, among a people being forced to adapt to a new civilization as quickly as any people in history. The burden of right relations with the Indians has long lain heavily on the conscience of Friends. This brief sketch in no way presumes to tell the complete story of how Friends have tried to follow the leading of the Spirit in service to the Indians but we have tried to find the links in the chain of friendship. 

The last three decades have witnessed a decline in the number of missions and missionaries representing the Society of Friends in Oklahoma, but the present workers seem to be reaching out in the traditional Friendly way to try to see life as a whole and the individual as a child of God. 

The Indian work continues to be administered by the Associated Executive Committee of Friends on Indian Affairs in

which all Friends unite. This committee carries the major financial responsibility for the work. 

Friends of the Five Years Meeting give through the American Friends Board of Missions. They contribute approximately twenty-five per cent of the budget and are responsible for a good deal of the publicity. Their largest share in the work, however, is through the missionaries who serve on the field, most of whom come from the Five Years Meeting group. The Joy Fund of the Woman's Missionary Union goes to a worker on the Indian field. Friends also cooperate with the Home Missions Council in its religious education program in the government Indian schools. 

The Associated Executive Committee meeting in its seventy-fifth session this year concerned itself with the problems of wardship, methods of Christian education practical for the Indian-white communities, and rural community building in the Seneca and Kickapoo centers. They indicate an awareness of new needs and new methods for a New Day in Friends Indian concerns.

CHAPTER VII MEXICO 

The events that led to the opening of a Friends Mission in Mexico were a manifestation of a Divine plan, so clear and convincing that they could not be dismissed as coincidences. 

They were a revelation of God working in His "mysterious" way.

The fact that the Beards and the Streets had to go to England to find some organization that could consider their concerns for work in the foreign field had stirred Friends in Indiana deeply. Yet the yearly meeting was not prepared for an official organization. In 1870, however, twelve Friends in the yearly meeting formed a voluntary Friends Foreign Missionary Association, as they were convinced of the need of such a body in America if the missionary enterprise was going to succeed.

Their program was neither ambitious nor involved. They stated it quite simply: "First, to present to those who may feel called upon to go abroad among heathen nations in the love of the gospel, an organization that can aid, counsel and advise; second, to be a channel for the gifts of the willing hearted in this direction and thus provide means for the necessary expense of those men and women who shall enter upon this service."

About this same time, John D. Miles, Indian Agent for the 

Kickapoos had to go to Mexico from time to time to look after the affairs of the Kickapoos who had gone there but had returned to Oklahoma. On these trips to Mexico he learned of conditions and needs in that country, and felt a great burden laid on him to tell Friends about them. He came to In-

diana Yearly Meeting in 1871 to lay his concern before the yearly meeting.

Sometime, even before these events, a young New York school teacher in North Carolina had written on the fly-leaf of his Latin lexicon "El Ramo de Olivo" (The Olive Branch), a name that was to become known in practically every Spanish-speaking country in the world within the next fifty years.

The young man was Samuel A. Purdie. He was about twenty-one when he felt the call to give himself to a greater service; he was twenty-eight when he was sent. He began at once to read missionary books and periodicals so that he became "conversant with the peculiar trials of a missionary life." There was no Friends Board in America to which he could apply and for some time he was tempted to offer himself to the Methodist Episcopal Board but his views on war deterred him. Like so many young men in the Society of 

Friends, he had not been able to take part in the Civil War, and the conflict to free the slaves had caused him great distress of mind. He had suffered court-martial on February 

19, 1864 for his refusal to bear arms. About the same time he was hearing about conditions in Mexico where there were frequent internecine wars and he "longed to do something to stay the tide of blood which was being shed, and to give them the gospel of peace and purity in place of their superstitious Romanism and the degrading influence of their priesthood." His desire above all else was to spread the gospel of peace. The news that English Friends had an organization for Foreign Gospel Service led him to hope that the Society in America would ultimately become "a missionary people" and follow the example of English Friends. In that hope he began the study of Spanish in order to be ready when the way opened. There was no teacher of Spanish available so he struggled on for some time alone. Then quite

Mexico 107 suddenly a Spanish miner moved into the neighborhood of Back Creek, North Carolina where he taught, and Samuel Purdie saw in the humble laborer a messenger straight from God. They spent long hours together, the pupil bringing to the lessons a finely ordered mind and an urgency to learn. 

When Samuel Purdie heard that some Friends in Indiana had formed a Foreign Missionary Association, he applied for service under them. They invited him to Indiana Yearly 

Meeting in 1871. John D. Miles was there also, with his concern. The streams had met, the earnest young man eager to go to some Spanish-speaking country, the Friend with a needy field in his heart, and the association ready to act. 

There was no reason for delay. Nor were there rules and regulations to observe in regard to physical examinations with their attendant inoculations, vaccinations, and so on. 

Samuel A. Purdie and his young wife, Gulielma Hoover

Purdie, left for Mexico in November, 1871. Their financial needs were to be met rather casually. "The Missionary Association," he wrote to his sister, later, "pay us what we expend for our wants as reported from month to month. We make nothing but have no greater anxiety than is caused by the rapid increase of our press work which is sustained by voluntary donations."

When Samuel A. Purdie was accepted, the two Friends who called on him said, "Remember, we do not send you to

Mexico to preach peculiarities but to preach the gospel." He replied, "These are the only conditions under which I could engage in the work." He was not a recognized minister and had no thought of founding a Friends Meeting in Mexico when he went there. "I expected to preach the gospel and let the converts unite with such churches as were accessible or simply band themselves together for mutual edification and oversight," he wrote. His hope was in the press, for which he arranged before leaving the States and its name

El Ramo de Olivo was burned on his heart. The years were to show how through the press and the school and the Church, the message of the gospel was to be made manifest in Mexico.

The Purdies' original plan was to locate at Victoria, but when they reached Matamoros across the border from 

Brownsville, Texas, it was clear that they should remain there. There were two advantages to this location. It placed them in the free trade belt where they could import paper free of duty, thus reducing costs, and they could use Brownsville, Texas, as a mail center which would facilitate the mailing of books and pamphlets to all Spanish-speaking countries. From the beginning, his vision was boundless. 

If there was disappointment that the more desirable climate of Victoria was to be sacrificed for the difficulties of 

Matamoros, they were quickly set aside. 

For sixteen years, Matamoros was the headquarters of the Indiana Missionary Association's work. This was the time when denominations were waking up to the benefits of cooperation and the evils of competition and these Friends accepted the territorial limits of the State of Tamaulipas as the field of work for the Society of Friends. 

This agreement was not always observed, as other denominations came in, but the fault was usually the Board's rather than the missionary's.

Within a brief time, Gulielma Purdie started a school for girls in their home and they had gathered a few people about them to study the gospels. New York Friends sent 

Samuel Purdie a press in the summer of 1872, and on September 1, 1872, the first issue of El Ramo de Olivo appeared. It must have been a great moment in Samuel Purdie's life when that first issue was ready for distribution. It was the fulfilment of years of prayer and hope. He had prepared the copy, set it up, printed it, and done all the

Mexico 109 work himself. Now the little paper which was to continue monthly for over forty years with its messages of peace and the gospel of Christ was a reality. The young missionary must have thanked God for this sign of His blessing on the work. For years this was the only press of its kind in Mexico and the publishing house of El Ramo de Olivo became known in almost every Spanish-speaking country in the world within the next twenty years.

The first problem the little school faced was getting suitable textbooks. Those available were either intensely 

Catholic or anti-religious. Characteristically, Samuel Purdie decided to prepare his own texts. He prepared a First

Reader, and printed an edition of one hundred forty-four copies which lasted two years. Just as they were exhausted, however, the Presbyterian and Methodist Missions began to organize schools in all parts of the country and the First Reader was in demand. It was so successful that soon they were selling one thousand copies a month to schools in Mexico, New Mexico, and Texas. That was followed by a complete series of reading books. Though decidedly evangelical in their teaching, they were used in the public schools in Mexico, also. As early as 1885, the Catholic Press declared the books for juveniles "the most dangerous element they had to encounter and unless they could be counteracted the coming generation would entirely abandon Romanism." He also translated and printed extracts from the Life of Stephen 

Grellet, William Penn, and Elizabeth Fry and a pamphlet of hymns, for the Mexican people loved to sing. There was also a grammar for common schools and a pamphlet of English lessons for Spaniards. Within ten years, he wrote to a friend: "Yesterday I had a call for books from Valparaiso, a few days before from Madrid, and from the Central America states we have interesting correspondence." Cuba, Venezuela, Argentina, the West Indies all were added to the coun-

tries where the publications of El Ramo de Olivo were welcomed. 

In the summer of 1873, Micajah M. and Susie R. Binford were sent out to help Samuel and Gulielma Purdie in the growing mission. They did not remain long but their service was a big help in the meeting work. Then William A. 

Wall, a Canadian Friend, was sent to help with the Boys school in 1880. They were able to get some very efficient 

Mexican teachers also and the schools grew rapidly. The 

Purdies visited the States in 1880 and aroused much interest in the mission in most of the yearly meetings. As a result, new workers were sent out and in the course of four years they decided they should build a boarding school for girls. 

This was made possible by C. G. Hussey of Pittsburgh for whom the school was named. Julia L. Ballinger who went out in 1883 became responsible for the school. The enrollment increased to nearly one hundred by 1885. 

Samuel Purdie believed also in the schools. He found time to help with them. He wrote to a cousin, "I shall teach English classes and deliver scientific lectures and thus aid the cause of science, the twin sister of religion in the elevation of the human race. In this manner we hope to overcome the holy horror of Protestantism among the better class of residents and meantime do all we can for the elevation of the sons of Guatineszin and not forget our Negro population.' 99 Although he had not gone to start a church, there was a meeting of eighty members and one native minister in Matamoros in 1878. He describes the Mexican members most appreciatively: "The Mexican converts evinced great independence of thought, carefully studying the Scriptures with desires to know the truth and to receive it from Christ alone and very unwilling that anything should hinder their doing the will of the Master. Casting aside the dogma of Papal infallibility, they rest less on the opinion of others than most

Mexico 111 Christians in a land where Romanism never held sway." In other words, the first Friends in Mexico were a highly intelligent, strong group. A young man of Indian descent, Luciano Mascorro soon gave evidence of special gifts in the ministry of preaching. He was recorded a minister in 1878 and spent the rest of his life in the mission, eventually serving as managing editor of El Ramo de Olivo. A mission chapel was built at a cost of about $4,000 in a public square in Matamoros and dedicated in May, 1880.

Within almost ten years there were now the printing-press of established reputation, two day schools, and a strong meeting in Matamoros. But the missionaries longed for a wider field and a new station was opened. Samuel Purdie had once visited Gomez Farias, an Indian village in the mountains in the southern part of Tamaulipas, and had been kindly received. In fact, he had preached to a large audience in an old Catholic Chapel which the villagers had kindly offered him. Luciano Mascorro went a year later, and he also was well received. The people asked for a church. So it was decided to send Luciano and his saintly wife Angelita, to Gomez Farias. Angelita was then in advanced stages of consumption and it was hoped the mountain air would benefit her. Angelita was among the first converts and a young woman of remarkable strength and sweetness of character. The 

Mascorros labored hard in their new work. They organized a meeting with sixteen members in 1880, and in 1884 a beautiful little chapel stood on a ridge between two mountain chains, facing the "most bold and precipitous range of the Sierra Madre." Three other meetings were set up by 1885 and Julio Gonzales Gea and Librado Ramirez were also serving as pastors.

One of the most interesting pastors however, was Gertrudis G. G. de Uresti, a woman of unusual intellectual attainments. She came from a family of wealth and culture

and so brought a rich background to her ministry. She spoke with such power and graciousness that she overcame the prejudice of the people against a "female preacher." She was a leader among her people, teaching and preaching, and won many to Christ.

Changes in the economic aspects of Matamoros made it seem wise to consider moving the headquarters of the mission to Victoria as had been originally intended. The work that had been done at Matamoros had been signally blessed but it was coming to a close. A hostile element was growing up in the city which made it difficult to carry on the work of the press. The city of Victoria, now the capital, was drawing away the business from Matamoros, also. In 1886 business was only about one-fifth as much as in 1871 when the Purdies went there. Many members of the meeting had moved away to find work in the more prosperous center. The time seemed right at last to go to Victoria and the move was made in 1887 and 1888. The press was transferred by 1891. 

Samuel Purdie was well received in Victoria. There were many opportunities for him to serve the community in the public schools and even as Vice-Consul for a brief time on the death of the regularly appointed one. He was appointed as a member of the board of examiners of the public schools to examine in such subjects as geography, astronomy, and philosophy. His interest in farming which had remained with him from boyhood caused him to introduce "Kaffir corn, Egyptian dhoura, Australian millet, and frosinte, the giant Central American grass" in order to improve the farm situation of the great rural population. 

Irving and Anna T. Kelsey, recent graduates of Earlham 

College, were sent out in 1893. They were supported by the 

Christian Endeavor Society of Indiana Yearly Meeting. Irv ing Kelsey at once took over the care of the publishing business which was a heavy load.

Mexico 113 Samuel Purdie resigned from the Friends Board in 1895 to go to Central America, a field to which he had long felt called. He left a good record of twenty-four years' work. 

There were at that time six monthly meetings, eight native pastors, and six hundred members of the Society of Friends. 

The press was widely known for its many excellent publications and there were good schools established. Nearly all the yearly meetings in the States and a large number of English Friends were contributing to the work. In the 1885 report, ten years earlier, acknowledgments are made to C. G. 

Hussey for the beautiful building for the boarding school for girls in Matamoros; to the Women's Foreign Missionary Association of Indiana Yearly Meeting for paying the rent of the Girls School; the salary of Louisa Flores, a teacher; sending new desks for the new boarding school "so that we need only books and bedding and some furniture for this school," and agreeing to pay the salary of the matron of the Hussey School when she shall be appointed; to the Women's Foreign 

Missionary Association of Philadelphia for supporting Julia

L. Ballinger, principal teacher of the Girls School, paying for desks for the Boys School and giving four-hundred dollars to the publishing department. Iowa, Baltimore, New 

York, New England, and Canada Yearly Meetings all helped too.

The price these Friends paid for their years in Mexico was tremendous. They were there through revolution, floods, and hurricane when their lives were in great danger, and they suffered from all the fevers of a tropical country. 

Gulielma Purdie early contracted tuberculosis which was not taken too seriously in those days, and she had frequent and long illnesses. Their story reads like a chapter from the life of Paul.

Samuel Purdie died from lockjaw, the result of an infected finger, two years after he went to San Salvador. Even so, he

had made an impression in the mission there which was an inspiration to the others. His son, Joseph Moore Purdie, carried on as a missionary in Cuba and Mexico, being a Spanish scholar and minister of much ability.

Two excellent schools were founded at Victoria. One by 

Indiana Yearly Meeting, Juarez Institute for Boys was opened in 1903, and Penn Institute for Girls was started by New York Yearly Meeting the same year. These grew out of day schools founded by Baltimore and New York Yearly

Meetings in 1887 and 1888. Penn Institute was later changed to Colegio Nancy L. Lee in honor of the loved American teacher, and served always both as day and boarding school. 

The workers hoped to develop an agricultural school to meet the needs of the majority of Mexico's people, but revolution and the long unsettled state of the country hindered that development.

Meeting work was carried on faithfully by the native pastors and their American associates but it was not always easy and there was constant hostility from the Catholic Church.

WESTERN YEARLY MEETING 

While Indiana Yearly Meeting was supporting the work in the northern part of the State of Tamaulipas, Western Yearly Meeting decided to open a mission in the State of San Luis Potosi to the south. They sent two young women out to Matamoros in 1884 to learn the language. They remained to help at the school for a year or two longer than they anticipated. They were then sent to Mexico City in 

1886 with Franklin and Sarah J. King. Ervin G. Taber and his wife Margaret joined them later in the year. Kansas 

Yearly Meeting gave some financial assistance to this mission.

However, it soon became evident that there were already

Mexico 115 too many denominations at work there, so in 1888 the mission moved to Matehuala in San Luis Potosi. This was a strong Catholic center and the workers faced great hostility, but by the exercise of great courage and Christian love, they finally won their way into the hearts of a number of men and women. Their first work was opening a meeting with its various activities which met with much success.

As usual with all our missionaries, the needs of the children touched their hearts and a school was started with Margaretta Marriage in charge. This was very successful. 

She later became the wife of Encario M. Sein and they both served the school and the meeting until Encario M. Sein was appointed general secretary of all the Sunday School work in the Republic of Mexico. 

Another school was opened in Cedral with Sarah A. Lindley as principal. Good buildings were put up in both stations. Medical work was introduced in 1900 by Dr. B. F. and 

Bertha H. Andrews of Iowa, who served for six years in Cedral. They combined meeting, school, and medical work in an unusual way and were able to meet a great need. They moved to Matehuala in 1906 but retired from the field in a year or two when the board decided that they could not see their way to set up facilities for adequate medical work. 

The work in this area was less spectacular in many ways than the northern work but a number of exceedingly able Mexican workers were raised up, some of whom keep alive the message of Friends in Mexico today. The girls boarding school grew to be one of the most important schools in the state. It was closed by the Revolution in 1912 but was reopened by Maria Castillo, a most devoted and efficient worker. Her brother, Fortunato Castillo, had been pastor of the

meeting for years and been referred to as the Quaker Saint of Mexico. Francisco Estrello, our special representative in

Mexico today, also came from the Matehuala Meeting. 

Friends work in Mexico entered a new era with the Revolution. Most of the missionaries had to return to the United 

States and much of the property of the Society of Friends was destroyed. The anti-religious propaganda and laws aimed chiefly against the Catholic Church fell heavily on Protestants, too. But there was encouragement in the new freedom that was in the air. The Friends Mission Board sent a commission to Mexico in 1919 to study the situation. This commission favored opening the schools at Victoria and improving the buildings, giving financial aid to Mexican pastors, supporting the Union Press rather than reviving the old publishing house of El Ramo de Olivo, and beginning medical work again. There were then thirteen Friends missionaries and a loyal group of Mexican Friends. The meetings that had survived the Revolution were all served by 

Mexican pastors.

In spite of all the efforts that were made, the following thirteen years marked a steady decline in the Friends Mexican Mission. At Matamoros, the first station, Hussey Institute kept open but found it increasingly difficult to compete with public schools. It was closed in 1929; and when a cyclone destroyed the meetinghouse in 1933, the meeting broke up also.

When the work was reopened in Victoria in 1917, Solomon Tice, who was in charge, had great difficulty in finding Mexican pastors for the meetings. At one time one pastor was trying to serve ten congregations. They were strengthened in 1928 by the transfer of Fortunato Castillo and his able wife from Matehuala to Victoria. The schools, Juarez Institute and Colegio Nancy L. Lee, closed in 1932 under much the

Mexico 117 same circumstances as Hussey Institute, inability to offer the advantages or pay the teachers as well as the state could. 

An attempt was made to revive work in the villages but because of difficulties brought about by other religious sects in some instances, in others, the changed economic conditions within the villages, and in still others, the difficulty of finding pastors, many meetings had to be laid down. 

The work at Matehuala was carried on largely by Mexican workers. George C. Levering, first principal of Juarez Institute, Emmett and Zoe Gulley, and Douglas and Rebecca

Parker were there at different times to strengthen the workers and maintain a link with the American Board. In 1932 

Clyde Roberts withdrew, our last American worker in Mexico. Maria Castillo carries on the school although she gets no support from the Mission Board. 

It is more than seventy years since Samuel Purdie and his wife went to Mexico. Between then and 1932, a great many 

Friends served for longer or shorter periods in the mission centers of Matamoros and Matehuala, venturing out into the villages, overcoming hostility, preaching the gospel, teaching, and joining hands with other Protestant denominations in bringing spiritual strength and freedom to a people struggling for that as well as economic and political freedom. A vigorous work was carried on at great personal sacrifice on the part of both Mexican and American workers. Naturally we ask what is left to show for all that effort.

In the Bridge of San Luis Rey, we will remember how the Mother of the Convent thought she had found her successor in the little girl whom she took into the convent and trained. 

The little girl grew into a gifted, devout young woman of great promise. When she was lost in the tragedy of the bridge the Mother wondered if all her years had been in vain. Would the work die with her? Then there came to her the realization that it was not for her to question the

Divine plan. It had been enough that she had loved and served throughout her life and that none of that devotion could be lost.

Friends must have felt something like that when Clyde Roberts left Mexico in 1932 after a long period of service. 

But as twelve years have passed, we begin to see that the seed that was sown through the years lives and grows. 

There are four monthly meetings in Mexico today, at Xicotencatl, Victoria, Monterrey, and Matehuala, and two congregations, Mante and Cedral, with two hundred active and associate members. The Mission Board has a budget of $2,477.66 for the work, to help with salaries of native pastors and various activities, particularly for young people. In a population of 21,000,000, two hundred seems very small. Yet, working with the Protestants of Mexico they are part of the leaven that is working through Mexico today. The work of Friends is under the direction of Francisco Estrello, a former member of the Matehuala Meeting. He is secretary of Christian Education. He has been particularly interested in the summer camp program for young people. Francisco 

Estrello is a recognized leader of young people throughout the Evangelical Church in Mexico. In 1941, he served as the 

Mexican representative at the first Latin-American Congress of Evangelical Youth held in Lima, Peru. Located as he is in

Monterrey, he is in a place of vantage to help Mexican Friends. He is at present secretary of the Press and Promo tion Committee of the Mexican Youth Evangelical Alliance and secretary of the Committee on Literature of the National 

Evangelical Council. Under his leadership, the indigenous 

Society of Friends should grow.

Last year, 1943, the Young Women's Department of the 

Woman's Missionary Union gave $379 to purchase camp equipment for the summer camp project. When Francisco 

Estrello was at the annual Board Meeting in April 1944, the

Mexico 119 subject of the future of the church in Monterrey was on his heart. That city has become one of the largest industrial centers in Mexico. Friends have been moving there, most of them from Matehuala, to get work, and they now want a meetinghouse. They have established a fund and are now asking the Mission Board for the privilege of selling properties no longer in use and using the proceeds on a new building in Monterrey where the need is urgent. If that is accomplished, it may be that a new day will dawn for this small group which is holding so fast to the beliefs of the Society of Friends. It is not easy yet in Mexico to be a Protestant and these Friends need all the encouragement the Society of Friends in the States can give them. 

The first Christian Youth Congress was held in Mexico in Alberto Rembao in Outlook in Mexico, "were the best legion ever sent out by any Christian Church in any century into a foreign field. They tackled problems of the day, practical problems by the building of churches, schools, and clinics by day and preached by night."

The first Christian Youth Congress was held in Mexico in 1939. There were more than one hundred delegates from eleven Protestant denominations. One speaker said, "We have come to guarantee our Master that He shall have a church in Mexico tomorrow." These were the children and grandchildren of the first converts to Protestantism. They represented a small but leading group of Mexicans. 

The Republic of Mexico is our nearest neighbor. Americans could wish that our record in government and business dealings with the Mexicans was clearer. Friends working with other Protestant groups have tried to represent the Church of Jesus Christ there and have left in Mexico a small but able group. Mexico needs devoted Christians to lead out in its social and economic pioneering, no less than in its efforts to throw off the superstitious practices that have passed

for religion but which have held them in bondage for so long. 

Mexican Friends carry a responsibility to help in the big program far beyond their strength. As their Christian neighbors, we need to continue to give our help and sympathy.

CHAPTER VIII JAMAICA 

Great Britain added Jamaica, the Jewel of the Caribbean, to her Crown in 1655. This was the England of Cromwell and the Commonwealth, of George Fox and the Seekers. 

Admiral Penn commanded the fleet that wrested Jamaica from the Spaniards, and his eleven-year-old son, William, must have heard of his father's exploits with pride. England had long coveted the island and its final conquest was a triumph for Cromwell.

Jamaica is one of the smaller islands of the Caribbean, 144 miles long and varying from twenty-five to forty-nine miles in width. It is ninety miles south of Cuba and about onetenth its size. When Columbus discovered it on his second voyage to the New World in 1494 he told his queen that it was a "paradise and worth more than gold." An abundance of rain produces lush tropical plants of great beauty and variety. It offers a wide range of physical and climatic conditions; from sea-level the mountains rise abruptly in varying heights to Blue Mountain Peak 7,360 feet above the sea; and from tropical heat at sea-level, the temperature varies to 45° on the highest mountain. It has been described as "a delightful land with many contrasts-hills and plains, ravines and valleys, rivers and dry courses, rain and drought, jungle growth and bare pasture—and always it is bathed in brilliant sunshine, save during the brief seasons when clouds and a stray hurricane may blot out the blue sky."

Colonists seem to have gone to Jamaica in great numbers in those early days. The wealth of Jamaica, chiefly its sugar, which had been going to Spain, these colonists diverted to England. Work on the plantations was done by Negro slaves.

Columbus found Indians in Jamaica, as in Cuba, and their fate was the same. The lust for gold and ruthlessness of the 

Spaniards caused the Indians to be exterminated within fifty or sixty years after the discovery of the island. Negro slaves were then brought in, in great numbers, to work on the plantations.

The fact that Jamaica with its large Negro population came under British rule when the Quaker movement was young and missionary zeal at its height, brought it immediately under Friendly concern, as well as for colonization. 

Within seven years after Jamaica came into the possession of the British, two Friends had died there, Ann Robinson and 

Oswell Heritage. John Taylor, writing in 1664, said, “I stayed there two years alone. I preached the gospel in my own hired house at Port Cogway and hired another at Spanish Town, then the capital city, and had meetings there also and several other meetings were settled in the Island." 

We have already referred to George Fox's visit to Jamaica in 1671 and to Elizabeth Hooton's death there early in 1872 in another chapter. The record of George Fox's visit with twelve companions is brief. He found a large number of 

Friends, he wrote his wife, who were "generally well; and here is great convincement but things would be too large to write of. Sufferings in every place attend me; but the blessed seed is over all." We remember also, that he had "much service" and that many received the Truth. He "settled several meetings and brought Friends into pretty good order." 

William Edmondson, who had accompanied George Fox, returned to Jamaica in 1683. "We landed at Port Royal and came to John Wilmot's house," he wrote. "After having a meeting there we travelled to many parts of the island and had many meetings for the worship of God; also men's and women's meetings about church affairs."

Jamaica 123 

Thomas Chalkley went to Jamaica in 1707 and held meetings in the three chief towns but he found "that the people were generally very wicked." Thomas Storey followed two years later, holding several good meetings.

All these religious visits of Friends up to this period appear to have been chiefly to the English Friends who had gone as colonists. It is claimed that there were 9,500 in 

Jamaica at the beginning of the eighteenth century. There are records of at least three burial grounds used by Friends; in Spanish Town, in Liguanea, a few miles northeast of Kingston, and in Lannia, near Black River. There was a meeting in Kingston in 1740 and a Friends School in connection with it and these are the only traces we have of this early group. It is thought many of the colonists retired to 

England when they had sufficient means, some came to the American colonies, and the rest probably left Jamaica and returned to England at the time the slaves were freed. Gilbert Bowles in Jamaica and Friends Missions adds: "The luxury, vice, and worldliness of the colonists were not favorable to the spirit of Quakerism."

The second coming of Friends to Jamaica was definitely in the interests of the Negroes. Joseph Sturge and Thomas Harvey went there in 1837 to investigate the apprenticeship system which was being tried as a substitute for slavery. "They freely exposed the cruelty of the masters to public opinion." 

Joseph John Gurney and others made religious visits in the next ten years to the free Black population. But it was the 

Negro rebellion in 1865 which revived the interest of the Society of Friends in England as a whole in the condition of the Negroes in this British colony. At a meeting of Friends in London in January 1866, we find them expressing sympathy for the Negroes, lamenting the harsh measures which were used to suppress the rebellion, and appointing two 

Friends, Thomas Harvey and William Brewin, to go to Ja-

maica to investigate the situation and render whatever aid they could. A general subscription was taken to meet the expenses of the journey and the matter placed in the hands of a committee for Negro Education. The committee gave aid to some of the schools already established by other denominations. English Friends' interest in Negro education had its influence on Negroes in America as we have seen in the story of the Southland Fund which received a large sum from Joseph Sturge.

American Friends were brought into the Jamaican scene through Evi Sharpless, a young minister of Iowa Yearly Meeting, in 1881. It would be interesting to know what combination of forces led this young man to come before Stuart Monthly Meeting with a concern to be liberated for service in Jamaica. He may have been stirred by stories of conditions of the Negroes in Jamaica from the writings of Thomas Chalkley and others, by the interest of English Friends in them, or by the fairly recent emancipation of our own Negroes. Certainly, through prayer and seeking he had been brought from his quiet Iowa community to service in what was then a fairly distant and obviously "wicked" country. 

His concern was carried to Bear Creek Quarterly Meeting and then to Iowa Yearly Meeting, which body "released" him for the work. There was evidence of very real sense of Divine leading as Evi Sharpless answered the call. The yearly meeting encouraged William Marshall of Iowa to accompany him and the two prepared at once for the journey. 

These Friends arrived in Jamaica in November, 1881, carrying minutes from the yearly meeting which helped open the way for service. As in the case of other Friends who had journeyed to other lands, they found missions already established. There were Scotch Presbyterians, English Baptists,

English Wesleyan Methodists, and Moravians who gave them a cordial welcome. There was an Episcopal Church,

Jamaica 125 also, but it then ministered chiefly to the small English population. This church has become the dominant religious body of the Island, including all races in its membership. 

William Marshall did not stay long but Evi Sharpless remained alone working with the missionaries in utmost freedom. It was not until 1883 that the way opened for him to start a mission. This he did at the request of the people of 

Cedar Valley. In their eagerness for a mission they erected a temporary booth for a meetinghouse. A tropical rain destroyed it at the first meeting so they secured the upper room of a large coffee house on the Cedar Valley estate instead. 

The room became not only meetinghouse, but study and bedroom for Evi Sharpless. A candidate class was organized and the beginnings of a settled work made. 

The missionary session of Iowa Yearly Meeting in 1883 was a memorable one. Isaac Sharp of England, world traveller, told them of the work of English Friends in Madagascar, India, Syria, and Palestine; Eliza C. Armstrong, presi dent of the Women's Foreign Missionary Society of Western 

Yearly Meeting and pioneer in the women's missionary movement, told of their interest in the work in Mexico and the Girls Training Home in Brumanna, Syria; and William 

Marshall laid the needs of Jamaica on their hearts. They had followed the course of Evi Sharpless' work for two years with much interest, and were familiar with the work of Samuel Purdie in Mexico which they supported. The time was ripe for Iowa Yearly Meeting to go forward and before the sessions ended the following minute was adopted: "Resolved that in view of the demand for missionary work in Jamaica, the time has come for Friends to establish and support a mission station on that island, and we recommend that 

Friends of Iowa Yearly Meeting consider that their special field." Funds which had been going to Mexico were to be diverted to Jamaica and the Missionary Board was author-

ized to raise the money for the purpose by voluntary sub scription. The Women's Foreign Missionary Society was but recently organized and ready to take its full share of responsibility. They were probably responsible to a large extent for the action of the Yearly Meeting, as women's societies were in so many instances. 

Jesse and Elizabeth R. Townsend of Iowa City attended yearly meeting that year under a sense of expectancy. They had felt a call to missionary work but were not clear where they were to go. They prayed that the Lord would open the way for service soon. The appeal for a teacher and general helper for Jamaica spoke directly to them and they readily responded. They sailed for Jamaica in November of 1883 and went at once to the Cedar Valley Mission. Evi Sharpless turned over the work to them and continued the evangelistic work to which he had felt specially called. 

The Townsends found a congregation of about one hundred and fifty persons which met for weekly meetings.

There was also a large candidates class which met for study one night a week. Evi Sharpless had already sent some names to his own meeting at Stuart, Iowa, and they had been accepted into membership. The Townsends in the course of time sent a list of applicants to Springdale Monthly Meeting. Eventually, on July 4, 1885, the first monthly meeting was organized in Jamaica with a membership of sixty-one. 

Meanwhile a new building had been put up for meeting and school and mission home. Within another year, the Glen 

Haven property was bought for $800 for a Girls Training Home. This property, two miles from Cedar Valley, was a beautiful home for the missionaries and the girls they took into it.

While the Townsends were laboring at Cedar Valley, Evi Sharpless opened a mission at Seaside in the east end of the

Jamaica 127 island and at Amity Hall five and a half miles from it in St. 

Thomas parish. These three locations are centers of our work today. At Seaside a chapel and a cottage were built under interesting conditions. Evi Sharpless made a trip to 

America in May 1885 to enlist support for his work in New York and other yearly meetings. When he was in New England, he told Timothy B. Hussey that he had come to get $1,000 for a chapel for Seaside. Timothy Hussey is reported to have said: "Well, Evi, when thee gets the thousand dollars, I will go to Jamaica with thee and put up the building." 

So successful was Evi Sharpless that when he returned to 

Jamaica Timothy B. and Anna Hussey and Emeline Tuttle, a minister, sailed with him and on the boat was lumber fitted and ready for a chapel thirty-two by fifty feet, a cottage twelve by sixteen feet, and some household furniture. Timothy Hussey supervised the building, managing it with *"grace and energy, teaching the people the much-needed lessons of honesty and accuracy." Seaside Chapel was dedicated on the last day of the year, 1885.

Feeling that his work was done, Evi Sharpless returned to the States in 1886. He had blazed trails others would build. 

But trouble came to the little mission so well begun. Jesse 

Townsend's health was not good so the family moved to 

Seaside. Unfortunately their little son, who had not stood the climate very well, sickened and died. The Townsends tried to keep on but early in 1887, they, too, returned to the States. Other workers had been sent out but none remained long. Naomi George, an Earlham College student, was sent out in 1888 for the Seaside School. Assisted by Mary Dillon, daughter of Josiah Dillon who spent a few years in Jamaica, she carried on but her health broke and she remained only until 1891.

In ten years ten missionaries had gone and all had come 

Friends in Jamaica, Gilbert Bowles.

home. They had been trying years, years of learning. When the missionaries had gone out, there was no body of knowledge to guide them or the Board in matters of preparation or care of health. Appointments had been indefinite. The converts on the field had suffered, too, from a sense of insecurity and uncertainty, and the fear of being left. The temporary character of the work kept many from joining the mission. 

The future of the Jamaica Mission was very uncertain in 1891. 

Then, as so often happens, some hopeful signs appeared on the horizon. In the spring of 1891, Evi Sharpless at home in Iowa received an invitation to return to Seaside.

The small group of Friends living there had collected funds to pay his expenses on the boat from Boston. But he did not go alone. He took with him Arthur H. Swift, a young man from Worcester, Massachusetts. Arthur Swift had visited Jamaica in 1889 and had felt called to return. The illness of Naomi George left the school at Seaside without a teacher so he went with Evi Sharpless to take her place. 

He was the first of the great company that was to give their lives to Jamaica. Evi Sharpless remained three months, greatly strengthening the meetings, then left Arthur Swift, for a time the only representative of American Friends on the island.

Evidently Evi Sharpless was able to arouse interest in Jamaica when he returned to the States, for at Iowa Yearly Meeting in 1891, the Christian Endeavor Union reported to the missionary session of the yearly meeting that they were ready to pledge four hundred dollars toward the support of a missionary. Gilbert L. Farr of Oskaloosa, Iowa offered himself for service in Jamaica, was accepted, and left within a month or two under a five-year appointment. 

The next year his daughter Helen and son Arthur joined him and helped greatly in the work. He married Anna Mc-

Jamaica 129 Pherson of New Sharon, Iowa, in 1892, and the establishment of the new family for permanent residence gave the work a fresh impetus. Gilbert L. Farr took charge of the work at Seaside and Arthur H. Swift at Amity Hall. This was a splendid adjustment and as the two men labored the confidence of the Jamaicans grew, also.

Sorrow struck again and for a brief time the future of Arthur Swift's work was threatened, but the Divine Hand was upon it and it went on. Naomi George returned to Jamaica in 1892 to marry Arthur Swift. Before a year passed she and her husband both suffered serious illnesses and she was not strong enough to overcome hers. His own illness and sorrow over the death of his young wife left Arthur Swift in a serious condition. He was advised to go to his home in 

Massachusetts for a time to recover his health but he returned to Jamaica in 1894.

Within the next ten years there were sent to the field a company of women who were to give the work continuity, consecration, and Christ-like devotion for the rest of their lives.

The first of these women was Alma Penrose of Lawrence, Kansas to work among the people at Amity Hall. She and 

Arthur Swift were married in 1894, and their united service brought much blessing to the young people of Jamaica. After Arthur Swift's death in 1909 she continued alone until she passed away in 1934. 

Mary E. White of Spiceland, Indiana and New Sharon, Iowa went to Jamaica in 1895. Her ministry has been rich and varied. She has assisted with preaching, teaching, and home visiting. Although she is retired from active service, she finds continued opportunities to serve the people she has loved for almost fifty years. 

The third one was Alsina M. Andrews of Iowa. She joined

the mission in 1897. Almost her first duty was to nurse her gifted sister, Sarah B. Andrews, in an illness which proved fatal. Alsina Andrews is still active although officially retired. A woman of exceptional talents, she has been a tower of strength to the mission.

Sada F. Stanley of Ohio was sent out in 1900. She has taught, preached, and served as matron of the Lyndale Home for many years. Also retired, she gives of herself to the meetings in the spirit that has characterized all her years of service.

The fifth one was Alice I. Kennedy of Crawford County, Illinois. She has cared for meetings and done general missionary work. Through her home and church she is still working, although like the others, she is retired. 

The mission was greatly blessed by this company of permanent workers. Others came for shorter periods through the years but these splendid women, the Farrs, and Arthur Swift formed a group on whom the Jamaicans could depend for the understanding that comes from long, loving relationships.

Another messenger was introduced into the mission that was to have a long and useful service. That was the Friends 

Jamaica Mission, a monthly paper. This was first published in 1893 on a hand-press. Later on, Friends sent a foot-power press and for more than ten years the paper went to Friends all over America. Someone called it the little paper that saved the mission. With its debut, one might say, the mission at last was sure of itself and its message, and ready to help 

Jamaicans face the complexities of the new century.

Since it would take long to go into all the details of each meeting and school, perhaps the best way to see what has been happening in Friends Jamaica Mission will be to look at the work of the yearly meeting today, and the educational

Jamaica 131 movement which in Highgate has culminated in the Friends 

Educational Council. 

YEARLY MEETING 

Evi Sharpless went to Jamaica in 1881. Exactly sixty years later a yearly meeting was set up. This was the realization of a long felt need, a hope that was fittingly fulfilled in the jubilee year. Sixty years may seem a long time but progress was slow and Jamaican Friends wanted to be sure they were ready to assume more responsibility before they committed themselves. They did not wish to sever the tie with Friends in the United States which had meant so much to them through the years but the Board encouraged them to take this step.

The Friends in Jamaica who applied for a yearly meeting to the 1940 sessions of the Five Years Meeting, had reason to feel that they had a good foundation on which to build. 

There are at present twelve Friends churches in Jamaica with a membership of about one thousand, and seventy-five candidates. These churches are served by three recognized native pastors and the missionaries. They have the usual program of meeting activities with special emphasis on young people's work.

When one considers the beginnings of the work and the tremendous social, economic, and religious problems that our workers faced, that is an achievement of no mean proportions on the part of both Jamaicans and Americans. 

We recall that the first monthly meeting was organized at Cedar Valley on July 4, 1885 with sixty-one members. Seaside Monthly Meeting was set up in 1888. They both suffered when the missionaries left. When Cedar Valley was reorganized by authority of Springdale, Iowa, Quarterly

Meeting on July 4, 1897 there were only nineteen, young and old who claimed membership. But the Swifts and the Farrs

and others had worked hard and the report sent to Iowa Yearly Meeting in 1900, gives a splendid account of the mission. 

Three monthly meetings had been revived and the membership was three hundred and thirty-two. There were twelve 

Sunday Schools with more than seven hundred children enrolled.

The wisdom of sending out workers for longer, definite periods of service was beginning to be realized. From 1900 on, there was continued, steady growth. The missionaries wisely took plenty of time to teach applicants for membership in the Society of Friends, Bible truths and the beliefs and practices of Friends. Milo Hinckle, during his ministry, prepared a valuable little pamphlet in question and answer form for understanding the latter, adapting the language and forms of Friends to the comprehension and background of Jamaicans. This careful preparation did not attract great numbers to the Society but it insured thoughtful, understanding members.

Another sign of growth in the indigenous group was the setting up of a Central Evangelistic Board in 1928 appointed by the Annual Conference upon the approval of the monthly meetings. This body was to guide and help teachers and ministers in all co-operative work and from it has come the organization of the yearly meeting itself.

Because of the burden of work laid on the pastors and teachers, the board sent Wendell and Faye Farr to Jamaica in 1943. They had already spent a term in Jamaica and 

Wendell spent a few years there as a boy with his father,

Gilbert L. Farr, so they are unusually well-equipped to serve the Highgate Meeting. Wendell Farr is also superintendent of the yearly meeting.

In addition to these centers of work, there is a Quaker group in Kingston which is organized on the traditional Quaker method without a pastor. All the other meetings

Jamaica 133 have found the pastoral system best suited to their needs. 

This group is made up of leaders in their respective fields, law, medicine, education, and religion. They are an influential group and eager to keep alive Friends testimonies in that old center where Friends were once so active. This revival of a meeting in Kingston after almost two hundred years is significant.

EDUCATIONAL WORK 

Religion and education have gone hand in hand in Jamaica as in all Friends mission fields. The need of both was so obvious to the first missionaries that both activities began simultaneously. The room in the coffee-house at Cedar Valley served as meetinghouse on Sunday and school room on Monday. Perhaps nowhere has educational work been more discouraging or more rewarding.

A missionary to Jamaica in 1824 found there, “a people torn from their native environment, with no language of their own, with the moral and social sanctions which acted as controls in Africa obliterated. They were a pagan people whose contact with European civilization had taught them nothing but its vices and prejudices. They were broken by long oppression, brutalized, ignorant, habituated to deceit." 

More than one hundred years later, in 1942, in The Church and the New Jamaica, Dr. Davis says: "Ninety per cent of the island population live in scattered villages, hamlets, and homesteads, and upon the large estates are aggregations of workers housed in barracks or cottages. . . . Outwardly conforming to the sanctions of the white man's system of life, the bulk of the people are still swayed by other than Anglo- Saxon or Christian sanctions. The obeah man, with his sorcery and witch-craft, holds the power of success or even of life and death over many people. Pocomania groups and emotional religious sects give outlets for the untamed emo-

tions which church and school taboo. The young people discover opportunities for recreation and self-expression which are not supplied them by the church. The outward controls and patterns are Christian, but the inner, intimate life of the countryside is still in large part African, pagan, superstitious, and undisciplined."

Jamaica is predominantly Negro and colored. They form about ninety-eight per cent of the population. The Spanish, then the English, imported them in large numbers to work on sugar plantations. The colonists did not become permanent, and large areas of the land were owned by absentee landowners and as a result the Negro population has become the largest element in the country. The cessation of the slave trade in 1808 followed by freeing them in all parts of the British Empire in 1833 marked the beginning of a new era in the history of Jamaica. From then on the population would increase according to the fertility of the people, the capacity of the country to support them, and the general improvement of social and sanitary conditions.

Many missions were established at this time, and they faced a hard task. Perhaps no secular body of that era could have tackled it. It required faith in God's love for His creatures and His power to charge men's lives for such a task. 

The entire population of Negroes, which was practically the population of Jamaica, was illiterate. They had been dominated by the strange civilization of their masters without being educated to understand it. The second problem and one which is still the major social and economic problem of Jamaica is the high rate of illegitimacy. In a slave society, marriage was simply ignored. Slaves formed their own attachments, permanent or temporary, and neither their masters nor the church was concerned. Men predominated among the slaves as they were more necessary for the plantations than women and this increased promiscuity. For one

Jamaica 135 hundred years the church, government and social agencies have been attempting to control the situation, yet the most recent statistics, 1943, give the rate of illegitimacy at 71.6%. 

Illiteracy is estimated at 50%.

These facts will help us to understand why the first workers realized that the long, slow, sometimes tortuous path of education was absolutely essential. We can also readily understand why boarding-schools seemed essential, too, in order to get the children into an atmosphere of wholesome living and insure regularity in attendance at school. 

While schools had been carried on from the beginning, it was not until 1898, when Alsina M. Andrews went to Seaside to open a school for under-privileged girls, Jamaican and East Indian, that a good start was made. The East Indians in Jamaica have had a peculiar appeal to Friends. East 

Indians had been brought in under the indenture system after slavery ceased. They kept their language, customs, and religion. A highly intelligent, industrious, creative people, they needed to be brought into the stream of Jamaican life. At Happy Grove the two races learned to live in friendly comradeship. Within a few years, a boys' unit was added and the school has continued as a coeducational institution ever since. The emphasis is on agriculture, the sciences, home economics, and human relationships. Montclair Hoffman, known to Friends in America through his frequent visits to this country on behalf of the school, served as principal for many years. Kenneth Crooks, a Jamaican with a degree from Harvard and several years of teaching experience at Hampton Institute, Virginia, has succeeded him. The school is inadequately housed, yet with its enrollment of seventy boys it has a vital place in the life of the island. 

There are six day schools maintained on Friends property, but supported and directed by the government. The mission appoints for each school a manager who co-operates

with the government in selecting teachers who will carry on in the spirit of Friends. 

Much of the educational program of Friends in Jamaica is centered in Highgate where there are five separate institutions. These are Lyndale Home for Girls, Swift-Purscell 

Home and Industrial School for Boys, the Continuation School, Friends College, and the Nursery School. 

Lyndale Home was preceded by a home for orphaned East 

Indian girls at Happy Grove Girls School. When that school became coeducational in 1921, the Home was moved to Highgate and named Lyndale Girls Home. Sada F. Stanley was the successful head of the Home from 1921 to 1941. The school accommodates between thirty and forty girls. Teaching emphasis is on music, crafts, gardening, butlering, and home-making. Jamaican, East Indian, and white girls live together. The present principal is Myra B. Binford who had many years of experience in America as matron, dietitian, and dean of women. The young people of Iowa Yearly Meeting have taken a special interest in Lyndale, usually supporting a girl for a period of years.

When Alma Swift took a few orphan boys into her Buff Bay home during the influenza epidemic, in 1919, she did not realize she was starting a school. By 1928 she had twentynine boys to look after. When Paul and Margaret Michener of Iowa were sent out that year to take charge of the boys the "orphanage" was moved to Highgate and named Glenleigh. The academic work was simple and much stress was laid on agriculture, carpentry, and industrial subjects. 

Martha White, niece of Mary White, took charge in 1933, until a principal could be found. It was not until 1938 that Harry and Esther Wellons were sent out so that Martha White gave five years in the school.

The children, most of them, are sent there by the government and supported by it. They range in age from five to

Jamaica 137 sixteen and the enrollment is around forty. The school, on the advice of the government, was combined with a similar one called the Purscell Home which operated on a trust fund of $30,000 left by Mrs. Purscell of Jamaica for "an orphanage for needy children." A larger property more suitably located was bought and the old school, Glenleigh, was moved there in 1937. The new location demanded a new name and the one chosen suitably honors the two women who had been so interested in the orphan children of Jamaica. 

The school continues to carry on an industrial program combined with elementary academic work. Most of the work of the school is done by the boys, including shoe-making and tailoring. It costs $150 a year to educate a boy, part of this coming from the farm and the shop, part from the trust fund, and the remainder from the government. 

Friends College was organized chiefly for training in social welfare work. It is almost the only training of this kind which is given in Jamaica. A small group of young men and women work together with their teachers in fine comradeship on practical social problems. In addition to handcrafts, they study clinics, day nurseries, and community activities of all kinds. 

The Continuation School attempts to meet the needs of girls who are out of the regular school system but who wish further academic training as well as practical experience in domestic science and hand-crafts. It also prepares for the 

Pupil-Teacher examination for which there are a number of candidates each year. The enrollment averages around fifty although attendance is not very regular. 

The Nursery School gives the girls in the Continuation School practical training in teaching and working with children. There are about forty pupils enrolled. 

These five institutions were all within a radius of four

miles, each under separate management. A few years ago 

Friends began to dream of uniting them all in one location and under one head. A Friends Educational Council was formed to work out a plan of union. A gift of eighty-one acres of land near the Swift-Purscell Home and an endowment fund of $112,000 have been made to the Council by a Friend in Jamaica. Some of the school buildings have already been moved to the new location, a beautiful place at a good elevation which commands a fine view of the sea, six miles away.

Harry Wellons, head of the Swift-Purscell Home and Industrial School for Boys, is principal of the Council. He has resigned but has returned to Jamaica for two years to help work out the new program. The story of the Friends Educational Council is just begun. It has made a good beginning but the remaining chapters will be written in the lives of the young people of Jamaica who will benefit by the vision of those who see the unfolding of that story in the years ahead. 

Through the meetings and schools, through the lives of devoted men and women, American and Jamaican, a new chapter in the history of Friends in Jamaica has been written in the past sixty-three years. What the future holds one cannot know. The Society of Friends is exceedingly small on the island; one thousand out of a population of 1,050,000 seems incredibly few. Nearly three hundred years of British rule (1655-1944) and more than a hundred years of missions would seem at times to have made little impression on the people. Yet a big step forward has been made by the government in its recent act of granting Jamaica a constitution. 

Great Britain may be testing out a new type of rule for all the colonies, in Jamaica. The people are trying to forget differences of race and are saying, "We are not Negro, East Indian, whites-we are Jamaicans." They are eager to prove their readiness for self-government.

Jamaica 139 One wonders if the problems of illiteracy, illegitimacy, and promiscuity which have baffled the church and the state for so long will be solved when Jamaicans realize that homes, and security and education are essentials for children in a country that is developing its own government. 

As the new Jamaica rises to the challenge of its new responsibilities, the seed that has been sown through the past century, “in the love of the gospel," in the schools and churches may be seen to bear a richer harvest than any one has dared to dream of.

The people of Jamaica have rich gifts to bring to the family of nations: rare gifts of song, poetry, imagination, and music. They need to be free from superstition. The obeah man still thrives, more dangerous than in Africa because he masquerades often as a preacher with a Bible in his hand. 

The task of Christian Missions is not ended in Jamaica, but the modern missionary must understand the forces that have beat upon these people for at least three hundred years and be ready with tact, understanding and Christian love to help them attain the abundant life. 

Perhaps Wendell Farr's impressions on his return to Jamaica in 1943 suggest something of the vision Jamaicans have for their country and the attitude of the missionary who would seek to help. Although expressed in modern terms, the heart of the message is unchanged; the application of Christianity to all of life, the sacredness of human personality, the eternal truth that "there is that of God" in everyone, and that through the life and teachings of Jesus mankind can be brought to the throne of God. Wendell Farr wrote as follows: "General conditions give the impression of much agitation, talking, and scheming to try to improve the economic, political, social, and educational defects in Jamaican life.

Land settlement schemes in a number of localities are striving to increase the proportion of owners of small homesteads. "The situation there today suggests many opportunities for Friends in the future. Certainly here is a chance to apply such Quaker principles as the religious significance of all human situations; the importance of every personality; the measuring of all progress in terms of human values, not by institutional statistics. Besides infusing our ideals into the changing scene, perhaps Friends may aid effectively through small-scale experiments in solving human difficulties. All of which calls for resources-financial, personal, and spiritual."

CHAPTER IX PALESTINE 

The beginnings of three Friends mission stations were made in the twelve years between 1869 and 1881. These years also mark the height of the evangelical movement in the Church in the United States. In each case, the missions grew out of the concerns of individuals and in only one was there an organization to establish a mission, in the accepted sense of the word. That was in Mexico in 1871, and the organization was the Friends Foreign Missionary Association, a voluntary association of Friends of Indiana Yearly Meeting. 

Four years before that, however, Eli and Sybil Jones asked to be liberated by China (Maine) Monthly Meeting for service in Europe and the Near East. These were not young

Friends and this was not their first religious journey. Sybil 

Jones had a great influence on the Society of Friends during the middle years of the nineteenth century, both in Europe and America, being a preacher of great power and spiritual depth. Their meeting, Vassalboro Quarterly Meeting, and
