New England Yearly Meeting readily liberated these Friends for what proved to be an eventful journey. A large company, which included the governor of the State of Massachusetts and a general, saw them off at Boston on April tenth, 1867. 

They did not arrive in Syria until nearly the end of October as they travelled in England, Scotland and France rather extensively in the ministry before going on. The American 

Presbyterian Board had been at work in Syria for forty years so they found a vigorous work going on there. There were also British Missions in Syria doing splendid work.

These missionaries welcomed the New England Friends gladly, inviting them to visit their schools and churches and opening the way for them to a wide ministry. In those early days visitors from the homeland were few. Eli and Sybil 

Jones entering into their work with sympathy and understanding must have heartened them. And the hearts of the visitors were deeply touched by the work that was being done, particularly for the women and girls of Mount Lebanon.

The rigors of the winter climate of Syria coupled with many arduous trips in the villages around Beirut, however, were too much for Sybil Jones whose health was at best very delicate and the party was compelled to return to England early in 1868. Rest in England restored Sybil Jones to fair health and with returning health came an insistent reminder that her task in the Near East was not complete. 

On the first trip to Syria, they were accompanied by Alfred Lloyd Fox of Falmouth, England, and Ellen Clare Miller of Edinburgh, Scotland. They were invaluable companions, taking care of many of the details of the journey and entering into the mission faithfully and tenderly. We owe much to Ellen Clare Miller for many excellent accounts of the trip. She accompanied them again, lovingly easing the way for Sybil Jones, still somewhat frail. 

While Sybil Jones was convalescing in England, Alfred 

Lloyd Fox and Ellen Clare Miller had been interesting English Friends in the needs of Syria and had collected quite a large sum of money to send back to the missionaries already working there. Some of it was gone when the second trip was planned but the remainder was given to Sybil Jones to use as she wished.

It was on the occasion of the second visit, in May 1869, that the party of Friends visited Ramallah, a village ten miles north of Jerusalem. There was a small school for boys in

Palestine 143 Ramallah but none for girls. The story is told that one morning, a young girl living there, whose father was a teacher, asked Eli Jones if he would not open a school for girls. When he asked who would teach such a school, she surprised him by answering, "I could." She had been fortunate enough to have been a student at Talitha Cumi, a German deaconness school in Jerusalem, and so was better qualified to teach than anyone else in the village. Her earnestness touched the hearts of the visitors and they left money with their interpreter, Joseph Hishmeh, for a day school. When they returned to England and told the story of the Ramallah school, English Friends, who had already organized a Provisional Committee for Foreign Gospel Service, immediately assumed responsibility for it and supported it until 1888, when 

New England took it over.

The Americans journeyed north again to Syria, after a few days, where a second episode occurred which was to have far-reaching effect and was proof of God's leading in 

Sybil Jones' call to this second visit.

One man who heard them speak was Theophilus Waldemeier, a German who had recently come from missionary service in Abyssinia. He was a teacher in the British Syrian schools at the time. After hearing the New England Friends he was drawn to the Society of Friends and wrote later, "Their addresses were so powerful and edifying, that our hearts were touched, and I began to think that their religious principles must be of a superior nature. I went to the hotel where they lodged and made their acquaintance, and from that time I have believed that the Quaker principles are the right basis for a true spiritual church. When these dear 

Friends left the country, their blessed influence remained upon my heart though they had not the slightest idea of it, nor had I any hope of seeing them again."

Eli and Sybil Jones, their mission accomplished, felt clear

to return home that same year. But God had not guided these Friends in vain, for Theophilus Waldemeier, through the further influence of English Friends joined the Society about two years later and started the splendid work for Friends in Syria that is being carried on today by the Friends 

Service Council of London Yearly Meeting. 

One of the most fanatical villages in Syria was Brumanna, about ten miles from Beirut. Once when some American missionaries attempted to open a mission there, they were expelled from the village and their Bibles and Testaments burned. Theophilus Waldemeier, now a Friend, knew of the situation in Brumanna and being eager to get into missionary work again was challenged to go there. The year 1873, four years after his eventful meeting with Eli and Sybil 

Jones, found the Waldemeier family living in Brumanna.

But he had no means of support and was not able to do much. 

In his distress, he wrote to Hannah Allen of England for help. Hannah Allen forwarded the letter to Eli Jones. It was close to yearly meeting time when the letter arrived, and not knowing what else to do, Eli Jones decided to take it with him to present to Friends "if the way opened." 

By one of those beautiful cases of leading which are so clear that the Divine Spirit at the heart is unmistakable, Charles F. Coffin, a Friend of Indiana Yearly Meeting, attended New England Yearly Meeting that year. The work which the Indiana Association had established in Mexico two years before was bringing a great blessing to the yearly meeting. He made an earnest appeal to New England

Friends to identify themselves with some definite mission work also. That opened the way for Eli Jones to present the letter from Theophilus Waldemeier. Friends were interested, fifty dollars was collected at once and sent to Theophilus Waldemeier, and Eli Jones was asked to write to him. to see how his religious views agreed with those of Friends.

Palestine 145 In due time a satisfactory answer was received, further interest was stimulated, and Eli Jones was able to write to him: “I am glad to be able to say that our Friends in New as well as Old England seem much interested in thy work in 

Mount Lebanon. I think that thyself and dear wife and your helpers may be encouraged to give yourselves to the work of the Lord there, with full trust that your temporal wants will be supplied." English Friends united in this new mission and committees, secretaries, and treasurers were appointed. On the other hand, individuals in America sent money to the English Committee for Ramallah. 

Sybil Jones was not to see the fruits of her labors for, in 

Twelfth Month, 1873 she went to the heavenly home, to which she had lived so close all her life. However, her husband was to have a great part in the Brumanna Mission. 

Rufus Jones in his Life of Eli and Sybil Jones says, "He worked for it, begged for it, and prayed for it." He went to 

Syria in 1876 with a letter from his yearly meeting expressing the belief that the time had come to organize a monthly meeting in Brumanna. This was done with six native Christians and the Waldemeier family as the membership. He started the Boys Training Home at this time. 

Four years later, in 1880, an appeal came to New England Yearly Meeting for funds for a training home for girls at Brumanna. In reply, Eli Jones wrote: "At New England 

Yearly Meeting, thy appeal for a girls' training home was read and was given a ready and remarkable response. Soon after the meeting we found that the subscription had reached eleven hundred dollars. The women Friends of 

New York Meeting also raised two hundred dollars thus making a total of thirteen hundred dollars in the hands of our treasurer, George Howland, for the purpose of starting a school for girls on Mount Lebanon."

The new building was completed in 1882, and Eli Jones,

then in his seventy-ninth year, again undertook the long journey to Syria to be present at the dedication of the school. 

He and Charles M. Jones of Winthrop, Maine, who had accompanied him, remained through the winter to attend to the business end of things. A board of trustees consisting of three English and three American Friends was set up to be responsible for the mission. 

The work expanded to take in villages around Brumanna. 

It captured the hearts and imagination of Friends generally -the thought of a mission on Mount Lebanon gave a glow to the whole missionary program. It appealed especially to the women's societies. New York, Western, Philadelphia, Ohio Women's Foreign Missionary Societies, as well as New

England, all helped with the program of activities, sending money for the support of students and a Bible woman and the building. Individuals in other yearly meetings were also giving generously.

By the year 1887, it became clear that a distinct division of the two fields in the Near East would simplify administration. English delegates to the Friends Conference in Richmond, Indiana that year consulted New England Friends on the subject and plans were made for English Friends to take over the work in Brumanna, and American Friends, Ramallah. Timothy B. and Anna Hussey were appointed by New 

England Yearly Meeting to go to Ramallah the following year to attend to the business of taking over the properties and other details of the new venture, and look to opening a school for girls, which had been contemplated before the division. Joseph J. Mills, president of Earlham College and 

Sarah Hussey, sister of Timothy, accompanied them. They stopped in England on the way where papers for the transfer of properties were duly signed and the Ramallah Mission became the official responsibility of New England Yearly 

Meeting, Seventh Month, First, 1888.

Palestine 147 The mission at Ramallah was named at first the Eli and Sybil Jones Mission in memory of the two faithful ministers who had been obedient to the call to serve and to whose brief but eloquent ministry in Palestine the work owed its beginnings.

Ramallah, in 1888, was described as a village of 3,000 people, most of whom belonged to the Greek Orthodox or Latin (Roman Catholic) Churches, surrounded by Moslem villages. Friends owned three acres of land "on the highest point of ground near the village on which is already erected a fine building for educational purposes." A later report states that some of the strongest Friends in Palestine, members of the Ramallah Meeting, were brought into the Society during the first years of mission work there. 

There were day schools in Ramallah, Ain Arick, and Jiffna.

Medical work was carried on by Dr. George Hassenauer, a 

German reared in Palestine and trained in England, who spoke Arabic fluently. He and his wife had charge of the mission for five years. Although there was not yet a monthly meeting, services were held regularly after the manner of Friends, and younger men's Bible classes were held in the villages where there were schools. The pioneer days were over to an extent, but starting a boarding school for girls was a pioneer adventure, too. Thanks to the work already done, it was not so hard or so tedious as it might have been. 

Plans for the boarding-school advanced speedily. One thinks of a school beginning with pupils but in Ramallah it began with a building. This building "on the highest point of land" in the village was most suitable but without furnishings. The Americans immediately began to fit it up for a home for a small number of girls. Timothy B. Hussey went to Beirut, Syria, for some of the furniture, while Anna and Sarah Hussey with some helpers made sheets and pillowcases and other necessary equipment.

The problem of finding a teacher was the next big one to face. "The educational centers around Beirut and the 

Lebanons were faithfully searched, but failure to find one suited to the position seemed to meet us on every hand. . . . 

In a few weeks, however, we received a telegram from Theophilus Waldemeier informing us that he had found a most superior teacher to lead in our work, one who had been at the head of a large school for girls in Beirut for several years, and was admirably qualified for the position we wanted to fill," wrote Timothy B. Hussey in telling the story of the Eli and Sybil Jones Mission. The teacher was Miss 

Katie Gabriel and her life was to be identified with the Girls 

School for the next forty years. Huldah H. Leighton of New 

England went out as matron in 1889.

Getting students was no small task. Comparatively few people in Palestine had an education at all, and those few were mostly men and boys. They did not readily accept the idea of a school for girls, particularly a boarding school.

Only one person applied. One man who had met Eli Jones wrote when he heard of the proposed school, “For the sake of Eli Jones, I hope you will receive my daughter into Thou's

School." His daughter Lydia was accepted and remained six years. The rest were gathered in after many discouraging visits to many villages. The staff from the first decided to try to get girls from as many villages as possible in order to spread the influence of the school. Fifteen girls were finally gathered in from Jaffa, Lydda, Ramleh, Jerusalem, Ophrah, Jiffneh, Cana of Galilee, Shichem, Beirut, Ramath 

Gilead, and Ramallah. The Girls Training School opened the first of Tenth Month, 1889. This was a small beginning in an obscure village in Palestine by a group of people who knew little of the needs of the country as a whole, but out of their religious faith and unquestioning belief in the power of love they builded far, far better than they knew and the school

Palestine 

149 started with so much difficulty was to grow in increasing service to the womanhood of Palestine. 

The next development in Palestine was the setting up of a monthly meeting. When Eli and Sybil Jones went on religious visits, they were chiefly concerned to preach the gospel of love and so to reach the souls of men with the power of the message of Jesus to change men's lives. It was very natural then, that from the beginning of the work there had been a real concern for the meeting. A house was rented for ten years to serve as a place of worship in connection with the school. It could accommodate one hundred and sixty people but the attendance at first was not that large. A monthly meeting was organized in 1890 with a membership of thirty-five people. It was laid down within five years "as 

Friends did not feel equal to the task of carrying it themselves." However, it was revived in 1903 by Elihu and Almy 

Grant and has carried on continuously since that time. It is the only monthly meeting that has ever been set up in Palestine.

With the setting-up of a monthly meeting the need of a suitable meetinghouse arose. The Ramallah people bought the land for this in 1916. It was an excellent site, half-way between the Girls Training Home and the land that had been purchased for the Boys School and cost about three hundred dollars. Later, through the efforts of David and Marguerite 

Alsop, Haverford Friends contributed $1,000 for the meetinghouse. Timothy B. Hussey describes the joy of the people in the building as follows: "Friends of Ramallah are sweetly united, and the stone work of their new Meeting House which they have been building is now nearly completed. 

The meetings are conducted quite after the order of early Friends; often seasons of silent devotion, followed by prayer and scripture." The building was actually completed that fall. It was dedicated Third Month 6, 1910 on Timothy B.

Hussey's seventy-ninth birthday. "A firm wall of cut stone with an iron gate and a side-walk in front of the meetinghouse stands as the last token of love built by this untiring worker and his children as a memorial gift to their dear wife and mother." This excerpt from Rosa E. Lee's story of the 

Ramallah Mission seems a fitting eulogy to the service of Timothy B. Hussey and to Anna Hussey who had passed away in Ramallah a few months before.

BOYS SCHOOL 

The following paragraph is from the 1892 report of the Ramallah Mission and is probably the seed from which grew the decision to erect a Boys Training Home. "Now that the people of Ramallah have seen the good effect Christianity and education are having upon their daughters, they are asking for the same blessing for their sons. 

Often they say to us: 'Why do not your people provide a training home for our boys? Please, will you tell them that we love our boys, too, and want them also, to be trained rightly.' How all would rejoice if, in the near future we could see upon this "Hill of God" in Bible lands training homes for both boys and girls."

It was not until 1901, however, that the Christian Endeavor Union of New England Yearly Meeting made the opening of the Boys Training Home possible. Elihu and 

Almy Chase Grant were sent out from New England to have charge of the new work. Report says that eighty boys applied for admission but there was accommodation for only fifteen, and it is probably accurate to say that in every year since then, more boys have applied than could be accepted. 

The Grants retired from the mission after two years but had laid a good foundation for the school in that time and their interest in Ramallah was a continued help. 

Edward Kelsey and his wife, Mary Macomber Kelsey, ar-

Palestine 151 rived with their two children in Ramallah on November 8, 1903 to succeed the Grants. By spring of the next year Edward Kelsey wrote, "Dr. Merrill, our U. S. consul at Jerusalem, contends that we have the best girls school in Palestine, and I assure you that we shall not rest satisfied until our Boys Training Home is at least on a par with its sister." 

Neither he, nor Timothy Hussey, who came to assist in the purchase of the land and the erection of the building for the proposed Training Home could then see the long, hard, weary task that lay ahead before the splendid building that today houses the school could be opened for the boys of 

Palestine. 

The Kelsey family had to return to the homeland in 1907 on account of Mary M. Kelsey's health and Absalom and 

Florabelle Rosenberger were sent out as superintendents of the mission. Work on the new building was hindered at every turn by long delays in obtaining permits and so on and only prayer and faith kept hope in its completion alive. The 

Christian Endeavor Union of New England Yearly Meeting collected funds all through the years to the amount of several thousand dollars.

Edward Kelsey was able to return to Palestine in 1913 and under his supervision the building was finally completed. 

During the years between 1901 and 1914, the Boys School was held in a house in the village. They were never able to accommodate more than some thirty boys, including daystudents. The work was of necessity very elementary but the school was comparable to the average school in Palestine at that time. Only a few boys completed the course, many of them coming to America as soon as they had acquired enough English to speak it easily. With a new building and better facilities, it was hoped that the school could meet a greater need.

When the war broke upon Europe in August 1914, the Boys School building was ready for use. There it stood on its height, a large, airy, sturdy looking building, dominating the landscape, and inviting the youth of this much loved country to its doors. It also stood as a symbol of the good will and love that had grown through the years between the people of Ramallah and their many friends in New England. 

To carry on a school at such a time was impossible and the American workers were compelled to return to their homes. 

The lovely building had to be left to the fortunes of war. 

It served as a hospital for the armed forces, first of the Central Powers, Germany, Austria, and Turkey, then as the tides changed, of England. The dining-room on the first floor was used as a stable for the horses of the Turkish soldiers. 

VILLAGE WORK 

The majority of the people of Palestine live in villages. 

These compact little communities are the backbone of the agricultural life of the country as well as one of its most interesting social units. The village of Ramallah gives its name to a district of about sixty-six villages. The present population of this district is about 39,000 of whom 31,895 are Moslem. The rest are Christian. A 1936 census reports one 

Jew in the Ramallah district. Ramallah, itself, is the largest and most progressive village in the district and is Christian. 

Five of the villages are Christian, about five others are mixed and the rest are totally Moslem. When Friends first visited 

Palestine in the early days it was the needs of the people in the villages that moved them. Some work was being done, but neither then nor now has it been adequate. An account of the journey of Eli and Sybil Jones in Palestine and Syria reads like Into all the Villages and that village ministry was to influence the future plans of Friends as they undertook a more permanent work. New England Friends enlarged the

Palestine 153 day school program. They had three in Ramallah alone to meet the needs of the family or tribal divisions in the village. 

Hope School, founded by Marion, was continued for many years. When one sees Ramallah today, it hardly seems possible that as recently as 1912, children from one section could not go to school in another. There were also schools in ten other villages at various times. These little schools taught many things besides reading and writing and arithmetic. 

They were centers of light and love and healing. 

When the war closed the work of the mission towards the end of 1914, there were the meeting, the boarding-school, and the village schools which were now well established. 

Friends tried to keep the meeting going but the meetinghouse was being used as a canteen by the army when Edward Kelsey arrived as a Red Cross worker in 1918. Fortunately, he was able to get it restored to its former use. 

Developments in the mission at Ramallah have largely reflected the post-war situation throughout Palestine generally. Fortunately, A. Edward and Marion Kelsey, Alice

W. Jones and Rosa E. Lee were able to start work there a few months after the Armistice-the Kelseys at the Boys School, Alice W. Jones to take up her work again as principal of the Girls School, and Rosa E. Lee to the village work. Edward Kelsey also took care of the meeting. 

It was no easy task to begin again in a country which had been the scene of so much conflict and where children had not been able to attend schools for four years.

Alice W. Jones quietly gathered about her some of her former teachers. Miss Katie was on hand to continue her work as matron, and with her and others of long association with the school, Friends Girls School was very quickly functioning normally, and meeting the challenge of the new 

Palestine.

In the fall of 1919, the board sent Moses and Mabel Bailey of New England to have charge of the Boys School. They arrived early in 1920. Mabel Bailey and little daughter Margaret returned to the States that summer but Moses Bailey remained until the close of the school year in 1921. 

John Haramy, a Palestinian, and his wife, Ruth Shera Haramy, of Richmond, Indiana, both graduates of Earlham College, followed Moses and Mabel Bailey but they, too, remained only a year. Both families, even in that brief time made their contribution to the new school in a difficult period.

The third couple sent out in this brief period were A. Willard and Christina H. Jones. They remained in the school for five years and in the mission, helping with the schools, village work, and meeting three years longer. In the five years the teaching staff was enlarged, a science laboratory started, the library, particularly the Arabic section, grew slowly through the interest of students and alumni, and the curriculum was extended by 1926 to include a Palestine Matriculation Class. In addition, athletics became an increasingly wholesome activity for all the boys and each year the playing-field came in for its share of the improvement fund. Dr. Heiser, American Consul, presented a silver-cup for intra-mural competitions. Every corner of the building was put into use to house the growing student-body. 

In the fall of 1926, Khalil and Ermina Jones Totah joined the staff. The former was among the first students to enter the Boys School in 1901. He attended Oak Grove Seminary at Vassalboro, Maine, Clark University, and in 1926 finished work for a Ph. D. at Columbia University in New York. He returned to Palestine that summer and accepted the position of history teacher at the school.

In all modern missionary circles after World War I the

Palestine 155 idea of devolution, of turning the work over to nationals as soon as possible, was stressed. Accordingly, in the spring of 

1927, it seemed right to Willard and Christina Jones to resign from the Boys School in order to let Khalil and Ermina Jones Totah take over a work for which they were eminently fitted. They began their new work in the summer of the same year. Ermina Totah's health broke at the beginning of 1928 and she died in April leaving a very beautiful memory of her all too brief ministry.

Khalil Totah and Eva Rae Marshall of South Dakota, the latter a graduate of Penn College, were married in the spring of 1929 and together they have served the school to the present time. They have three children.

The school has grown rapidly between two wars and its facilities have been extended to meet the increasing demand for education in Palestine. That first building which was prayed for and worked for through so many years has been kept in splendid condition and two beautiful new buildings, architecturally suited to the country, have been added within the past fourteen years. One is Grant Hall, a dormitory and principal's home, and the other, an auditorium which not only serves the school but the whole community. A dwelling-house, called Wright Cottage, serves as a dormitory and recreation center.

From the fifteen boys that could be accepted in 1901, the enrollment has grown steadily until in October 1943 Khalil Totah reported one-hundred and seventeen day pupils. This work with its modest beginnings carried on in the spirit of the Master is a testimony to youth. The Christian Endeavor 

Society of New England Yearly Meeting caught the vision in 1900. Who can say how far or in what ways the youth of 

Palestine will serve their generation?

Khalil and Eva Rae Totah resigned from their duties at the

school in 1941. Because of the war, they have remained until 

1944 when they expect to come to the United States to educate their children. In 1942, A. Willard and Christina H.

Jones were again appointed to service in Palestine. Willard 

Jones assumed the principalship of the Boys School in the summer of 1944 after spending part of a year aiding in the educational work of Friends in Kenya Colony, East Africa. 

Postwar development at the Girls School has been as rapid as at the Boys School. They cannot accommodate all who apply. The school teaches children from kindergarten through high-school, accepting boys for the lowest classes. 

The kindergarten is one of the most charming features of Friends Girls School. Carried on in a beautiful building erected for the purpose, under a well-trained teacher, it is thoroughly modern.

A model cottage for domestic science work was erected in 1929. Khalil Totah solicited funds for this during the nine months he spent in America in 1928 and 1929 and the cottage was dedicated to the memory of Ermina Jones Totah who had endeared herself to the people of Palestine and who was deeply interested in its womanhood. 

The Old Students' Association of Friends Girls School has been very active. When the main building was remodelled in 1925, they gave one thousand dollars for the main entrance. They have also placed a gate at one of the entrances to the grounds in honor of Miss Katie Gabriel. The Alice

Jones scholarship is another of their projects. The school has been a happy home for hundreds of girls in the past fifty-five years. Although originally set on "the highest point of ground near the village," it now seems to nestle in its heart for Ramallah has grown out on all sides of it. Alice 

W. Jones resigned in 1929 and was ably succeeded by Mildred E. White of Straughn, Indiana, who served as principal for one term. Mildred White is still at work in Ramallah.

Palestine 157 Through the years she has taught at the Boys School as well as the Girls School, and engaged in village work, serving where she has been most needed at the time. 

Miss Victoria Hannush, who entered the school as a very little girl, is the principal now. She came to the United 

States for her advanced education and graduated from Earlham College in 1934. A pupil of Katie Gabriel and Alice W.

Jones, and long-time teacher, she embodies the spirit of Friends Girls School in a beautiful way. A woman of rare tact and innate gentleness of spirit, she is a worthy successor to the able leaders which the school has had through the years.

YEARLY MEETING OF PALESTINE AND SYRIA 

It will be remembered that English and American Friends worked closely together in the early days in Ramallah, Palestine, and Brummana, Syria. When the work was separated, however, each group worked independently and because of the difficulty of travel, they did not have much opportunity for fellowship. When good roads came to Palestine after the war and the automobile made the two stations just ten hours apart, Friends felt that they would all be helped by closer communion. As a result of this concern, the first general meeting of Friends in the Near East was held at Brummana, Syria, in the spring of 1926 and was attended by five Friends from Ramallah. The gathering brought such inspiration and fellowship to those who attended that it was decided to hold such meetings annually, alternately in Syria and Palestine. 

Soon, it seemed practicable to form a yearly meeting. This was accomplished in 1929 and is known as the Palestine and Syria Yearly Meeting. American Friends sent contributions of money and letters of encouragement when it was formed.

London and Dublin Yearly Meetings were represented by nine Friends.

This was the Easter season, a wonderful time to be in Palestine, and the large group of Friends who met in the meetinghouse, Palestinian and Syrian, English, Irish, and

American, felt a deep sense of oneness as they entered into a united testimony for Friends in the Near East. It is a small yearly meeting but it has a great responsibility in its unique setting in a part of the world where there are grave tensions in the religious and political life of the people. 

The meeting at Ramallah serves an increasingly large number of young people. Sunday school, Daily Vacation

Bible School, village relief work, contributions to other missions, all have a place in its activities. Because students make up such a large part of the meeting attendance, a more formal type of service has developed than was used at first. 

There is some feeling that participation in or responsibility for the meeting on the part of the local members has suffered because of this. The membership is very small, active and associate is around one-hundred in all. Yet, as students attend regularly during their school days, the meeting inevitably has great influence.

While the schools have been growing and expanding in every direction, attracting students from all over Palestine and Transjordania, and becoming practically self-supporting, the work in the villages has declined. Village work has always been supported by funds from America. When the 

Mission Board was forced to begin to retrench nearly twenty years ago, the little schools for girls that were being carried on were the first to suffer. Rosa Lee had eight schools during her last year at Ramallah, 1922-23, with nearly twohundred and fifty students. By 1931, there were only two village schools, one at Bireh and one at Deir Ghazanni with a total of fifty-seven pupils. The mission had to give up even

Palestine 159 these, eventually, but the Bireh school happily was continued by a local committee.

For many years the mission has supported a Bible woman, Naameh Shahla, a birthright member of the Society of Friends. The meeting has shared in her support also. She goes to the villages, Moslem and Christian, with her messages of Christian love and hope. She usually carries simple medicines as there are always sick ones who need the ministry of healing, also. Her presence in a village is eagerly anticipated as she is able to bring comfort to those women whose needs are great.

One very encouraging village project is the baby-clinic at Beit Ur. This clinic is housed in a good stone building set aside by the village for the purpose, equipped by the government, and the salary of the nurse is paid from funds collected by Dr. Elihu and Almy Grant for Syrian relief. The 1943 re ports show over one-hundred babies on the register; over three-hundred visits are made by mothers with their babies to the clinic each month, and the nurse makes over onehundred fifty visits to homes during the same period. It seems an especially beautiful work in a village not many miles from Bethlehem. This is only a modest beginning of the type of work that needs to be done. A memorial fund has been established in memory of Elihu Grant for this work in order that his love for the people of Palestine might have a continuing expression.

Friends have reason to feel some satisfaction in the splendid schools they have in Ramallah. In a country where educational facilities are most inadequate, where the majority of the Arab population is illiterate, where economic conditions are insecure, they meet a great need. Young men and women are prepared for college or university work in a Christian atmosphere. Parents are willing to pay the fees necessary to keep the schools going.

Friends in America have contributed good buildings and many workers, and splendid Palestinian and Syrian teachers have worked faithfully to make the schools vital. 

The decline of the village work program has been unfortunate. It is interesting to note, however, that the people of 

Palestine are aware of the needs of the villages as never before and are trying to do something about it by financial contributions. A woman's society in Ramallah is very active in a village work program. The clinic at Beit Ur is suggestive of the type of work that needs to be done. 

Friends in Palestine are a small group. The meeting is not very strong and is the phase of the mission program which needs the most attention. In a country where much ceremony and form is a part of religion, the simplicity of the Friends Meeting seems very strange. But Ramallah Friends have done remarkably well in the past. When the missionaries returned to America during the war and the meetinghouse was taken from them, they met in homes for worship, and held the group together. They tried at all times to take care of the interests of the mission. The yearly meeting association with Syrian Friends has been very stimulating and they have taken the responsibilities well. 

The Holy Land, holy to the three great monotheistic religions of the world, has even been in the center of world history. The year 1944 sees it again playing its historic role in the affairs of the nations. Forces of world-wide importance are at work growing out of religious and political factors in the little country. The vexed Arab-Jewish question which has kept Palestine in a state of unrest since 1917 is still to be settled.

The Palestine which Eli and Sybil Jones visited in 1869 is vastly changed. Then it was under a despotic and unenlightened government, and progress was at a standstill.

Palestine 161 Today the Near East is awake and a vigorous nationalistic spirit abounds. Education is demanded by an increasingly large group of people so that every school in the country is filled to capacity. Magazines and radios keep the people informed on the news of the world, and newspapers are widely read. In many of the villages the old type of windowless houses is giving way to modern well-lighted and well-ventilated homes. Sanitation and health are concerns of the leaders in villages as never before. Farmers are eager to know how to improve the agricultural conditions of the country, their live-stock, and orchards.

However, educational facilities as provided by the government are inadequate. Mission schools, British, American, French, Italian and others, still provide most of the higher education and much of the elementary education of the country and are much needed to meet the great demands of the present. Many problems have to be overcome before the entire health needs of the people can be met. 

The development of the vast oil-fields in Arabia together with all the aspects of modern civilization which influence people, may quickly change the complexion of the whole 

Near East.

Palestine is still a land of tension—a crucial spot in the heart of the world. The Society of Friends is uniquely placed in an area where the ministry of reconciliation is needed. 

Jesus found that need when he ministered to his people there nearly two thousand years ago. His belief in the power of love is the heritage of the modern Christian. As an era ends, Friends are challenged with a tremendous responsibility in an ancient and hallowed land.

CHAPTER X 

CUBA In the late summer of 1898, Zenas L. Martin and Captain 

L. D. Baker sat on the deck of a steamer that was on its way from Jamaica to Boston, quietly enjoying the coolness of the evening breeze after a hot day. The steamer had rounded the northeastern end of Cuba and was sailing fairly close to the island. As they sat, they talked of recent events in Cuba which were to make such a difference to the country. 

These two men understood and respected each other. 

They were not unlike. Captain L. D. Baker was president and manager of the United Fruit Company of Boston, a suc-` cessful, upright New Englander who took a personal interest in the welfare of the Jamaicans who were employed by his company. He is credited with the introduction of the banana to the United States, a very great service indeed which has provided a seemingly unending succession of banana salads, banana splits, and banana sundaes to delight the palate of America. Captain Baker was an old and trusted friend of the Friends in Jamaica. He had watched them at work through the years and admired and trusted them. He had helped them in 1889, when the Happy Grove property they so desired for a school was almost lost to them, because they had no money on hand when it was for sale. He bought the property for $2,100, secured the title to it, and turned it over to the mission. They repaid him in three years and Jamaican Friends have been grateful to him ever since. As he anticipated the future of his company in Cuba, he wished to continue his association with Friends. 

Zenas L. Martin, evangelistic superintendent of Iowa Year-

Cuba ly Meeting, had been a successful business man before he heard the call to religious service. His sound business judgment combined with religious zeal had made him very useful to his yearly meeting's mission in Jamaica and he went occasionally to study the needs of the field, advise the workers, and keep the yearly meeting informed and stimulated. It is said of him that once when a mission required some surveying, he took time off to study surveying in order to perform his task intelligently. Through the work in Jamaica, he had become acquainted with Captain Baker, and on the voyages between there and the States a fine friendship had developed. 

At this time Cuba was very much in the spotlight. The 

Spanish-American War was just over, and the United States government was planning to maintain a temporary occupancy of the island until Cuba had somewhat recovered from the unsettled conditions of the past forty years which culminated in her revolt from Spain. The Protestant Churches of the United States and Canada were awakening to the needs of Cuba, too, so long under the domination of an unenlightened Catholic Church. Some of the churches were already anticipating the establishment of missions. The time apparently was ripe for such a venture as the Catholic Church was identified in the minds of the people with the State from which they had sought release. The two men talked of the great needs of the people of this island so close to the United States geographically and economically. As the steamer sailed slowly along close to the shore line of Oriente Province, Captain Baker told his friend that his company was planning to plant acres and acres of bananas there. Then he said: "Why don't Friends start a mission in Cuba? There are some of the finest children you'll find anywhere on these plantations. Go home and tell Friends about them. I will

help all I can. My company is interested in the people who work for it. It will stand by you." 

Zenas L. Martin placed the concern before Iowa Yearly

Meeting when he reached home but the work in Jamaica was all Iowa could support. However, they tried to get some yearly meeting without mission field obligations to take over 

Cuba. There was no yearly meeting ready to assume so much responsibility but a number felt that they could help with it, if the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions would undertake it. That part of the story of Cuba has been told in the chapter on the American Friends Board of Missions, how in great faith it proceeded to reorganize and incorporate so that it could take on a piece of work on which 

Friends could unite.

There were many factors which made the appeal timely and propitious, the chief one being the Spanish-American War so recently undertaken and soon over, the United States staying on in the capacity of protector.

One evidence of the great interest in Cuba was the fact that there were seven applications to consider when the Board met in its first session on Cuba in Second Month, 1900. 

Very regretfully they had to refuse some as they had not the means to start a big mission. They sent Zenas L. Martin to 

Cuba to study the field and decide where work should begin. He was retained as general superintendent and identified himself so closely with the work that he and his wife 

Susie J. Martin spent the rest of their lives there.

Last of the candidates chosen were Emma Phillips, an Indiana Friend, then a missionary in Mexico, and Maria de los 

Santos Trevino, a Mexican girl with rare ability as a teacher.

Their knowledge of Spanish would help open the way more quickly for service. The other two were Sylvester Jones and 

May Mather, seniors in Penn College in 1899-1900. These

Cuba young people had planned to get married as soon as college was over and go to a mission field. The opportunity to go to 

Cuba came to them as direct leading of the Spirit. In his book, Not by Might, Sylvester Jones tells us that when he was a sophomore he had been moved by the stories of the physical suffering of the people of Cuba in their struggle for independence. When the United States declared war on 

Spain in April, 1898, he had been deeply stirred. The call for 

300,000 volunteers to help free Cuba came to him with tremendous appeal. He wanted to do something for Cuba, he was young and strong and deeply concerned but he could not volunteer for military service and keep faith with his conscience. Within a few months the war was over and Americans settled down comfortably again having felt the impact of the war only mildly. But Sylvester Jones still had Cuba on his heart and he was very ready and eager when the opportunity came to do something for the situation that had caused him such distress of mind. His college friends, members of the Penn College Missionary Society, knowing of his desire to go to the foreign field promised "to support Sylvester Jones in whatever foreign field he is called to serve for a period of five years." This was both a tribute from his friends and a comfort to the board. Sylvester Jones and 

May Mather were married in the summer of 1900 and signed the first contract entered into by the American Friends 

Board of Foreign Missions on October 31, 1900. They sailed for Cuba on November 3, 1900, accompanied by Emma Phillips and Maria de los Santos Trevino.

The contract which these Friends signed shows how much thought and prayer went into the new enterprise of the Mis sion Board. It is as follows: 

Contract of Sylvester and May Mather Jones of 

Oskaloosa, Iowa, with the American Friends Board of 

Foreign Missions.

These articles of agreement between Sylvester and May Mather Jones and the American Friends Board of 

Foreign Missions witness that; 

Sylvester Jones agrees to go D. V. to Gibara, Cuba,

Santiago Province in the employ of the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions and engage there, or in that vicinity, in mission work among the people. 

His duties are to be, first, to arrange for a temporary home for the missionaries while Zenas L. Martin, the board's agent and Superintendent, is unavoidably detained away from there; second, to begin and conduct, in connection with the missionaries of the board going there, such missionary work as there is opportunity for in the way of Bible, Church, Evangelistic, and Educational work instructing also both publicly and privately in morals and right living; third, to be diligent in the study of the Spanish language and as he is able, preach the Gospel, preaching always Christianity rather than denominationalism-Christ crucified, the Savior of men -yet not departing in preaching or practice from the established doctrines and views of Friends. 

He agrees to have a care to protect the property and interests every way of the A. F. B. and endeavor to carry out such plans and work as said Board may from time to time direct, and to be a counselor and helper when practicable in all lines of work in which the Board may see fit to engage, and send monthly reports to the secretary of the work and the conditions of the mission. 

The American Friends Board of Foreign Missions agree on their part to pay the traveling expenses of Sylvester Jones in going to Cuba and if he remain in their employment five continuous years, to pay his traveling expenses in returning home, if he should, after the length of time, desire to return home; but if for any cause he leaves the mission sooner than five years he shall bear his own expenses in coming away. The cost of providing a house for the home of the missionaries and a specified small amount of its furnishing, shall be an expense of the Board, and the Board agrees to pay

Cuba Sylvester Jones and wife, five hundred dollars a year and to pay it in monthly installments and this salary shall not be diminished by the Board for the months they are engaged in its service as above described. 

These articles further witness that May Mather Jones, wife of Sylvester Jones, agrees with said A. F. B. to go with her husband to Cuba and engage with him and the other missionaries in any and all of the missionary work to which her time and strength are adapted, having for her especial department, to preside over, order, and manage the mission home making it in simplicity, economy and neatness and in the influences that pervade it, a model for that people, of a Christian home. It is understood and is a part of this contract that Emma Phillips and Santos Trevino, and Juan Francisco Martinez are to have room in the mission home and board in the family of Sylvester and May Mather Jones, paying them for their board such reasonable price as shall upon trial be found to be equitable, but the care of their rooms and clothing and the furnishings of their rooms beyond what the board supplies shall be their own care and expense. 

The specification of this part of the contract: as to care to acquire the Spanish language, being counselor in the whole work of the mission, being careful of the property and interests of the employing board and reporting monthly of her work and the condition of the mission and also the agreement on the part of the board as to traveling expenses and salary are the same as in the foregoing part.

This contract is to take effect from November first, 1900. 

Signed in duplicate in the city of Richmond, Wayne 

County, on the 31st. of October 1900. 

Sylvester Jones 

May M. Jones

Signed behalf of the American Friends Board of For eign Missions 

Mahalah Jay, Sec'y.

In 1900, Cuba was in the first flush of freedom-at least her lands were free. The way ahead was not to be easy; for the next forty years there would be shackles to loose and struggle, first of all against the selfish interests of foreign financiers and business men, and then with her own leaders, but there was hope and aspiration and great optimism in the first year of the century. Cubans looked at their country and saw its assets and its liabilities. For the present they must accept the beneficent rule of their powerful neighbor to the north. While her intervention may not have been wholly altruistic, still, there were marked benefits already apparent. 

For the first time in years Cuba was at peace and with peace came a sense of security from foes without and within the island. The chronic state of insurrection which had prevailed for more than thirty years was over. Other benefits were the separation of church and state; the development of public utilities, the improvement in sanitation in the cities, the conquest of yellow-fever and preventable diseases, and last but not least, the setting up of a public school system. It would have taken the young republic years to achieve what the government of the United States had done almost in months. Most important of all, they had the assurance that the present government situation was temporary. 

What were the liabilities of the republic? First of all practically two-thirds of the population were illiterate. Out of a population of 1,572,797 in 1900, more than 1,000,000 could neither read nor write. The next sobering fact was the impoverishment of the country resulting from more than thirty years of continuous insurrection. Again, land tenure was uncertain and great estates were in the hands of a few. Cuba had been a Crown Colony of Spain and the land had been granted to nobles, public servants, grandees, or favorites.

These estates in turn were apportioned to henchmen under

Cuba grants which authorized the holders to graze their cattle on somewhat casually defined areas provided they used the land and constructed a home at the center for the benefit of travellers. This led to many difficulties when a new system of land ownership was attempted. Although the soil of Cuba was capable of growing a variety of crops, the wealthy class had practically limited them to sugar and tobacco because of their export value. Cuba had depended largely on her foreign trade but years of war had ruined it. But, if she had serious liabilities, she also had great assets.

Cuba, the largest island of the West Indies, lies very close to the United States. The island is over seven hundred miles long and about sixty wide with an area about the same as the State of Pennsylvania. Her great coast-line affords excellent harbors, a decided asset to a country which depends on exports. She has the best climate in the tropics, equable with adequate rainfall, and the heat is not oppressive, being tempered by the cool trade winds of the north Atlantic.

The soil of Cuba is good and capable of raising several crops on the same land in one year. Crops are grown easily. 

The variety of crops possible is indicated by the fact that more than 3,500 indigenous plants have been catalogued in Cuba. The Spaniards sought gold and were bitterly disappointed when they did not find it-Cuba's gold was her rich soil. Nowhere in the world can sugar be grown so easily or cheaply. This fact really conditions the economic and social life of Cuba.

When Columbus discovered Cuba he found Indians there. 

They were a gentle, kindly people who received the white men as gods. The conquistadors who followed Columbus baptized the Indians then made them work so hard in the mines to find gold for them, that they were practically ex-

terminated within fifty years. Negro slaves were brought in to work for the Spaniards who came in large numbers. 

These two groups form the largest part of the present population, although the early Spaniards came to call themselves Cubans. The difference between Spaniards and Cubans is that the former are Spaniards born in Spain and the latter are Spaniards born in Cuba. Negroes are about one-third of the population and there is a small group of mixed bloods. 

The native intelligence of the people is high. 

Cuba then, in her climate and soil, in her nearness to good markets, in her harbors and in her people had enviable assets. Another asset she had in 1900 which was fundamental was the will to develop into an independent state. J. Merle 

Davis in The Cuban Church in a Sugar Economy says: 

"There is no more thrilling example of the power of an unconquerable idea than the story of this rising of the masses of Cuba and the tenacity with which they carried on an apparently hopeless fight and dislodged a seemingly impregnable system of oppression."

That was the Cuba to which our four young missionaries went with a message of Christian love and a helping hand. 

The political and economic aspects of our American life were influencing the new Cuba; it was fitting that Friends join other religious groups in reaching out in the name of the Master to the souls of Cubans to help them achieve the abundant life.

The United Fruit Company of Boston had its land in Oriente Province in the northeastern part of the island. 

This was the busiest part of the country with the prospect of greatest development. On the advice of Captain L. D. Baker, who had given generously to the work, and Zenas L. Martin, Gibara, an important harbor city, was chosen as the location of the first mission.

Cuba It was fortunate that there were two Spanish-speaking people on the staff to begin with as it was possible to start work almost immediately. In less than two months after they arrived, January 3, 1901, Maria de los Santos opened a school with three children.

Our workers realized from the very first that the work must be religious and educational and the school was symbolic. Emma Phillips, with her gift for personal evangelism and knowledge of the language, began at once to meet people. Sylvester and May Jones set to work to learn Spanish and made rapid progress in it. When the Mission Board met in Seventh Month, 1901, they recorded that Zenas L. Martin had purchased a lot, built a home for the missionaries, and had a meetinghouse under way in Gibara. By the time the meetinghouse was finished, Sylvester and May Jones were able to begin their work. Study groups, Bible classes, Sunday school, and young peoples' societies were all soon organized and helped develop strong native leadership. 

Within two years, the congregation at Gibara asked for a monthly meeting. In the minutes of the board, almost exactly two years after the missionaries arrived in Cuba, the following minute was made: "The Executive Committee directed the opening of the Gibara Monthly Meeting according to the order of Friends, proceeding under the Uniform Discipline and the minute adopted by the board in 1901." 

The minute referred to was a statement embodying the thought of the board upon the subject of organizing churches in Cuba. It was a thoughtful guide for the meetings and indicated the desire of the board that the native churches should be self-supporting and undertake full meeting responsibilities from the beginning.

The work moved speedily. A second mission station was established in Holguin, one of the most important towns in the province about twenty-four miles inland from Gibara.

Charles and Orpha Haworth went there in July 1902. Maria 

Trevino went there in September of that year and opened a school, laying a foundation for the splendid institution which Friends have there today. 

The next year, Raymond and Minnie Cook Holding organized the work in Banes, February 1903. Within a month a modest chapel was dedicated in the presence of officials of the United Fruit Company, who had urged the opening of a mission there, and other influential citizens. A monthly meeting was formed on March 9, 1905, with twenty-seven members and thirteen associates. This small group pledged itself to systematic giving from the first.

In the meantime, work had been started in Puerto Padre which was about forty miles overland westward from 

Gibara, and had, as its name "Father of Ports" indicates, a fine harbor.

Wilmington Yearly Meeting became responsible for this work. In a letter to the American Friends Board of Foreign 

Missions their Foreign Mission Board informed it of their desire to start a distinctive work believing that the future welfare of the mission work of the yearly meeting was directly dependent upon having its own field. 

This must have been almost a threat to the unity which the Friends Mission Board had been working for but they met this crisis with common sense. They indicated that Wilmington Yearly Meeting might hold its property in its own name, but since the Americans Friends Board of Foreign Missions was incorporated and legally empowered to hold property in Cuba, it would be desirable for the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions to hold Wilmington property in trust for them. They further suggested that the salaries of all their missionaries be in harmony with the general plan already adopted by the American Friends Board of

Cuba Foreign Missions and under its general direction; that the general management be under the direction of the superintendent of the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions; that churches be established in harmony with its plan for organizing monthly meeting; and they reserved the right to approve its missionaries who were to be known as Wilmington Yearly Meeting missionaries of the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions, and direct reports were required from them. 

Emma Phillips and Juan Francisco Martinez, the latter of

Mexico, were married early in October, 1903. The mission had been desirous of starting a mission in Puerto Padre for some time so when the opportunity came, these Friends were recommended to Wilmington for the work. 

Eva Terrell of Wilmington was sent to aid the Martinez' in Puerto Padre and soon a thriving mission was under way. 

Juan Martinez and Eva Terrell opened a school and Emma Martinez did the meeting work. In addition to or in connection with these activities, a night class was begun for prominent business people for the study of English. 

North Carolina had opened a mission station in Havana under Ellen Woody of their yearly meeting in 1900. She was married to Arthur Pain in 1904, and they continued in the work together until his death in 1938. Martha J. Woody, sister of Ellen W. Pain, assisted them from 1909-1917. Ellen

Woody Pain and her sons carry on an independent work in that city at the present time.

These are the five chief centers of Friends work in Cuba but they are just centers. Extension work has always had an important place in the Cuban Missions.

The record of the first five years is good. There were stations in five principal centers, Gibara, Holguin, Banes, Puerto Padre, and Havana. All the missionaries that had been

sent were still there. They made fourteen in all including the superintendent and a native minister, Juan Francisco Galvez, "faithful, self-denying, laborious.” There were schools in Gibara, Banes, Holguin, and Puerto Padre, none very large but all with good foundations. The public school system itself was not far advanced. There were as yet no schools in small towns, villages, and rural sections. The problem of building up a school system amid so much illiteracy was necessarily slow. Even today, high schools are not common. Holguin, one of the most important towns in 

Oriente Province, did not have a public high school until 1938. The first Friends schools were oases in a desert of illiteracy.

The story of the next forty years is remarkable for the way in which Friends work has developed in Cuba, for the shift from foreign to native leadership. Cuba offers a rather unique study of modern missionary effort as it seems to have more nearly completed the cycle which missionary bodies agree is the goal of all missionary work. 

It would take long to tell the complete story of each mission station with its outreach through the forty-four years Friends have worked there. A picture of the field today will indicate the trends.

We have seen how quickly Cubans desired monthly meetings and assumed meeting responsibility. When the Mission Board began to face curtailment of their work, they felt that perhaps Cuba might have to be given up. The work had developed well and the board urged the meetings to take it over. But the nationals did not want to lose the benefits of helpful association with American Friends and expressed a desire for some Americans to remain with them. 

They were encouraged to form a yearly meeting and, after much prayer, aware of the big task they were assuming, they volunteered to be responsible for the entire support of

Cuba their own work. Zenas L. Martin reported at the time: "The churches with possibly one exception have rallied heroically to the new standard of self-support. It seems to some a staggering load they are attempting to carry, but notwithstanding the fact that it has been a year of financial depression, the native staff consisting of four pastors and twenty-two teachers have received good salaries from the schools." "The monthly meetings have contributed $3,268.52 to the support of the work, an average of $13.85 per member. This total is $1,087.45 more than last year. There was an increase of membership of 80% over the gain of the year before. An average of five hundred fifty-six persons have attended the regular meetings each week and four hundred sixty-one were in the Bible classes each Sabbath. Temperance meetings, cottage meetings and social occasions have had their place among us as well as charity and play." That seems a splendid record for twenty-five years of work which began so humbly. Accomplished against a background of the hostility of the Catholic Church it is doubly remarkable. Cuba seemed ready for its own yearly meeting and the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions sent the request to the Five Years Meeting to consider in its 1927 session. In the meantime the eight monthly meetings appointed a committee to prepare a discipline suited to the needs of Cuban Friends.

The Five Years Meeting in session in Richmond, Indiana, Tenth Month 18 to 24, 1927, approved the formation of a yearly meeting in Cuba and made the following recommendations: "We recommend that the Five Years Meeting proceed to establish a Yearly Meeting of Friends in Cuba to be organized and maintained under their own Discipline. The arrangements for the establishment of the meeting should be left to the Executive Committee of the Five Years Meeting. We suggest that there shall be a friendly and helpful tie

of relationship maintained between the new Yearly Meeting and the Five Years Meeting through the Mission Board." 

The following spring, Cuba Yearly Meeting was officially organized, all but Havana being part of it. Another interesting development in Cuba is the young Friends Conference which has been held annually almost without interruption since 1927. It follows the usual pattern of conferences with classes, lectures, preaching services, and wholesome recreation. Between two and three hundred young people attend. For some years now, Friends from the United States have gone down to take part in the program, usually speaking on some aspects of Quakerism, thus maintaining the bonds of friendship between Cuban and American Friends.

This conference is held in Gibara, our first mission center. 

Cuban Friends publish a monthly paper called El Amigo 

Cubano (The Cuban Friend). The editor at present is Rosa 

Blanca O. de Morell.

They also have their own home mission program and the women's societies are active in charitable work and sponsor health, peace, and temperance education. They seem to have an amazing amount of energy. The Mission Board Report for 1940-1941 says, "The eight Monthly Meetings and thirteen other organized congregations are served by three pastors and one itinerating missionary. Arsenio Catalá, pastor at Gibara, cares for two additional monthly meetings at Auras and Bocas and for two country congregations out from Gibara. Add to this his responsibilities last year as 

Presiding Clerk of the Yearly Meeting and his share in general community activities, and it will be obvious that he is carrying the work of several men. Juan Guzman cares for the monthly meeting at Banes and three out-stations as well. 

Jose Diaz is the pastor at Velasco, which conducts two home mission centers. Henry D. Cox holds preaching services at 

Puerto Padre two Sunday nights a month, visits Velasco on

Cuba [X] the preceding Saturday evenings, directs the Thursday night services at Holguin and on Wednesday night at Vista Alegre, and when free is in charge of the Sunday evening services at Holguin."

Cuba Yearly Meeting has had many problems to face in meeting its obligations. They have shown resourcefulness to a marked degree. The following passage taken from The 

Cuban Church in a Sugar Economy by J. Merle Davis, shows how Banes met a difficult situation and is a good portent for the future of Friends in Cuba: "A successful co-operative church enterprise and, so far as we could learn, unique in 

Cuban Evangelical circles is the bakery of the Friends Meeting at Banes, Oriente Province. About five years ago, the depression threw out of work a number of the members of the meeting. Mr. Miguel Angel Tamayo, the president of the meeting and a leading druggist of the town, explored with his executive committee the possibilities of setting up some business enterprise for relieving the distress within their membership. When one of the two chief bakers closed his business, the Friends Committee leased the property and took over the bakery. A member of the meeting who had been employed by the retired baker was put in charge of the project. This church bakery has prospered from the start. 

It bakes and distributes to the community eight hundred loaves of bread a day. It pays $100 a month rent for the premises and from its profits is able to pay good wages to eight Friends families whose former employment had disappeared. It is building up a capital fund with which to purchase the bakery premises. This church bakery is also paying the salary of a mission worker for a circuit of rural chapels. The business ability of Mr. Tamayo and his associates is a large factor in the success of the project. "The Friends Meeting at Banes is notable in being one of

the four self-supporting Evangelical churches in Cuba. 

Three hundred members, and fully as many others, according to the pastor, are living as genuine Christians and participating in the work of the church and giving to it. The pastor explained: "The church has two budgets; one for itself and one for missions, both of which are contributed locally. 

We stopped thinking about help from outside many years ago, and from that moment we went forward with the new energy that was released.' 999 Friends schools are maintained in four centers, Holguin, Puerto Padre, Banes, and Gibara, and two in rural areas, with a total approximate enrollment of five hundred and fifty pupils, and forty teachers. All the teachers are Cubans. 

They are respected for their high standards of scholarship and Christian education. Much of the leadership of Cuba 

Yearly Meeting has come from the schools, which keeps the intellectual as well as the spiritual tone of the yearly meeting very high. Each school in its different area has different emphasis.

When Friends began work in Cuba, Gibara was chosen for the location of the mission because of its excellent situation on the coast. However, with the coming of the railway and later, the central highway, transportation within the island was simplified and Holguin became the commercial center of the northeastern part of the island. Because of the facilities it offers and its central location to Friends work, the yearly meeting has usually met there during recent years. 

The Holguin school, or Colegio "Los Amigos" as it is called, has become an important central elementary and secondary coeducational school. While the board encourages Friends to establish secondary schools where it is desirable and they can be supported by the patrons, it has not seemed wise for the board to try to equip and support more than one in Cuba.

Cuba Colegio "Los Amigos" began as a small school under the care of Maria Trevino in 1902, coincident with the birth of the Republic of Cuba. It did not begin to make much progress, however, until ten years later when Clarence G. and 

Bertrell McClean were sent to Holguin in 1912. 

Under the principalship of Clarence McClean, the school made steady growth in enrollment and academic efficiency. 

Classes were held in rented buildings until 1916 when a site was purchased and two rooms erected. That same year the school was officially recognized by the government as part of the educational system of the country. The next year, the secondary school program was initiated. With a continued increase in enrollment it was necessary to add several new rooms. They were built in 1919. 

The year 1921 was significant in the history of Colegio "Los 

Amigos" for that year the first student finished the high school course. It was also the peak year in enrollment for a number of years to come as a devastating depression that swept over the country made it impossible for many parents to pay for their children's education. Clarence McClean resigned to become pastor of the meeting and Merle L. Davis was chosen as his successor. 

Under the leadership of the new principal, progress continued. A five year program of improvement in the instruction of the school was adopted by the mission. Higher academic standards enabled a larger percentage of the students to pass the official government examinations each year. 

By 1927 the enrollment had increased and the building was enlarged to its present size. Emphasis was placed on the continuity of the staff as a means to greater efficiency. 

With the establishment of the yearly meeting in 1927, it quite naturally followed that nationals should be appointed to positions held by missionaries. This was also in line with

modern missionary ideals. Ramon Morell was appointed director of the school in 1929 with Roy C. Votaw to assist him with counsel and aid for a few years. 

The Colegio "Los Amigos" has greatly influenced the growth of the Society of Friends in Cuba. In addition to the leadership it has given to the church and its influence on the character of its membership, it has sent out a large number of graduates who have a sympathetic attitude toward the Friendly approach to the social, economic, and religious problems of society. From the point of view of enrollment, it is the largest school under the direction of the board. 

In a tribute to the schools founded by pioneer missionaries of all denominations in Cuba, J. Merle Davis says: "The pioneer missionaries were men and women of unusual personal equipment, including statesmanship, deep religious fervor, and high scholastic standards. Witness to this statement is the wide occupation of the cities and towns of the island with the many strong city churches and the unusual number of high grade academic institutions. These have made a remarkable record in the training of Cuban leaders and in the respect and affection in which they are held by the public. 

Cubans are the first to describe such schools as La Progresiva at Cardenas, Candler College in Havana, Colegios Inter nacionales at Cristo, and the Friends College at Holguin as the premier institutions of their kind in the country."* 

Banes school, which is in the center of an agricultural area, emphasizes vocational training. It also offers a course to prepare teachers and workers for the home mission centers. 

Friends of Banes are building a new school house there to replace the very inadequate one they now use. It is on a plot of ground leased from the United Fruit Company for ninety-nine years. When completed, at a cost of more than 

*The Cuban Church in a Sugar Economy. J. Merle Davis

Cuba $30,000, it will serve a large number of the children of Banes as efficiently as any school in the country.

The school at Puerto Padre gives a preparatory course for the government high school or technical schools at Havana. 

This school ministers chiefly to the privileged class of Cuba. 

It has carried on continuously since it was opened in 1903 by Emma and Juan Francisco Martinez, and Emma de Martinez still gives it her devoted supervision. It has an enrollment of around ninety pupils and is noted for the excellent quality of its work.

These three are the outstanding Friends Schools. All are needed and giving excellent service. These schools and the yearly meeting represent a splendid work extending over forty-four years.

When the first Friends missionaries went to Cuba, they went to a people who had just achieved their independence at great cost. They were eager to develop their own institutions but accepted the help of the United States in the emergency. American intervention, unfortunately, was followed by the control of Cuba's economy getting into the hands of international bankers and business men. Later, for a period of years, Cuba was under the dictatorship of a ruthless leader who brought a reign of terror to the island. The program of public schools did not advance as rapidly as they had hoped and dreamed-as late as 1935 about half of the people were still illiterate. The present world situation has been another set back. On the other hand, the population of 

Cuba has grown to almost 4,000,000. The May 1944 election in which the president's candidate was defeated is an indication that at long last democracy may have come to the island. A literacy campaign under the direction of Dr. Frank

Laubach promises to help speed up the educational processes.

The missionaries from the beginning encouraged the Cuban people to be independent and the rapidity with which they took over control of the church is a witness to their success. As Cuban Friends assumed more and more responsibility, the missionaries gradually withdrew. Of the original twelve sent out in the first five years, Emma Martinez alone remains, as a missionary and loved friend. For some years, she and Henry and Alma W. Cox were our only representatives. The Coxes retired from the field in 1942 after thirty years of devoted service and with their retirement an era ended.

Cuban Friends have expressed a wish to maintain a close relationship with American Friends and to have some continuous contacts. In 1942, the Mission Board, therefore, appointed Hiram and Janet Hilty as their representatives in

Cuba. They have no authority over Cuba Yearly Meeting. 

They were appointed, rather, to give whatever aid they can in the development of an understanding and appreciation of the Christian way of life as interpreted by Friends. As liaison persons they are a link in the chain of friendship and love that was forged when two men sat on a cargo steamer one night as it sailed on the Caribbean; perhaps it was started when Iowa Friends became concerned about Jamaica. 

Many have given consecrated service to Cuba through the years; each name and each service, though not recorded here, is written in the hearts of Cubans. It would take long to write it all down adequately but those who served had their joy and reward in service-and the names of Cubans lead all the rest.

CHAPTER XI AFRICA 

Africa is an ancient land but it has ever been a land of mystery and magic, a land alluring but forbidding, drawing the Asiatic and the European as with a magnet yet making him pay a great price for everything it has given him. For it is a land of desire and death. Within it, deep in its heart are the gold and diamonds men covet; great stores of copper are held in its mines; roaming its forests and jungles in ancient times and the present, ivory bearing animals, beasts to challenge the hunter and the trader alike, have played their part on its stage. A great deal of the wealth of the world is hidden there not to be taken easily.

Not only have its gold and its diamonds and other minerals, and its ivory been desired, but the people of Africa have been stolen to toil for others in Arabia, in the Caribbean area, and in the United States of America. The shame of slavery can be shared by Arabs and Spaniards, English and Americans, and almost all people who have lived on the Mediterranean. Africans have been forced to slave in the mines and the forests of their own country in order that Africa's wealth might enrich the people of other lands. 

Yet in its great forests and on its hot coastal plains, the white man has had to fight his battles against strong animals and tiny insects, both deadly. Fevers and hunger and thirst and loneliness have been the lot of countless adventurers and explorers.

Geographically, it seems to hang like a pendant from the great continent of Asia. The infinitesimal link that held it has been made a canal which links the East with the West.

It is such a big continent itself that it could easily encompass several European nations.

The rim of Africa has long been known and used but the interior has only comparatively recently been explored. 

When Robert Moffat met young Livingstone in London in 1840, he told him of "a vast plain to the north where I have seen the smoke of a thousand villages where no missionary has ever gone." People were content to stay on the edge rather than face the great unknown of the heart of Africa.

We know it today as a country of high mountains and vast plains, of jungles and forests, of deserts and rivers, of beautiful lakes and giant waterfalls, of lovely singing birds and fragrant blossoms, of fruit and grain, and of great mineral resources. It is indeed a land of infinite variety. The throbbing of the drums in the long African night, weird ceremonies in the mystery of the moonlight, and the rhythmic step of the dancers at the feasts in the setting of Africa, have all had a strange fascination for the white man.

A study of the names of its various areas gives one a quick introduction to fairly recent history and shows how the nations of Europe have helped themselves greedily to bits of 

Africa. We find French West and French Equatorial Africa,
