Italian East Africa, Belgian Congo, Portuguese East Africa and Angola and Mozambique, the Anglo-Egyptian Sudan, North and South Rhodesia, British East Africa, South Africa with cities named Pietermaritzburg and Durham, Springbokfonteino and Worcester to remind us of the Boers and the English. These names and others are pregnant with the meaning of Africa, with its past history and its present problems. Last, but not least in the list is Blantyre, Nyasaland, named for the humble Scottish village that gave Africa one of its greatest friends, Livingstone, one of many who have gone to Africa to give rather than to exploit.

But it is with the people of Africa that our study is con-

Africa 185 cerned, and one cannot possibly describe them in a brief paragraph. Among them there is also variety. There may, however, be a general background picture we can form as we study the Friends Mission in Kenya Colony. 

Africa had Christian missions for almost one hundred years before Friends started their work, yet incredibly, Kenya was almost untouched in 1902. The Church Missionary 

Society of London sent seventy-nine missionaries to Liberia between 1812 and 1830. Forty-four of them died during the first year. It seemed almost impossible at that time for 

Europeans to live on that coast. The American Presbyterian and Methodist Churches sent out large numbers of missionaries with the same results. There is a beautiful story told of Melville B. Cox, first American Methodist missionary to 

Africa who died within four months after landing there. As he lay dying he pleaded, "Though a thousand fall, let not

Africa be given up." Many, many did fall in those years before modern medicine and modern science taught the missionaries how to combat malaria and other diseases. But the call of Africa was insistent. A knowledge of the horrors of slavery was a challenge to sincere Christians and like soldiers of the Cross they faced dangers beyond description to tell the story of Jesus who died to make men free. 

Although slave trade was abolished in the British Empire in 1808 and slaves emancipated twenty-five years later, it did not end the slave trade in Africa. Livingstone seeing it wrote, "All I can add in my loneliness is, may Heaven's rich blessing come down on every one, American, English, or 

Turk, who will help to heal the open sore of the world." And it was like an open sore on the body of the world, which festered and poisoned wherever it appeared. Traffic in human life was a cruel business built on the misery of human souls. 

The first missionaries sometimes met with hostility of a vio-

lent nature as they taught a message of love in the midst of so much cruelty.

Eli and Sybil Jones were the first American Friends to go to Africa. Hearing of mission work in the Republic of 

Liberia they felt called to visit it. This they did in 1851. 

They were gone six months on this mission feeling a special tenderness toward this new republic which had been set up for the Negroes. Perhaps the encouragement they gave to the missionaries who were there was no small part of their mission. It was fifty years later that Friends awoke to the needs of Africa and established a mission there. 

The Friends Africa Industrial Mission grew out of the concern of a body of young men. Willis R. Hotchkiss, a minister in the Society of Friends presented the opportunity of work in Africa to Friends in America. He had spent four years in 

Africa as a missionary and he returned to the States with a burning desire to see something done for the almost untouched Kavirondo tribe in Kenya Colony. This was in 

1899, just at the time the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions was getting ready to start work in Cuba. The board could not then consider two fields. 

Willis Hotchkiss was a graduate of Cleveland Bible Institute, then under the management of Walter and Emma Malone. He spoke to the student body and aroused much interest in what he hoped would be an industrial mission in East Africa.

It happened that Arthur B. Chilson of Iowa, then a student at Cleveland Bible Institute, had for some time been under a conviction that he was called to serve in Africa and had been preparing himself for that work. He readily united with Willis Hotchkiss in his concern and with William M.

Smith they formed a temporary organization to secure funds for a mission. Arthur Chilson was well qualified to take

Africa 187 charge of a program of industrial training. His father was in the hardware, implement, and tinning business in Iowa. 

From him Arthur received valuable training in tinning, plumbing, and general mechanical work. To that training he added a high dedication to the winning of souls for Christ. 

He and Willis Hotchkiss started out at once to present the plan for an industrial mission in Africa to all American Yearly Meetings. Seldom has any concern met with such enthusiastic support and quick response. The appeal of the two young men in their earnestness and consecration touched the hearts of Friends. It came at the time the Christian church in America was preparing for the great ecumenical conference of 1900 with its missionary vision, stimulating and inspiring the layman as well as the cleric to get into the work of the Kingdom. It was a good moment in time to arouse interest in darkest Africa. As a result of their visits, Iowa, Western, Indiana, Kansas, New England, Canada, and New York Yearly Meetings each appointed two delegates to assist in the setting up of the board of the Friends Africa Industrial Mission. There was such a sense of urgency about setting up the mission, and waiting for yearly meetings to act seemed so long, that Willis Hotchkiss and his two companions proposed the formation of a temporary committee to act at once. This committee served so well that when the board met in Cleveland, Ohio, on February 2, 1901, they presented the names of twenty persons who wished to go as missionaries and reported that they had on hand $2,436.67 and pledges for $2,300.

The board realized its great responsibilities. There was 

Africa with its great need, there were twenty gifted young men and women who wanted to minister to that need, and they had nearly five thousand dollars in cash and pledges to use. In deepest humility, they prayed for Divine guidance in carrying out their trust. They stated their aim in the lan-

guage of the period and placed it on the records: "The primary aim of the F. A. I. M. is the evangelization of the heathen." If there were some who expressed doubt about the value of an industrial mission and wondered what that had to do with winning souls for Christ, they must know that: "The industrial feature is introduced into the work for the purpose of exerting a continuous Christian influence over the natives employed with the hope of obtaining the following results, viz: teaching them habits of industry and ultimately establishing a self-supporting native Christian Church." There was real vision in the realization that the emphasis must be on the development of independence in the natives. When one realizes the state of the Africans in 

Kenya Colony at that time such vision seems "visionary," but events have shown that they were fully justified in their hopes. Within another year, on February 18, 1902, the Board of the F. A. I. M. was duly incorporated under the laws of the 

State of Ohio, where it remained until it came under the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions in 1911. 

It seemed wise to the committee to send out a prospecting party to Africa before deciding how many missionaries to send or what plans should be made. The choice of a location was most important, particularly one where Americans could live with a reasonable minimum of health hazards. 

Three were chosen to go as prospectors. They were Willis 

R. Hotchkiss, a minister who had already spent four years in Africa and to whose vision and enthusiasm the organization owed its progress so far; Arthur B. Chilson, minister and industrial expert; and Edgar T. Hole of Salem, Ohio, a business man, long interested in missions, and a consecrated layman. The latter was appointed business manager of the 

F. A. I. M. which office he combined with evangelistic work. 

They sailed on April 23, 1902, with the prayers of hosts of Friends to speed them on their great mission. Willis Hotch-

Africa 189 kiss and Edgar Hole each left a young wife and baby daughter which added poignancy and sacrifice to the leave-taking. 

The three men spent a few weeks in England on the way. 

English Friends were much interested in the prospect of a mission in Africa and gave the Americans a warm welcome. 

They also gave them the promise of an iron house which could be shipped from England and set up wherever they settled. This house arrived in due time and in grateful remembrance of much kindness was called Devonshire House. 

The party arrived in Africa in June but it was August before they had selected the site for the mission. They decided to locate, if possible, on the highlands near Kisumu and Lake Victoria because of the healthfulness of that area, but more because it was the center of a populous district of needy people. The climb from the seaport of Mombasa to the hill country was slow and tedious. Government officials treated them kindly and facilitated travel for them to the best of their ability but they had to depend almost entirely on native guidance which was not always dependable. 

That did not necessarily indicate duplicity on the part of the Africans. These white men were strange and could not make themselves understood and were not even sure of their exact destination.

Eventually, they reached a place 5,500 feet high in the lovely Kavirondo district where they felt they would surely find a site for permanent work. But the eight weeks trek had been hard. They travelled about two hundred miles through jungle country where tall grass and giant trees covered the land before they even began prospecting. Paths were so narrow that they had to go in single file, and as they followed them, they were often following the tracks of wild animals.

About the time they almost despaired of finding a suitable

camping place, Willis Hotchkiss and Edgar Hole succumbed to malaria and were too ill to go on. Arthur Chilson realized that he must find a place as quickly as possible and tramped about alone many miles to find it, with no success. 

Then he did a very wise thing. He climbed a tree. A long time before in Jericho, Zaccheus, fearful lest he should not see the Master when he was there, climbed a tree. Meeting 

Jesus changed his whole life. Jesus seeing him said, "This day is salvation come to this house."

Arthur Chilson from his tree saw a river and on the other side of it a grassy slope, a perfect place to pitch their tents. 

Hastening back to the sick friends he told them the good news and, with the help of their porters, the party moved across the river to possess the land. 

It was an unoccupied tract with an abundance of heavy timber on it, with a broad stream of water flowing through it, ending in a good falls and rapids. And there was an abundance of good drinking water available. The day was Sunday, August 10, 1902, a blessed Sabbath to the weary travellers, these three men from the heart of the United States who had ventured into the vast unknown to win the souls of the despoiled people of Africa “in the love of the Gospel." 

As they looked about they knew that God had led them well. This great country was exceedingly beautiful but almost untouched by modern civilization. Later they described it to their friends in America as follows: "After spending months here we find that God has given us a choice land, pleasant, healthful, and possessing a beautiful outlook. 

Standing with our backs to the rising sun, with a high hill and a dense forest back of us, we can see Mount Elgon towering thousands of feet above the surface of the plain, sixty miles to the right, and lovely sloping hills and valleys to the front and left. At the southeast corner, extending over almost a fourth of the estate is a timber belt, containing lumber of

Africa 191 good quality. At the southwest part of the land is the Lugoli 

River with a fall of twenty feet. The soil is generally fertile though lacking in lime. There are several good springs which furnish an abundance of water for household purposes." Truly a place of "God's own choosing" as one of the party described it. They also found building stone for their homes and schools and churches, and red, white, and yellow clays which mixed with water made a fair grade of paint. 

They purchased more than eight-hundred acres of this excellent land for the F. A. I. M.

Although Kaimosi is practically on the equator, the climate is mild and healthful because of the altitude. The sun of noon may be hot but nights are cool so that fires may be necessary. The temperature varies from forty-eight to ninety-eight degrees.

The district in which the mission was established was well populated. There were as many as five hundred to a thousand people within a radius of one-half mile. They were described as rather a simple people with a keen sense of humor and capable of learning to perform all sorts of manual labor. They were well-fed, robust, and the average stature was just slightly below that of Europeans. The dress of the men consisted chiefly of a sort of apron made of cow, goat, or sheep skin, tanned or untanned, suspended by a cord made of fiber from the stems of banana leaves. Women used only a few strands of banana fiber tied about their loins, and children, nothing at all.

The home of a Kavirondo was a low, mud hut, with a thatched roof. In one section the women ground the meal in the morning and the cattle stood there at night; another space might be for the chickens and goats; and a third served as the family bedroom. Because polygamy was common and each wife was entitled to her own hut, villages consisted of little clusters of thatched huts and varied in size from one

to hundreds of households. The Kavirondo did not limit themselves to definite villages too closely but moved about rather freely in tribal units in their large territory. They had, however, clearly defined and established moves. 

When the British government took over Kenya Colony, a state of anarchy existed in the district. They had to settle constant fights between the Kavirondo and Nandi tribes. 

Although a state of quiet existed in 1902, the Nandi tribe was still hostile. This government supervision and control was almost the only contact these people had with white men. 

The religion of the Kavirondos was animistic. To them the material world was moved by unseen forces. They looked on their world and saw everything as nature doing things with definite purpose; nature was personified completely. And because there were so many unseen forces to bring pain and insecurity their religion was one of fear rather than love. They tried to understand and appease the spirits which surrounded them and in so doing developed countless superstitions which bound them. 

And there were many things to fear in Africa! There were the strong, wild beasts that roamed the jungles taking their cattle and attacking and killing men; there were deadly snakes hidden in the long grass that struck out at them; there were tiny flying things that pierced their flesh and made the blood hot; and there were the unseen things that possessed their minds and left their bodies weak. Adult life was at best difficult and dangerous and demon-filled. 

Some very remarkable stories of great beauty have come out of Africa which reveal Africans as people capable of great kindness and understanding. No one who has read a life of Livingstone will ever forget the sensitiveness that caused his loyal companions, black men all, to remove his heart when he died and bury it in the Africa he loved, then

Africa 193 carry his body thirteen hundred miles to the coast to send it back to his loved ones.

The Kavirondo chiefs welcomed the white men and asked to be taught the white men's ways. They realized how necessary it was to meet the demands of the civilization which was encompassing Africa. The three young men set to work at once to prepare a permanent settlement. 

They had left nearly three tons of goods at Kisumu which had to be brought to Kaimosi by porters along narrow winding paths, through dense undergrowth of jungle, part of which was through the territory of the Nyangori tribe, a very fierce and warlike people. It was a memorable journey, testing the endurance of Arthur B. Chilson, upon whom the burden fell as the others were not yet strong enough to undertake it.

The weeks and months that followed were filled with the hardest kind of labor. The land had to be cleared, paths cut through the jungle, bridges built, gardens planted, oxen broken to the yoke, temporary buildings erected, and wheelbarrows made. In addition, they had to learn how to communicate with the natives who swarmed about them. The 

Africans came at first wondering and suspicious, but as they watched the strangers toiling with their hands, and as they felt the healing ministry of these same hands on their own broken bodes, they accepted them and asked to be taught. 

In a short time Willis Hotchkiss was able to conduct religious services and start a school which met under a big tree. Where words failed, no doubt their actions were an open book which filled the natives with wonder and they showed kindness to the missionaries. 

The second missionary party arrived from America in July, 1903. In it were Dr. Elisha and Virginia Hole Blackburn, Adelaide Hole and eighteen months old Leona Hole. The

addition of a doctor to the staff was a great blessing. He had patients, within an hour after he arrived. What it meant to the three lonely men to have the two women and the baby at the mission can be well imagined. They gave a sense of permanency and completeness to the little mission and were a happy portent of the future development of the work. Two homes were set up bringing companionship and comfort which was needed. What it meant to the two courageous women to leave the security of their homes in America and face life in such a new country is but another evidence of the power of Jesus to win great devotion to His cause. 

Emory and Deborah Rees were the next additions to the staff. They had worked in Africa for four years before uniting with the F. A. I. M. Emory Rees was from Ridge 

Farm, and Deborah Gorman Rees was from Ogden, Illinois.

When Emory Rees had a concern to go to Africa in 1899, his own quarterly meeting of Vermilion Grove, Illinois, made it possible for him to go. His work had been with the Zulus employed in the gold mines in Johannesburg. This was a valuable experience to bring to the new mission. Both he and Deborah Rees were good linguists and upon them was laid the responsibility of reducing the native dialect to a written language.

Within two years the pattern of the mission work was outlined. Willis Hotchkiss did not remain long but the rest settled down to earnest effort. The industrial, medical, educational, and evangelistic departments were headed by capable men, all of whom held the ministry of the gospel of Jesus their supreme task. In the pattern of training, we see a fourfold vision for the mind, body, spirit, and hands of the Africans. How the work developed in each line and how well and wisely the foundations were laid can be seen through a brief study of each department.

Africa 195 

INDUSTRIAL WORK 

The name given to the mission indicates the work the committee planned to have emphasized. In the hands of Arthur 

Chilson it was to have a quick growth. It seems to have been hard for many of the contributors at home to see the connection between making bricks or cutting lumber for a home and "evangelizing the heathen" but those on the field never doubted the value of the industrial training. 

Tools were practically unknown to the Kavirondo in 1902 -they had not even learned to use the wheel. In a brief moment, as time goes, they learned to use the tools the rest of mankind had struggled through long, hard eons of time to invent and master. Working side by side with the missionaries, the mechanic, the doctor, the scholar, the minister, they put up buildings, widened paths through the jungles, unconsciously cutting away the fears that lurked there as they did so, built strong bridges that eased their paths, and harnessed water to serve them. The 1905 report says: "The natives have been trained as they have assisted in the erection of houses, cultivation of four or five acres of land, construction of a dam and a sluice way, the setting up of a sawmill, the building of a brickkiln with a capacity of eight thousand bricks, and the making of about thirty miles of roadway. About twenty-five houses have been built besides the setting up of the iron house the English Friends gave us, now named Devonshire House."

They turned their attention to agriculture, also, introducing new fruits, grain, and vegetables. Maize, millet, and beans were the main crops of the district. Sweet potatoes and yams were also grown. The missionaries, practical men that they were, experimented with wheat, sugar-cane, coffee, cotton, and the castor-oil bean, and planted large numbers of orange, lemon, apple, quince, and other trees. Frank

J. and Blanche Conover of Iowa spent several years in Africa, assisting with the agricultural program. 

Kaimosi is the center of the industrial program. A large sawmill provides lumber for all the needs of the mission, and government orders for large supplies of lumber are met; brick making is still carried on extensively; the boys engage in carpentry; manual training is emphasized in the school; and a good deal of grain is ground at the mill. There is also a tailor shop on the place. The mission hopes to install electricity and a water-system in the hospital and the schools as soon as possible. They already have the large turbine to which the motor can be attached. The printing-press which has been a great blessing to the mission is still an important factor in the work.

The Industrial Department of the mission is a financial asset, also. In 1942 Jefferson Ford wrote to the board at home that the department had provided food to the institutions more reasonably and conveniently than it could be secured elsewhere; provided lumber for buildings and schools and given oversight to building projects; had paid all the transportation and salaries of the industrial men and their families and all the expenses of the department; and had contributed $1,000 annually to the mission work to date. In addition, they have valuable equipment, and forests from which to secure excellent lumber. 

Fred Hoyt, who went to Africa in 1912, has long been in charge of the industrial work. Under his supervision, splendid buildings for the schools, homes for the teachers, and meetinghouses have been, and continue to be, built. An 

English friend writing of Fred Hoyt says: "It is not given to many men to leave their mark on a district as he has done. 

Men who combine missionary zeal with mastery of many crafts fit exactly into the post-war picture." The same could have been said of Arthur B. Chilson.

Africa 197 

MEDICAL WORK 

Ten weeks after the location of the mission was decided upon, Edgar Hole sent in the first report. He wrote of clearing eight acres of ground, planting a garden, putting up six temporary buildings among other things, then adds "Willis 

Hotchkiss also leads our daily morning gospel service in

Swahili which is held with all hands on the station before beginning the day's work and attends to most of the dispensary work. He is sometimes assisted in this by Arthur 

Chilson... Some days we have quite a few patients... 

They need an earthly physician as well as a heavenly one." 

Small wonder, then, that they urged the board at home to send a doctor and that in less than a year one was on the field. Dr. Elisha Blackburn wrote soon after he reached 

Kaimosi: "Not less than six persons whom Arthur Chilson had been treating very creditably were awaiting our arrival on June 5, to have their various wounds and afflictions cared for. We feel the need of a suitable building and equipment for cases that need to remain for a longer or shorter time." 

According to Dr. Bond who is in Africa now: "The ignorance and superstition of the people, together with the nature of the country, were responsible for much disease, suffering, and disability. The country was malarious and was infested with rats which at times carried plague-ridden fleas. The many streams were often polluted with the organisms causing dysentery and other diseases. An adult native was seldom seen without small-pox scars. Wounds and snake-bites, bad colds, pneumonia abounded."

In the early days of medical work in Africa, the doctor had to do his share of building, and road-making. This must have seemed a great waste of years of costly training and skill when there was so much suffering on all sides that could be lessened, but it seemed necessary then. The new

doctor wisely took time to win the confidence of the people before he attempted to do much medical work. The witch doctor was still the most important person in the tribe. He was the one who knew about the spirits or demons that surrounded them, which ones brought certain ills, and which ones must be appeased. He knew how to do it. If his treatment brought more pain than relief, as it did in many cases, then the patient himself or some person in the tribe could always be held responsible. However, he was the best they had. He was the known; the foreign doctor with his strange ways was at best another mystery.

There were those, however, who accepted the help of the doctor and as the benefits of his treatment became known, others sought it also. A dispensary was opened in a mud and thatched hut but later on the end of the doctor's brick kitchen was walled off for the purpose. 

Work on a hospital was begun in 1911. This was interrupted by the war in 1914, so that the building was not completed until Dr. Bond and his family arrived in 1917. In the meantime, a medical center was established in Lirhanda in 

1912, by Dr. Andrew and Lilah Estock. The Estocks re mained one term only, but during the time two doctors were on the field an extensive work was possible. 

Patients began to fill the hospital before it was equipped to receive them giving Dr. Bond a speedy initiation into African lore and African confidence. One of his big problems was to find and train native assistants. That took time and patience but was accomplished. When Margaret Parker joined the staff in 1925 a regular course in nursing and firstaid was started for attendants.

Women were slow to come to the hospital for treatment and it must have been a great day in the work when the first of them did come. When that first woman came, she opened

Africa 199 a door to countless others to whom the hospital has been a haven of peace and comfort ever since. Maternity work was introduced later, encouraged by the local native council. 

Ruth Kellum, a registered nurse, arrived on the field with her husband, Everett Kellum, in 1929. Her willingness to 'help with the hospital work made it possible for Dr. Bond, who was already doing extensive work in the district, to spend more time visiting various dispensaries. During the years 1925 to 1929, Dr. Bond travelled an average of 10,000 miles a year in addition to performing heavy duties at the hospital. 

Dr. Bryan and Edith Michener went to Africa in 1930 when 

Dr. Bond came home on furlough. Dr. A. A. and Mira Bond returned to Africa in 1931 and from then until 1938 there were again two doctors serving the field. The Micheners retired from the mission that year. 

The hospital continues to serve although the burden is a heavy one. There is an ever-increasing demand for its services throughout the district. Statistics for the year 1942-1943 show how important the hospital work is: "A brief reference to our statistical record for the year shows that 24,805 outpatients (all races) were treated, 2,027 in-patients were cared for, 185 operations were performed, and 154 maternity cases were conducted."

EDUCATION 

One of the most distinctive features of all Friends mission stations is the educational program. The first missionaries to Africa realized almost from the beginning that if Africa were won for Christ it must be done by Africans. They wrote, "We are therefore striving to help solve the problem of supplying native workers, by not only leading them to salvation, but also teaching and training them." Their first schools were started to train native evangelists. When they

started these schools, however, they had a very real problem to face, the question of language. Swahili, which was the common language used in Kenya for commerce and trade, and in which the Bible and other writings were printed, was not known by most of the tribes about Kaimosi. It became increasingly clear to the missionaries that if they were going to reach the people about them, they would have to study the vernacular. The problem of mastering any of the dialects and putting them into writing seemed formidable and the task monumental. They felt, though, that God had given them a mission to these people and would help them if they put their faith in Him. A growing knowledge of the vernacular convinced them that the task, though difficult, was not impossible.

The entire staff helped with this study but it was upon the heart and mind of Emory Rees that the concern was laid and he, with his wife's help, dedicated himself to the task of "mastering the art of speech in an unwritten language with its strange and unusual idiom." 

It would take long to tell the story of more than twenty years of patient, critical study on the part of Emory and Deborah Rees and their African helpers, a story of hard work, painstaking study, discouragement, and singular success. The Africans "were questioned to the point of distraction in the efforts of the white man to follow the devious ways of native thought and expression." Some of the helpers were: Abonya, first convert of the mission, who contributed folk-lore, native traditions and legends, and was tireless in his efforts to help; Amagune, who contributed most of the translation of the Scriptures and "bore the burden and heat of the day in the first attempt to put them into Maragoli"; and Joeli Litu, without whom the work would not have been done as accurately or as well as it was. With him Em-

Africa 201 ory Rees went over the whole vocabulary of several thousand words, and every word and phrase in the New Testament.

At the end of the first year of language study, they made a translation of "Come to Jesus." This was followed by a 

First Reader which grew into a graded series of three booklets that "exerted an influence out of all proportion to its size." The Book of Mark was printed in Marion, Indiana, on the press of W. H. Wright. He also printed all the hymns then in use. This was possible because English characters could be used for all the sounds in Luragoli. By 1925, the 

New Testament was printed. Five thousand copies were sold in less than a week. It must have been a wonderful experience to the native Christians to have a book in their own language about the new religion of love that was transforming their lives.

A printing-press was added to the mission equipment in 1913. Africans were taught to be printers in a very short time and "no other bit of equipment has yielded so important an influence upon the progress of the mission's enterprise. It cost $1,200 to install it and in a short time it more than paid for itself."

Fascinating as the story of the language is, we must go on to the schools. These were set up in the different stations, Kaimosi, Maragoli, and Lirhanda. When the F. A. I. M. 

came under the American Friends Board in 1911, there were ten schools with almost five hundred pupils enrolled. Five years later there were thirty-two day schools. The annual budget for the Africa mission nearly doubled in that same period. It was $8,554 in 1912 and $16,609 in 1917. Schools grew like the traditional mushroom and it was a constant effort to furnish teachers and supervisors for them all. By 

1922, twenty years after the mission was started, there were

seventy-seven day schools with a total enrollment of fivethousand pupils.

A paragraph from the report of the Mission Board in 1922, describes the beginning of a new step in the educational program. A special school for the advanced training of ministers, teachers, and other church workers had long been a recognized need on the field but it was always something for the future. Plans had been discussed many times and estimates made "but it was always something to be done at some future time when conditions were more favorable. Then one day when everything was unfavorable a beginning was made and the school came into being. There was no principal, no building, no curriculum, no very definite plans, no boys, and little money, but in the face of all this shortage of what was necessary to a successful school it was decided to start it forthwith. And the school has done a year of successful work.. all of which indicates that the one essential thing for the opening of a school was decision. Twenty-five entered the boarding department for the first half semester and twenty-four completed it. During the second semester the number enrolled rose to thirty-five and closed with thirty-two." There were at the same time two boarding schools for girls, one at Kaimosi and one at Lirhanda. 

Latest figures from Africa report that in 1943 there were 

381 day schools with 24,520 pupils and two boarding schools with 265 pupils. School fees from the pupils amounted to 37,- 

797 shillings. The day schools are scattered over a wide territory which makes great demands on Everett Kellum who is in charge of the educational program. Three hundred and sixty-one of these schools do work equivalent to first grade in the United States and are entirely self-supporting. The remaining twenty schools which do higher work receive financial aid from the government. They follow the syllabus of minimum standards set by the government and receive

Africa 203 a certain amount of government supervision. The government inspector says of the work of the Friends schools: "The carpentry work is maintained at a very high standard considering the small amount of time available. In the days to come the all-round craftsman who can build as well as do the carpentry required for a house will be urgently needed. 

This school should provide a Post-Primary Course which would aim as high in its own way as the academic secondary course. Combined with instruction in farming and business management it would be a most valuable contribution to 

African development. "I do not wish to lay undue stress on technical education; 

I only want to emphasize how sturdy and self-reliant a community yours is, how firm a place it holds in the regard of those who are concerned for African development and what an opportunity is offered. . . The whole tradition of the 

F. A. M. qualifies it for a leading role in post-war development. The inspiration of Cornell is so obviously more appropriate than London."

In years past Roxie Reeve and Roxie Stalker have assisted with the school program. In 1943 Dorothy Pitman was sent out to take over the Girls Boarding School at Kaimosi. Helen K. Ford and Ruth Kellum have done yeoman service. The burden of the program at the present time is being carried by Everett Kellum but it is clear that no one person should be asked to carry so much work. There seems to be no limit to the opportunities before Friends in Africa for this work and the record of forty-two years is truly remarkable.

EVANGELISTIC PROGRAM 

In any record of work that has engaged the lives of men and women in devotion to a great cause, one realizes how hard it is, or almost impossible, to depict the story behind

the statistics, the story that has in it the hopes and disappointments, the successes and failures, the hard work, the loneliness, the love and joy in service, and the rewards in friendship and confidence that are part of the missionary's life. This is true in any phase of the work and particularly true of the church work. Every one who has gone to the 

Africa field for any special service has held the preaching of the gospel to be his chief task. All activities have been directed to that end.

The original three, toiling in the African sun making highways and bridges and houses, the doctor bringing physical comfort, the translator giving a song and a book, the teacher in the school were united in a common purpose. 

Because of this, the church in Africa has grown very fast. 

The first convert was Abonya who has served Arthur Chilson as "boy" and interpreted the gospel services for seven months. That was more than a year after the mission was founded. He was associated with the mission for many years, giving very special help to Emory Rees in his first language work. Although it was long before people publicly became Christians, all the services were attended by literally hundreds. As they came to understand the message and as it was put into their own language, however, response came more quickly. Mission stations were established at Lirhanda in 1905, at Maragoli in 1906, at Kitosh in 1913, and in Malava in 1929. These were centers from which the missionaries itinerated throughout the large district. 

Within twenty years there were six monthly meetings, sixteen other organized congregations, nearly five hundred members, almost one thousand adherents, and eight thousand who attended services weekly. A large staff of native people helped in this program which was so large. In each station there was a missionary family to train and advise and work

Africa 205 with them. In 1928, they reported a quarterly meeting with a total active and associate membership of 3,899 people. The church work was entirely self-supporting as every member brought his tithe to the meeting. They were building their own churches, conducting Sunday schools and Christian Endeavor Societies, and organizing gospel teams to go out to all the villages.

The missionaries have always felt that a probation period was essential before a confessing Christian should be accepted into membership, and they have encouraged individual conversion rather than community or tribal acceptance of Christianity.

The evangelistic program is under the care of Jefferson Ford. He and Helen Farr Ford joined the mission in 1914 after both had served in Jamaica for some years. He has been associated with the mission ever since. There are now eleven monthly meetings with an active and associate membership of 13,474 people. The average total weekly attendance is 54,128. A third quarterly meeting was organized in 

1943 with 1,100 in attendance at the first session. The meetings continue to be self-supporting and willing to assume many responsibilities. One meeting, for example, sent 1,200 pounds of corn to the Girls School when there was a shortage of food due to famine conditions, and another recently sent fifty-one shillings to the American Friends Service Committee to relieve the sufferings of Jewish children. 

Jefferson and Helen Kersey Ford try to visit all the meetings in the district and serve them as counsellors and friends. 

It is a very big work they are called to do and much more than any evangelistic superintendent should attempt. 

African Friends are now asking to form a yearly meeting. 

They feel that they have shown themselves ready for it. This will be brought to the Five Years Meeting in 1945. The

mysticism and poetry and religious sensitiveness of these Friends will enrich and inspire the Society of Friends in countless ways. In the post-war world, Friends should welcome that spiritual association with a people coming into the family of nations.

Before closing an account of the work in Africa, some tribute should be paid to the wives who have been such able and loyal workers. From the beginning, when Adelaide

Hole opened the first little school they have all taught and preached and used their homes for the advancement of the kingdom. The hardships of pioneer life were theirs as much as their husbands'. Edna Chilson worked tirelessly in her days at the mission; Edith Michener, young wife and mother, was responsible for the Girls School; Helen K. Ford in the past two years has sacrificed her home life to keep the Girls School open until help arrived from America; Alta Hoyt has been an evangelist of great power; Ruth Kellum has helped at the school and the hospital; and Mira C. Bond has been the able "manager" at the hospital doing countless tasks that have helped the doctor and nurses render more efficient service. One cannot separate Deborah Rees' name from that of Emory in the splendid language work that they did. 

Then there was the committee in America that worked so hard to secure funds and recruits for the fields. They published a magazine, The Africa Record, a little paper full of news and pictures of the Africa Mission. The annual reports indicate a great amount of work. The names of Walter and 

Emma B. Malone of Cleveland, Ohio, and William C. Taber of New York, for many years treasurer of the board, perhaps lead all the rest. They thought in big terms and accomplished big things. But there were men and women and children in all the yearly meetings who gave and prayed for Africa.

The Friends Africa Mission in 1944 is under-staffed at the

Africa 207 moment of its greatest opportunity. The place of Africa in the new world is bound to be significant and important. 

Christian missionaries have had a big share in helping Africans get ready for the civilization which was inevitably coming upon them. Africans have suffered slavery at the hands of white men, yet they have been "set free" by those who have gone with the gospel and the love of God in their hearts. 

If Africa comes into its own peacefully and intelligently, the world will owe an incalculable debt to the Church. Friends have been privileged to be called to Africa and should be humbly grateful to all who have been faithful. 

Perhaps the following extract from *African Friends

Dedicate a Church, written by A. Willard Jones in the spring of 1944, just forty-two years after the arrival of the first little party of Friends in Africa will show how the plan of making the natives independent and of "teaching them habits of industry and ultimately establishing a self-supporting native Christian Church" has been realized. The church was built at Chango, Vihiga, and dedicated March 19, 1944, after thirteen years of planning and cooperative effort. "The grounds around the new brick building had been beautifully laid out with well-kept lawns and flower beds. Some stately old trees had been left from the primitive forest, just enough to give shade and dignity but not enough to conceal the impressive vista toward Lake Victoria and the escarpment. . . 

Joeli Litu spoke briefly on the faith that had led them to the construction of the new building. He recalled that he had seen Emory Rees build the first brick house in their district long ago and how they felt sure they could never build a house equal to it. Even when Mr. Rees assured them they could, many did not believe him. Yet some did and as a result, a house was built. . . Then the treasurer read the financial statement. It showed all debts paid and contribu- *American Friend, May 4, 1944, p. 196—.

tions from various sources of nearly five thousand shillings and much voluntary labor. . . It seemed remarkable to me that this very adequate building was planned, built, and financed entirely by African Friends without the supervision of a single European. . . In this demonstration of the faith of a community, one sees more than a new meetinghouse-one sees the answer to a missionary's prayer and the vindication of the missionary enterprise."

CHAPTER XII YEARLY MEETING MISSION BOARDS 

Philadelphia, Ohio, Kansas, Oregon, California, China

When the first union report of the Woman's Foreign Missionary Societies of Friends was published in 1887, the following statement was included: "To those unacquainted with the peculiar constitution of the Society of Friends, it will be a matter of surprise that we should have nine W. F. 

M. societies rather than one general organization with nine co-ordinate branches. But this corresponds essentially to the type after which our church has been formed and governed. Its yearly meetings are virtually independent bodies, having no central authority or common source of legislative appeal. They are united under one general name and cemented together by bonds of comity and fraternal correspondence." 

Friends have maintained this loose type of organization, depending on their spiritual ties and common testimonies to bind them into what to others often seems an exclusive Society. It is exceedingly bewildering to people of other denominations to find so many "kinds" of Friends within such a small denominational group. Missions in the Society of 

Friends reflect the variety of religious experience and practice that is found among them.

When the question of one Board of Missions for Friends was presented in 1887, one of the chief fears expressed then was that such a union might infringe on the authority of the separate yearly meeting boards. Even within the Five Years

Meeting organization, which recognizes the A.F.B.M. as its official body, some yearly meetings have their own projects. 

Today, eight yearly meetings are the main contributors to

the A.F.B.M. Two others, Canada and California, make small contributions. Five yearly meetings maintain their own missions under separate boards. These are: the Board of Missions of California Yearly Meeting, the Mission Board of Kansas Yearly Meeting, the Foreign Mission Board of Ohio Yearly Meeting, the Board of Foreign Missions of Ore gon Yearly Meeting of Friends Church, and the Mission 

Board of Philadelphia Yearly Meeting of Friends. 

Full and interesting accounts of the work of these boards are available from the secretaries. However, it seems suitable to include a brief summary of their work in this account of Friends missions so that we may have a more complete picture of the wider out-reach of Quakerism and see how it is being interpreted in four continents, Asia, Africa, North 

America, and South America.

MISSION BOARD OF PHILADELPHIA YEARLY MEETING The Woman's Foreign Missionary Association of Friends of Philadelphia was formed Twelfth Month 12, 1882. This was the second of the women's organizations for foreign work in the Society of Friends and was the result of the "quickening" that was influencing Quakers generally through the great evangelical movement. For a number of years, individuals had been giving liberally to the work of 

Samuel A. Purdie in Mexico and Theophilus Waldemeier in

Syria, and in some degree to the work of English Friends in India. They had also been contributing to the missions among American Indians. But many began to feel that more effective work could be done if the women met for study and prayer and more systematic giving. Stories of the condition of women in what they called heathen lands stirred them deeply and they felt that Christian women must share the light of the gospel and the benefits of Christianity with them.

Their meetings were well attended and their programs interesting. There is a delightful account of one specially good meeting in the Missionary Advocate, Sixth Month, 1886. "Many no doubt were attracted by hearing that Pandita Ramabai from India was to be present. . . Three eastern nations were represented at our meeting. A young lady, Dr. 

King of China who is a student of medicine in Philadelphia, and Inaza Ota from Japan." Inaza Ota said, "The presence of three natives of three oriental empires shows that the 'Light of Asia' has lost its hold among these peoples. If 

Japanese women have a stronger claim than their Asiatic sisters to your sympathies, it is not so much because of their wrongs as of the critical importance of the times when the change in ideas and customs favors in every way their elevation to a higher social plane." Philadelphia Quakers had already sent their first missionaries to Japan. 

The first work of the organization was to send Julia L. Ballinger to Mexico to take over the girls school which Gulielma Purdie had started. They also sent money to Samuel Purdie for the publishing house in Matamoros. There was a girls school in the village of Ras el Metn in Syria which they made possible and which was called the Philadelphia School. 

They sent money, also, for the tuition of girls in other schools, for a Bible woman for Beit Mary, and the new hospital at Brummana, Syria. 

But it was Japan that was to engage their interest and eventually become the responsibility of the whole yearly meeting. They sent Joseph and Sarah Cosand of Kansas there in 1885 to initiate the work. 

When Commodore Perry sailed into Yokohama harbor in 1853 with a letter from the President of the United States to the Shogun, he little knew that a new era in Eastern Asia was thus begun. The President requested the protection of 

American sailors who might be shipwrecked on the coasts

of Japan, and expressed the hope that Japan would open some ports where American vessels could obtain supplies. 

Commodore Perry delivered the letter in July, 1853, and returned for his answer in February 1854. The Shogun had considered the letter carefully and being favorable to the ideas he signed a treaty in March, 1854, granting the privileges of two ports to American vessels. This was a great concession as Japan for a long time had desired no intercourse with the West. Later on, commercial treaties were signed with the United States and other countries. This precipitated civil war in Japan, resulting in the restoration of the emperor to real power, and the removal of the capital from 

Kyoto to Tokyo in 1868.

A two-hundred year isolationist policy thus ended, a policy of absolute and fanatical exclusion of Western influences. 

The door of Japan was now opened wide to the West. Its science, education, government, philosophy, and customs were studied widely. Young people were sent abroad in large numbers and Westerners were welcomed to Japan. 

The teaching of Christianity was sanctioned by treaty, taking its place along with Shintoism, Confucianism, and Buddhism. Missionaries from many denominations went in great numbers. Friends entered Japan during this period of good-will and general exhilaration and began work at once. 

The Philadelphia committee was encouraged in its desire to send missionaries to Japan by two young Japanese students who had come to America to study in our large uni versities. In the Friends Missionary Advocate for Ninth 

Month, 1885, the following report was made: "The Japan question assumed such grave importance that we called a special meeting in Sixth Month, 20th, to which were invited two intelligent young Japanese who were known to some of our members. These young men were very helpful to us by giving their views of the most practical work to be done in

their country. . . They urged our sending someone now and especially desired that it might be one who would be so consecrated to the work that he would live among the people humbly as one of them, would make it his adopted country, and as one of them expressed it, would be willing to 'bury his bones' there."

The two intelligent young men, Inazo Nitobe and Kanzo 

Uchimura, were to be strong links in the chain of friendship forged between American Quakers and Japanese that day.

Because the people of Japan were eager for schools, the Cosands soon bought a piece of land in the southern part of Tokyo overlooking the bay and commanding a fine view of Mount Fuji, built a school on it in 1887, and a meetinghouse in 1890. Canada Yearly Meeting sent William V. and Isabel 

Wright in 1888. These Friends laid the foundation of the work that was to continue for fifty-five years in which the emphasis would be on religion, education, peace, and rural welfare.

Friends Girls School 

Friends Girls School in Tokyo is one of the leading schools for girls in Japan. The buildings are beautiful, modern, and commodious. From the very beginning it has been under 

Japanese leadership with one or more American teachers on the staff. In more than fifty years there have been only three principals, the present one, Toki Tomiyana, having served in that capacity since 1926 and been associated with the school since 1898. The other two were Chuzo Kaifu, who was principal from 1887 to 1912, and Seiju Hirakawa, who served from 1912 to 1926. The latter resigned to become 

General Secretary of the yearly meeting.

Nearly five hundred girls are enrolled for a five-year course of study which prepares for the higher schools or colleges. The course includes the regular high school subjects

with special emphasis on English, Bible, Japanese, Oriental 

History, and Domestic-Science. 

In a history of Friends Girls School published in 1937 to mark the fiftieth anniversary of the founding of the school, the following statement is made: "The Self-Government Association is perhaps the biggest single contribution Friends Girls School is making to educational work in Japan and received much favorable comment from the Fact Finding Commission." A concluding paragraph from the same report gives the foundation upon which this program is based: "Like Friends schools in other countries, Friends Girls School is known and respected for its emphasis on character building. The school is dedicated to the finest type of 

Japanese womanhood as conceived in terms of Christian character and each Christmas at the annual consecration service no one can doubt the sincerity of the girls who choose to celebrate the birth-day of Christ, their Master and inspiration, by identifying themselves with the Christian Movement."

The Yearly Meeting 

Japan Yearly Meeting lasted only a quarter of a century, but its life though short was very full, and although the official organization has ceased to exist, one cannot know how much of its spirit will live on. 

The yearly meeting had its beginning in Tokyo in a small group that met in Sheba Meetinghouse about 1890. Young men met there for the study of English and western ideas, and girls for English, sewing, and knitting. A school for training Christian workers grew out of it. 

All went well until 1894 when the first Sino-Japanese war broke out. A wave of extreme nationalism swept over the land at that time and the Sheba Friends group was carried along with it. Soon it appeared that the young Japanese

were using their organization at the meetinghouse to support the war effort. Quakerism had not yet taken root in the soil of Japan and the Quaker peace testimony was not understood. Consequently, the meeting dissolved.

The comparatively easy victory which the Japanese had over the Russians gave them confidence in their powers, and this first international crisis resulted in a tempering of their enthusiasm or need for western methods. This was reflected to some extent in their attitude toward missions. The big day of easy access to the Japanese was clearly over and Quakers and other missionaries in Japan now realized that the long, slow process of education was necessary if the essentials of their message were to be understood or accepted. 

There were now two centers in Japan, one in Tokyo and one in Mito in Ibaraki prefecture twenty-five miles from Tokyo. These were important centers in Japanese life, the first being in the heart of the commercial life of Japan, and the other in a great agricultural area.

George Braithwaite, an English Friend, had paved the way for the work in Mito, and the Tokyo committee, respecting the scholarly traditions of the place sent Manji Kato, an intellectual, as one of the most important workers. He was a man who worked for peace all his life. Gurney and Elizabeth 

Binford, Edith Sharpless, and Herbert and Madeline Nicholson are the Americans most closely linked with the work there. They and their Japanese co-workers developed a well-rounded program of community as well as meeting activities. They built a brick meetinghouse in 1912, and were recognized as a monthly meeting in 1917, the year the yearly meeting was set up. Mito became a center around which other monthly meetings were established. Friends helped organize New Life societies with much the type of program which some rural life associations in America have adopted. 

The average farm in Japan is only three acres which puts a

tremendous strain on farmers to support their families from them. The young men in the New Life societies tried to introduce better methods of farming, increasing use of domestic animals, markets, and cooperatives. 

By 1900 four monthly meetings had been set up in Japan. 

From then until 1917 good foundations were laid through the meetings of the executive committee, business meetings, summer conferences, Bible study courses and the weekly meetings for worship. Gilbert and Minnie Bowles, Horace 

E. and Elizabeth Coleman, and Thomas E. and Esther B.

Jones all have been closely associated with this development. 

Gilbert Bowles' name is almost synonymous with the peace societies, for he gave his life to them for forty years. 

Thirty years after Friends started their mission in Japan, the mission committee urged the setting up of a yearly meeting as a symbol of independence from foreign authority. To the comparatively small number of Friends in Japan this was a great venture but they accepted the challenge. Seiju 

Hirakawa was elected clerk of the yearly meeting in 1925 and general secretary in 1927. He gave able leadership to 

Friends as long as he served. 

A further step in the growth of Quakerism in Japan came when it seemed essential in the face of common misconceptions of Christianity as interpreted by Friends for them to make a Statement of Faith. A very simple but beautiful statement was prepared by a committee composed almost entirely of Japanese.

War brought about a crisis in Friends work in 1894 and it again produced one more far-reaching in consequence in 1941. The third Sino-Japanese war began in 1937 and soon drew the whole Pacific area into the debacle. Nationalistic feeling became intensified and both westerners and native Christians were looked down upon. The situation forced

most missionaries to leave Japan by 1940, and with the beginning of hostilities against the United States in December, 1941, all American missionaries had to cease their activities. 

In the meantime the Religious Organization Bill became a law. This Bill recognized Christianity as one of the religions of Japan but required a union of all the Christian denominations into one body, severance of all foreign aid, and government supervision. The idea of denominational union was not unwelcome to many Christians as it had been under discussion for twelve or thirteen years, but it was exceedingly hard for some of the churches to agree to certain forms. 

To Friends it meant accepting the ecclesiastical program of ordained ministers, creeds, observance of sacraments and all the ceremonies that accompanied them.

If the union had been accomplished in time of peace, consideration of denominational differences might have been possible, but the mood of the times was not favorable for that and Friends were too small a body to have much weight. 

After much discussion, Japanese Friends placed the following minute on their records: "Consenting to the condition that Friends will observe the sacraments. the committee decided that Japan Yearly Meeting of Friends will join the 

United Church of Christ in Japan."

The situation in regard to Quakerism in Japan is somewhat analogous to that in Mexico, only that there has been no union in Mexico such as has been accomplished in Japan. 

In each case American Friends have labored for a number of years and then been compelled to withdraw. In each case, the question is asked about the value of all the sacrificial living and giving that have gone into the work. Fortunately,

Christian mission work also includes loving, and love is still the great eternal force in the world. The Christian must always remember that he is God's messenger and that he cannot always see the end. His message is primarily concerned

with the things of the spirit which are eternal and the measure of success lies not so much in outward things but in the evidences of spiritual values. Each denomination will take to the union in Japan some outstanding gift and Friends in America can expect that the small group of Friends will take rich gifts of the spirit, of the things that are eternal. 

We should turn for a moment to see what has happened through the years to the W.F.M.A. which started the work in 

Japan. 

It drew the men of the yearly meeting into its organization in 1899 and changed its name to the Foreign Missionary 

Association of Friends of Philadelphia. In 1923, the yearly meeting recognized this association as its official missionary body and a new name seemed to be called for. It is now the 

Mission Board of Philadelphia Yearly Meeting of Friends. 

Walter Haviland became the first president of the new organization and Sara M. Longstreth, recording secretary. 

There has always been a close association between the committee and the work in Japan. Many have visited there, large sums have been sent for buildings, many missionaries have been supported through the years, and a number of leading Japanese Friends have been brought to America for conferences and study.

The work of the board in 1944 is limited to the Japanese- Americans who have been placed in re-location centers in the United States, and to Japanese residents of Hawaii. Gilbert and Minnie Bowles are in the latter place continuing their long association with Japanese, serving those to whom the war has brought much distress.

The work of Friends in Japan, like that in Mexico, is left in the care of nationals. In Japan, the organization of a Society of Friends no longer exists. Fifty-five years of loving service in Jesus' name is Philadelphia Yearly Meeting's gift

to Japan and in all the essentials it has been a blessing to all who have been any part of it. 

FOREIGN MISSION BOARD OF OHIO YEARLY MEETING 

The Foreign Mission Board of Ohio Yearly Meeting had its origin in the Woman's Foreign Missionary Society which was organized on September 4, 1884. This original organization was the sixth of the woman's societies formed among Friends between 1881 and 1888. As in other societies, it was formed under deep concern for the evangelization of women and girls of non-Christian lands. 

Missionary interest, however, was already well established among Ohio Friends. They had contributed to Friends work in Syria, Mexico, and Jamaica. Through a union missionary auxiliary organized by Elizabeth Jenkins in Mount Pleasant, Ohio, monthly meetings had been helping with various missionary projects in Japan, China, and India. 

These contacts with the mission work of other denominations had given Ohio women good experience and they felt that the time was right for them to form their own board in 1884. 

While they were trying to select a field of work for their united effort, two opportunities came to them, each bearing the Divine stamp so plainly that none could doubt God's leading. The first field was China, the second, India. China

One morning in the summer of 1885, Esther Tuttle Pritchard, corresponding secretary of the Ohio Board and editor of the Friends Missionary Advocate, received two letters. One was from Esther H. Butler, offering herself for missionary service. She was a school teacher, she wrote, but she desired to nurse and teach the sick. The other letter was from 

Dr. Robert E. Beede, a young doctor in a Methodist Mission

in Nanking, China, asking if perhaps there might be some young lady among Friends who would be willing to come to Nanking as a nurse and helper in the hospital. He told of the great need for one consecrated and wise who would be willing to sit by the beds of the patients and read and talk to them. One recalls the story of the beginning of the work in 

Mexico with its similar chain of events, an organization, a dedicated life, and a need.

Esther Butler began at once to take some nurse's training, and by December, 1887, she was actually in China in the home of Dr. and Mrs. Beede. While she helped at the hospital, she studied the language and customs of the Chinese people. 

Within eighteen months, she felt ready to begin mission work in Nanking for Ohio Yearly Meeting, having gone out with the understanding that she would proceed in this matter as the way opened.

Esther Butler was an excellent person for this pioneer work as she was a young woman of rare ability, initiative, and judgment, which she combined with utter devotion to the cause of Christ. It took faith and courage for this lone woman to venture out in a strange land, but she never wavered. She was to give thirty-four years of her life to China as superintendent of the mission, and to keep the confidence of the board and her fellow-workers through hard and trying times until the very end of her life. 

The land to which Esther Butler came was an ancient land, and Christian missions in China were also old. From the days in the seventh century when the Nestorians went there from Syria, it had persistently challenged brave souls of many faiths. Roman Catholic missions were started there six centuries after the Nestorians and continued with much success for one hundred years. They were driven out but the

Jesuits tried again in the sixteenth century with more success for there are many Catholic missions in China today. 

Protestant missions in China are fairly recent. A small group led by Robert Morrison of the London Missionary Society were there between 1807 and 1842 but it was a very modest work. However, with the opening of five Chinese ports in 1842, under pressure from Britain, a new era began in that great empire. There were some nationals who like the Japanese in 1854, felt that China should adopt western methods as quickly as possible, but there were more who wished to continue their age-old customs. Missionaries from all denominations flocked to China in the years following but the Chinese were not very receptive to Christianity. 

While missionaries were seeking to spread their message, European powers were seeking wider spheres of influence. 

This, they secured through treaties which also demanded toleration and protection for missionaries. Naturally, the Chinese associated the missionary with the trader and government official, and his motives were under suspicion. It was not until 1877 and 1878 when flood and famine brought great suffering to China that the Chinese came to understand the spirit of Christianity. The missionaries of that period gave a marvelous demonstration of the unselfish spirit of true Christians by toiling and suffering and dying with the Chinese. 

When Friends began their work in 1890, there were nearly two thousand missionaries working under forty mission boards. China Inland Mission alone had more than six hundred missionaries. Esther Butler was taken into this great company who were "workers together" in the Kingdom. 

From 1890 to 1940, mission work in China has been carried on against six major disturbances accompanied by incredible cruelty and bloodshed. At times anti-foreign feeling has reached great heights of bitterness and Christians have suf-

fered terrible persecution. There were the riots in Nanking in 1891 when many missionaries lost their lives, much property was destroyed, and missions temporarily closed; the 

Taiping Rebellion in 1899 which was accompanied by much cruelty to the people of the Yangtse Valley; the Boxer Uprising, 1900, which was directed against foreigners and which resulted in the greatest massacre of Christians ever witnessed in a single year in the history of the church; the Chinese Revolution in 1911, which was a successful attempt to overthrow the Manchu Dynasty; the Nanking Incident in 

1927 which was directed against foreigners; and last but not least the present Sino-Japanese War which has necessitated recalling all missionaries from Occupied China. 

These six great and tragic incidents in a period of fifty years may be the birth pangs of a nation being reborn, but they are also the record of one of the most heroic periods in the history of the Christian Church. Against this background and in a critical center of Chinese life, American Friends in Nanking and Luhr have joined hands with other Christians, Methodists, Presbyterians, and Disciples of 

Christ, in a great work.

After she had bought land in Nanking and built a house which she called the Quakerage, Esther Butler started a training school for Bible women. She was just getting started in her work when the troubles in 1891 interrupted its progress but did not discourage this dauntless worker. As soon as possible she was back in Nanking salvaging what she could, getting ready to open an orphanage, and calling to her 

Board to send a doctor.

Other missionaries were sent out including Dr. Lucy A. 

Gaynor who brought money for a hospital. The building was put up in 1895 and was a great asset to the mission. Dr. 

Gaynor continued as head of the hospital until 1912 when she died from typhus contracted while she was caring for

Chinese sufferers. This plague came in the wake of the 

Revolution of 1911. The hospital was destroyed in the uprising but Dr. Gaynor had a temporary building put up and there worked tirelessly. When she herself was stricken, she had no strength with which to combat the disease. Fortunately, after her death an able Chinese woman who had been trained in America filled her place. 

Friends Girls Seminary was opened in the early days of the work under Lenna Stanley's supervision. Evangelistic work was stressed from the beginning. 

For the first ten years, the mission was strictly a women's project supported largely by women. But in 1899, two New 

England Friends sent William A. and Julia B. Estes to Nanking. Julia Estes died two years after her arrival on the field and her husband soon joined another mission. However, the advantage of having a man on the staff was acknowledged and led to the appointment from then on of some married people. This, in turn, led to the appointment of men on the home board and the merging of the Woman's Foreign Missionary Board into the present board of Ohio 

Yearly Meeting. 

A new station was opened in Luhr in 1898. This city of 

30,000 is situated in a rich alluvial plain north of Nanking on a tributary of the Yangtse River. Margaret Holme and

Dr. Isabella French were sent there to carry on evangelistic work and start a dispensary. Dr. George F. De Vol arrived two years later and married Isabella French, to whom he had been engaged in America. They set up a home and a hospital and did a splendid work until George De Vol's death in 1917. 

The next development of the work at Luhr was in the school. Margaret Holme started a boys school in some "miserable sheds" in 1905 but cherished a dream of a really beau-

tiful building that would accommodate the growing school adequately. The dream was realized in 1913 when such a building was ready and Walter and Myrtle Williams took charge of it. Some years later, in 1922, an attractive building was put up for girls of high school grade. These were eventually merged into a "co-ordinated" school with coeducation in all the grades. Ethel Naylor was appointed missionary supervisor of the new system which met with the approval of the Chinese people. The schools enroll between three and four hundred pupils. 

Evangelistic work has ever been central in the program of the Friends China Mission. A large number of exceedingly able and consecrated nationals have helped with this work. 

They have reached out to many villages in the district in many of which are chapels. The membership of the Society of Friends in the Nanking-Luhr areas is about one thousand. 

The story of Ohio Yearly Meeting in China is one full of courage and faith. A large number of missionaries have served and sacrificed and the board has faithfully kept the torch alight at home. After each crisis in China when much damage was done to mission property they have come forward with money to rebuild. Many of the missionaries have died in service. The two sons of the De Vols have taken up the work where their parents left off and are dedicated to the spread of Christianity in China. The only daughter of the De Vols, Catherine Cattell, is a missionary in India under the Ohio Board.

The last great crisis in China has brought much heartache to those who love her. All the American missionaries have had to leave Nanking and Luhr and the mission work is now left in the hands of the Chinese. There are able men and women among them whose lives have already borne much fruit. They have been tested in the past and have come

through victoriously. Strengthened by the prayers of their friends in America they carry on in the spirit of the Master. 

India 

Delia A. Fistler and Esther E. Baird, both of Ohio Yearly

Meeting, were good friends and both, quite independently, felt called to service in India. This was in 1892. Each expressed her desire to friends, the former to J. Walter and 

Emma B. Malone of Cleveland, Ohio, and the latter to Eliza beth Jenkins, president of the Woman's Foreign Missionary 

Society.

J. Walter Malone presented Delia Fistler's concern at a meeting of the mission board during the sessions of the yearly meetings and added that he believed Esther Baird should go with her. When someone asked if Esther Baird would be willing to do so, Elizabeth Jenkins happily told them that just the day before, Esther Baird had asked her how to go about getting to the field. Under an overwhelming sense of 

Divine direction, they accepted the two young women and proceeded with plans for them. 

Esther Butler's experience of being associated with an already established mission in China while she learned the language and studied the situation had been so successful that it was agreed to use the same procedure in India. Providentially, Bishop James Thoburn of the Methodist Church came to Cleveland about this time, and agreed to take the two women into his mission until they were ready to start one for their own board. Bishop James Thoburn had been a prominent speaker at the first great conference of the Woman's Missionary Societies and had the confidence of Friends to a marked degree. Consequently, they were assigned to the Methodist Mission at Muttra. Delia Fistler was made head of the Training School for Christian Workers and Esther Baird, who had nurse's training, was given charge of the

dispensary and medical work.

Later, they were transferred to the Women's Home in Lucknow. 

Eighteen months later, the way opened for them to start their own mission in an unoccupied place. A Wesleyan chaplain of a British regiment told them of a place southwest of Lucknow, sixty miles from Jhansi, called Nowgong. 

Delia Fistler and Esther Baird arrived in Nowgong in 

Bundelkhand on April 1, 1896. They were the first Christian missionaries in that area and they found that God had indeed given them a task that would require all their fortitude, and which had to be started at once. For they had come into the midst of one of the worst famines imaginable. When they opened their door on the morning after their arrival, they found nearly a hundred people who had come to beg for food. The sight of so much suffering must have been a challenge. Esther Baird in her story Adventuring with God says: "There may have been other ways of getting the gospel to Bundelkhand, but God introduced us to the field through famine."

For the next two years, they applied themselves to famine relief, feeding and clothing the hungry and naked, nursing the dying back to health, and finding homes in other missions for some five hundred children who were orphaned in the disaster.

Difficult and discouraging as this task was, famine relief took them at once into the heart of the Indian villages. The two women, isolated in large part from other Americans, gave of their means and their vigor and Christian love. The message that burned within them could not be given in words to these hungry people, but it was given in deed and the hearts of the people were touched. Doors opened to them that might have remained closed for years. 

Before long, they began to realize that they had an orphan-

age of more than fifty children on their hands. Help was very urgent and they found this in Charlotte Bai, a blind woman whom they had known in Lucknow. She was a remarkable woman, for in spite of her blindness, she was able to create a home for the little ones. The children, happily cared for in the orphanage, had to be taught, so a school was started. 

In the first ten years, many avenues of work opened up. 

The missionaries ministered to British soldiers stationed far from their homes, a Girls School was opened in the bazaar, zenana visitation was introduced, itinerating evangelists were employed, the orphanage was an established feature of the mission, and a Friends Church and monthly meeting organization were set up. It was a period of physical building, too. The orphanage consisted of two dormitories, a school room, a small home for the matron, and one for the missionary in charge. They also built stables for the animals and put down three good wells. Last, but not least, new workers had been sent to help.

During all this period, Delia Fistler and Esther Baird looked longingly toward Chhatarpur fourteen miles away. 

It was the capital city of the district and seemed to them the most strategic place for mission headquarters. Hindu prejudice was great, however, and there seemed to be a concerted effort to keep the Christian missionaries out of the city. It was impossible for them to buy land there. Actually, it was twenty-five years before they were allowed even to come to the city limits. In 1921, the Maharajah gave them two acres of land outside the city on which to build a home. Esther 

Baird in Adventuring with God says very humbly, "The open door to Chhatarpur, as well as the splendid work that has been done on mission buildings and the opportunity we have had for evangelization has come largely through the consistent Christian living of our splendid Indian communi-

ty." Some of the native Christians had endured years of persecution as they went about in the gospel ministry. 

Esther Baird was compelled to return home in 1922 because of her health. Just before she left, however, the

Maharajah asked her to bring back a woman doctor and the money to build a hospital. He, in turn, would provide the land for it.

This was an opening the mission had prayed for and Esther Baird's heart rejoiced over it. But money did not come in as quickly as she had hoped when she was at home and she had only a pitifully small sum for her hospital when she returned to India. She went ahead on faith starting with a small dispensary. Dr. Mary Fleming joined the staff in 1925 and advance was made.
