Eventually, a mission compound developed consisting of a simple but beautiful Quaker chapel, a dispensary, a hospital with a well-equipped operating-room, bungalows for the doctor and American nurses, and a small cottage to serve as guest-house for those who, having to travel many miles with their sick, needed hospitality and rest. 

The work at Chhatarpur was given signal recognition in 

1930 when the Viceroy of India, Lord Irwin, and Lady Irwin visited Bundelkhand. Lady Irwin and other important Indian officials visited the hospital and commended its work. 

At this time, Esther Baird was decorated with the Kaiser-i- Hind medal for public service. This was India's seal of approval on the work of Friends in this district and a recognition of nearly forty years of beautiful Christian living. 

The W. M. U. of Friends had honored Esther Baird years before by setting up the Esther Baird Love Fund at a time of financial crisis in America when the future of the mission seemed very dark.

Delia Fistler had to give up her work in 1914 because of

her health. She had never been strong, having been warned during her first year in India that she could not live long. 

The fact that she was able to live in India twenty years is a manifestation of the spirit that glowed within her frail body. 

Esther Baird has had the joy of giving more than fifty years to India, of seeing the work grow from the distressing times of famine relief to a Christian community which is contributing richly to the spiritual life of India. 

This sketch cannot name all those who have served in India nor all the steps in its growth. The mission stations at 

Nowgong, Chhatarpur, and Harpalpur, with their chapels and schools and happy homes are their monuments. KANSAS When Kansas Yearly Meeting was set off by Indiana Yearly Meeting in 1872 missionary interest was high. Samuel 

Purdie was beginning his work in Mexico under Indiana Yearly Meeting and the accounts of his labors there stirred Friends deeply. Kansas was young in 1872, still in a pioneer state, and life was hard. Yet these pioneers looked beyond their great plains to the broad fields of human need that existed in many parts of the world and saw that world won for Christ. They have given to the Society of Friends through the years a large number of its missionaries, men and women who have gone to almost all the mission stations under the American Friends Board of Missions and other boards as well.

The conditions within Kansas Yearly Meeting in the pioneer days are rather vividly described in the First Union 

Report of the Woman's Foreign Missionary Societies of 

Friends, 1887. The women of Kansas Yearly Meeting organized in 1885 and the secretary reported: "The society is just in its infancy. . and is located in a state that is not wealthy. Since Kansas Yearly Meeting was established its

members have met with many discouragements. Their fields have been visited by droughts, grasshoppers, and other destroyers so that they have often been called upon to assist each other. From many places the appeal now comes to help to build houses for worship. Many in the frontier gather in sod houses, some cold and uncomfortable, yet from these very meetings comes a response to the call from heathen lands and many a mite dropped into the Lord's treasury is given at a sacrifice of which those in the East have no conception." Even under these conditions, there were sixteen auxiliaries of the W.F.M.S. with two-hundred and thirty members in all.

In 1886, the yearly meeting decided to undertake the establishment of a mission in Alaska. Elwood W. Weesner of their own yearly meeting came before them and presented his concern to go as a missionary to that country. The yearly meeting considered it prayerfully, realizing how big a responsibility they faced. There was a letter before them from Western Yearly Meeting at the same time asking them to help them in their new mission in Matehuala in Mexico. 

The latter would have been easier, perhaps, but they chose to recognize the call of Elwood Weesner.

While events were moving thus in Kansas, Francis W. 

Bangham of Wilmington, Ohio, heard a call to mission work in Alaska, too. His meeting was in sympathy with his desire to go and agreed to support him. When he heard of the proposed mission under Kansas Yearly Meeting he corresponded with Elwood W. Weesner with the result that the two men decided to go together. They went in June, 1887.

Alaska was practically unknown territory to most of the people of the United States when President Johnson purchased it in 1864. The idea that it was an arctic land of ice and snow was the accepted one. The purchase of such territory seemed so foolish that the President's political en-

emies labelled it Seward's Ice-Box for the Secretary of State who accomplished the transaction. Slowly it dawned on 

Americans that there were Indians and Eskimos in Alaska who were now under the government of the United States, and with that realization came a sense of responsibility for them. Missions were encouraged by both civil and religious bodies and were started by some of the larger denominations in various parts of the new country. The reports sent back by missionaries did much to clear up many misconceptions about Alaska and its people.

The two Friends apparently had no special location in mind when they set out, trusting to the leading of the Spirit to continue to guide them and by the time they reached Alaska, the way opened for them to start their work on Douglas Island, in the southeastern end of the country. They received $200 from the government to help them open a school.

There were about fifteen-hundred Indians on Douglas Island who had earlier been described as war-like. They were now following the peaceful trades of wood-cutting, fishing, canning and drying fish, and working in a rich gold mine which had been opened just a short time before the missionaries arrived. These activities kept the population of the island scattered during the summer but they all settled in the town of Douglas for the winter.

In Douglas there were seventy-five children of school age with no school opportunities. A school was immediately opened by Francis Bangham. For the first few months only eighteen children attended. He continued to hold school for four years working against indifference and apathy, gradually winning the confidence of the Indians.

Meeting work was carried on by Elwood Weesner. That also was discouraging at first. At the end of the first five

years, however, forty people requested membership with Friends and a splendid Sunday school was being carried on. 

New workers had been added who strengthened the work greatly.

One interesting phase of the mission work was that of the orphanage which was in the home of Silas and Anna Moon. 

Starting with one little girl they sometimes had as many as fifteen children under their care.

Kansas Yearly Meeting maintained the Alaska Mission for practically twenty-five years. The work had been exceedingly difficult partly because of the fact that no workers remained very long, and also the fact that it had been a heavy burden for the yearly meeting. Government schools were established for the Indian children so that the Friends school was not needed long. However, Friends furnished most of the teachers for the government schools in Alaska for a long time.

In 1911, the yearly meeting had before it the question of retiring from Alaska and leaving their congregation of about ninety Friends under the care of the Presbyterian Mission. 

Friends had grown to feel a close fellowship with the Alaskan people through their missionaries and through some of the young people who had come to the United States to study in the government schools. The thought of severing the ties of Christian fellowship was hard. They realized, however, that they could not expand their program nor provide facilities for the education of the meeting children. 

Even more important was the fact that almost all the rest of the Indians were members of the Presbyterian Church. 

The Friends Church tended to separate the small group from the rest of their people and to create a barrier. The Presbyterians, on the other hand, were well organized having a number of missions in this territory. They were willing to

take over the Friends group and respect their views on sacraments. At the same time, the openings before Friends in 

Africa, Mexico, and Cuba were so challenging that it seemed wiser to turn the work in southeastern Alaska over to this competent board and support the other fields. The matter was accomplished by 1912.

For the next several years, Kansas Yearly Meeting supported the work of the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions in all their fields. Large numbers of their young people responded to the needs of Africa, Jamaica, Mexico, Cuba, Japan, and China. Kansas Yearly Meeting withdrew her official support from the American Friends Board of Missions about 1932, although some members continue to send contributions. Feeling that they would be happier in carrying on their own mission, they considered some of the African work or one of the provinces in Cuba. Eventually they decided to open an entirely new station in a new territory and chose Arthur Chilson, then serving as yearly meeting superintendent, to superintend it. 

In October, 1933, Arthur and Edna Chilson and their daughter Rachel Chilson, left for Africa to "find an untouched field" and start a mission for Kansas Yearly Meeting. 

This was not the first pioneer enterprise for Arthur Chilson. Thirty-one years earlier he had accompanied Willis

Hotchkiss and Edgar Hole to Africa to establish an industrial mission somewhere in Kenya Colony. The story of those days is told in the chapter on Africa. The years from 1902 until 1928 had been filled with service among a hitherto untouched people. Arthur Chilson knew this time what this new venture for the Master meant. Although he was older in body, the fire burned as brightly as ever in his heart. And the call of the untouched millions in Africa came as clearly in 1933 as it had come in 1900. What he might lack in youth-

ful vigor now was more than compensated for by experience and knowledge.

In the party of three this time were Edna Chilson, faithful companion and co-worker who had shared joyously in the African work for more than twenty years, and their daughter Rachel who had spent most of her life in Africa. This was indeed a happy fulfillment and they set out with the support of a loyal yearly meeting.

They went directly to the east coast of Africa then journeyed inland. They finally chose a location in the Belgian

Congo to the west of Lake Tanganyika in the Urundi territory. There they established the Friends Africa Gospel Mission in the most densely populated section of Africa. This part of Africa was formerly German East Africa but had been given as mandated territory to Belgium after the First 

World War. The Danish Baptist Mission had been given five station concessions which had been under the German Lutherans but had been able to occupy only one. They welcomed the Chilsons and did all they could to get them established in one of the stations.

The mission is situated on a 100 acre estate in beautiful country 5,500 feet high, 240 miles south of the equator. The sun gets very hot, bringing the temperature to 124° at times, but indoors it remains around 70°. There are nearly 500,000 people in the territory assigned to the Chilsons and theirs is the first mission to them.

The first days at the mission were much like the beginnings at Kaimosi years before. They had to build a home, a chapel, a school; there were gardens to make, trees to plant, roads to be cleared, and wounds to be bound. Always there was preaching to be done in the villages around. They anticipated a mission patterned after the one at Kaimosi, Kenya Colony, where educational, medical, industrial, and evangelistic work is carried on. That was the dream.

In a little while they had opened a school with nearly two hundred children enrolled and church services were generally well attended. Reinforcements were needed and these came in the persons of Esther Chilson Choate and her husband, Ralph Choate.

With help the work grew. They opened another school at 

Kitega and another station at Mutaho under Ralph and Esther Choate. They became a recognized mission in November, 1936.

Church work was growing so fast that a meetinghouse was necessary. The congregation usually numbered over a thousand.

More help was sent for the industrial program which was becoming too arduous for Arthur Chilson. The workers were Arthur Ford, son of Jefferson Ford of the F. A. M., and his wife. Unfortunately they could not remain long on account of Mrs. Ford's health but while they were there, much was accomplished on the buildings.

Sorrow came to the little band of workers early in January 1939, with the passing of Arthur Chilson. He was buried in the heart of Africa which he had loved and served for so long and even in his passing his death testified to his faith. 

Edna Chilson and six others are carrying on the work in Urundi. It was not easy at first when their leader was taken, but good foundations were laid and all are so deeply consecrated to their Saviour that the winning of the Africans to 

Him is a continuous challenge.

Kansas Yearly Meeting gives liberally to missions and the Woman's Society is very active. The difficult pioneer days seem to be over but Kansas Friends still have wide vision. 

Their interest centers largely in their African field with its chapels and schools and tremendous opportunities.

OREGON 

When a yearly meeting was established in Oregon in 1893, a missionary board was appointed, and a mission station opened at the same time.

Before that, however, Oregon Friends had been giving to missions. When they were a part of Iowa Yearly Meeting, they gave through their board to the mission in Jamaica. 

Kansas Yearly Meeting Mission Board asked Oregon to send a field matron to Alaska in 1892. They sent Frances Liter, a minister and trained nurse, to Douglas where she remained a year and a half. Naturally, they were interested in Alaska. 

It was their near neighbor.

Their interest in Kake Village on Kuprianoff Island was aroused by the death of Charles Edwards a member of the yearly meeting, in that village in 1893. He was teaching in a government school and doing missionary work that year. 

He saw that white men were introducing a drink like whiskey into Alaska and seeing its evil effects on the Indians, he tried to prevent the practice. He lost his life in the effort. 

He had succeeded in gaining the confidence of the Indians, however, and after his death, they wanted someone like him to take his place. Oregon Yearly Meeting heard the call and opened a station in Kake Village in 1894. Silas and Anna

Moon, who had been working for Kansas Yearly Meeting in Douglas, which was close to Kake Village, went for them. 

There were about three hundred Indians in Kake Village, thirteen houses and a small Government schoolhouse. The condition of the people was most wretched and pitiable as they lived in crowded houses, drank a great deal, and were incredibly superstitious. Frances Liter followed the Moons six months later to Kake Village where a healing ministry was greatly needed.

Conditions of living were exceedingly primitive for the

missionaries and the work difficult. The Indians at first exploited them in every way they could. Silas and Anna Moon, however, started church services and school work immediately and soon won their friendship and trust. Frances Liter visited in the homes, teaching them better health habits. 

She retired from the field in two years but others went to help.

The situation improved slowly. A small congregation attended services regularly, children settled down to better school work, the homes were better cared for, and the village in general was more wholesome.

When Kansas Yearly Meeting decided to turn its work over to the Presbyterian Board, Oregon felt that it would be wise to do the same with her mission. During seventeen years they had expended more than eleven thousand dollars for the mission and many had given a year or more to the work. The same reasons that Kansas gave for giving up the work were behind Oregon's decision, and the same sadness over severing ties of friendship was felt.

Oregon Yearly Meeting remained in association with the 

Five Years Meeting until 1927. Until that time she shared to some extent in the mission work of the mission board of the Five Years Meeting, but other calls came to her with which she had closer sympathy and when she broke off her connections with the larger body of Friends, she established an independent mission work.

The present work of Oregon Yearly Meeting is in Bolivia, South America. The writer is indebted to Mrs. Marie Haines of Portland, Oregon, for the account of that work from which this digest is made. A golden chain of events leads from 

Ramona, California to South America, back to Oregon and again to Bolivia resulting in a significant work. 

One day, William Abel, a young Indian in Ramona was in-

vited by an elderly lady to attend a revival meeting in the Friends Church. William was not particularly interested in religion but, having nothing else to do, he accepted her invitation and went. The young man was won to Christ in that meeting and dedicated himself to His service. He went to the Philippine Islands as a missionary where he remained eleven years. After further study and preparation, he was sent to La Paz, Bolivia, as a missionary to the Indians. 

This was a challenging and fascinating country for a missionary. La Paz, the capital of Bolivia, lies in a natural basin two miles wide and fifteen hundred feet deep at an elevation of 12,500 feet above sea level. It is called the city of the clouds. The average temperature is 50° F. The population consists of about 200,000 people, white, Indian, and mestizo or mixed.

Bolivia is the land of the Andes, and the llama, of ancient ruins and shrines, and the highest navigable lake in the western hemisphere. Spain conquered it in the brave days of the conquistadors, enslaved the people, and set up a strong Catholic Church. The latter has become so degenerate and mixed with such superstitious practices that it can no longer speak to the people. But the buildings with their tall spires reaching toward the heavens are constant reminders of the innate love of religion which is the Indian's heritage, and lasting evidence of the days of the Spaniards.

Life is simple in the mountains of Bolivia save as fear and superstition bind the people to a harsh religion. 

In one of these villages lived Juan Allyon in the first years of this century. His mother was a devout woman and brought her son up well. He was sent to a mission school in 

La Paz where he received an excellent education in Spanish, English, the Quichua dialect, his own Aymara tongue, and music. Religion came to him naturally and beautifully when

he was quite young. William Abel came to La Paz while 

Juan was a student in the mission school and the two soon became friends. For a little time, they preached and sang to the Indians in happy comradeship. Within a few months, however, William Abel was stricken with small-pox and died. It almost seemed that God had sent him especially to touch the heart of the young lad, for from then on Juan's life was completely dedicated to service to his own people. 

There was a Friends Bible school in Chiquimula, Guatemala, under California Mission Board to which Juan felt he must go. It was a long way from La Paz, Bolivia, to Chiquimula, Guatemala, and Juan had no money. But he set out and reached it after one hundred and twenty-four days and started his training the next day. That was March 9, 1921.

While he was studying there he met a lovely orphan girl from Honduras. As soon as he was graduated, he married

Tomasa Valle, and they were sent to Bolivia as the missionaries of the small mission church in Chiquimula. It was a wonderful day for Juan when he could set his face again to his beloved mountains and his own people. 

Juan and Tomasa Allyon opened their first mission in La Paz. They had to endure many hardships and much persecution at first but they remained faithful and in a few years they had a church organized. They also had a good meeting, Bible training, night classes, and with his gift in music Juan was able to bring that joy to his Indians.

The work soon was growing so fast that the little mission church at Chiquimula could no longer support it and Oregon Yearly Meeting was asked to undertake it. 

The mission was officially adopted by Oregon Yearly Meeting of Friends in June, 1930. Almost at once they began to send workers from America to help Juan and Tomasa Allyon.

For three years all went well. They reached out to the villages, trained native pastors, and opened schools. But they were destined to face some dark days. Juan and Tomasa had to return to Central America because of Tomasa's health, it became difficult to get workers, and of the eighteen young men who were in training, some were either taken into the army or had escaped to avoid it and a few were not strong enough to face persecution.

But these Friends persisted, still more workers were sent out, and new ways were opened. Native Christians volunteered to take more and more responsibility even to organizing themselves into teams for the evangelization of their respective districts.

In addition to the work in La Paz, there are centers in 

Amacari, Corrcovo, Pongon Huyo, and Puerto Perez. In all these centers, there is growth as more and more villages are open to the native and American pastors. One pastor walks twenty miles every Sunday to speak to one congregation.

One development that has brought much satisfaction to the mission as a whole is the part they have been given in translation work. The following minute from the Yearly 

Meeting Report 1940 tells of that. 

Aymara Translation and Revision of the Gospels 

"A Committee composed of eight members representing four missions engaged in the evangelization of the Aymaras, was appointed by the American Bible Society of New York. 

Mr. John Richie, A.B.S. representative for Ecuador, Peru, and Bolivia, was the man to whom the committee was directly responsible. Carroll G. Tamplin was asked to preside at the sessions of the Translation and Revision committee. This came as a compliment to the whole mission. "After five months of severe and exacting work the committee finished its task of revising the Sr. Angel Medina

Manuscripts of the four gospels and the Acts of the Apostles. 

The committee disbanded and the president spent another month in getting the new Manuscripts in order and catalogued to send to the printer in New York. "In a day when the Scriptures have been translated into more than a thousand tongues and dialects, Oregon Yearly Meeting is to be congratulated on having had a part in such a work and in having four members of its staff participate in it. "Special tribute should be paid to Juan Allyon without whose sacrifice, separation for many months from his family, richness of vocabulary and intimate knowledge of the Indian manner of expression and psychological reactions, his attention to exactness, harmony, and adornment which have enriched the practical literary value of the work, the revision committee must have fallen far short or failed of its purpose."

Yearly Meeting Minutes, 1940 

Since that writing, Juan Allyon has finished the translation of the entire New Testament into the Aymara dialect and it is now ready for the revision committee. The gospels thus printed have already borne much fruit and are a source of great help and blessing in the work. 

The most recent report of the state of the mission lists seven missionaries from the United States and six Indian pastors on the field. There are two other missionaries under appointment. Oregon Yearly Meeting is not large and this seems a remarkable program for a yearly meeting of its size. 

Increasingly, people in the United States are aware of the great needs in these South American countries. 

As our political and economic relations with them become closer, it is well to know that small groups of Christians are at work not to win favors for their government or to exploit their

mines, but to share with these neighbors the fruits of the spirit.

BOARD OF MISSIONS OF CALIFORNIA YEARLY MEETING 

California Friends remained part of Iowa Yearly Meeting until 1895 and participated in its missionary program in Jamaica and also assisted with the Ramallah, Palestine work. 

Independent mission work was undertaken to answer the call of Anna Hunnicut to service in northern Alaska. cern.

Anna Hunnicut was a student in Penn College in Oskaloosa, Iowa, in 1895. She greatly desired to work with the 

Eskimos and wrote to her meeting in Whittier of her con- The yearly meeting was just being organized at this time and Friends generally felt that they were not prepared to be responsible for a new mission. However, they did feel the burden of Anna Hunnicut's call laid upon them and could not ignore it. At this time, the way opened for them to send her and Lizzie Morris to assist the Oregon Mission at Kake

Village, and advised her that she was to study the possibility of a yearly meeting work in the future. The Christian Endeavor Societies of California Yearly Meeting undertook the support of the two workers.

The two young women remained at Kake Village for a year after which Anna Hunnicut accepted a position as a government teacher on Douglas Island where Charles and Mae Replogle were in charge of the Friends Mission. 

But Anna Hunnicut could not give up her interest in the Eskimos. She spoke to the United States Commissioner of 

Education for Alaska about it when he was visiting her school. Dr. Jackson kept it in mind and when a delegation of Eskimos at Kotzebue met him and pleaded for a teacher, he hinted that there might be someone in Douglas who would come.

After Dr. Jackson left, the Eskimos held a council and de-

cided to get immediate action. They selected two men to go to Douglas at once. It was autumn and too late for a passing steamer so the two men rowed two hundred and fifty miles to Cape Prince of Wales where they could get a boat to Sitka near Douglas Island.

They went at once to Charles Replogle who heard it as a call to Macedonia. When Anna Hunnicut, who was away at the time, heard of it she wrote to her yearly meeting asking if they would stand by her if she went. The way opened wonderfully for both Charles Replogle and Anna Hunnicut to go to the States to plead the cause of the Eskimos. They were successful in their efforts to arouse interest and enthusiasm generally. Robert Samms, a member of the Los 

Angeles meeting offered to go for a year to do the pioneer work. In the summer of 1897, Robert Samms and his bride, Carrie Rowe Samms, and Anna Hunnicut left San Francisco for the sixty day voyage in a sailing vessel to northern Alaska.

The three young workers received a warm welcome from the grateful Eskimos.

Kotzebue at that time had a population of about sixteen hundred people. They lived a nomadic life part of the year, getting their living by hunting, fishing, and trapping. But when the long winter night settled on Alaska they came into the village and that was the time when the missionaries were busiest. 

Anna Hunnicut married Z. E. Foster two years later and moved to Deering, Alaska, where she continued to teach. 

The Samms who had gone for a year remained five before going home. They remained in the States a few years then returned to Alaska where they still reside. 

Other workers were sent to Kotzebue and proceeded to open new mission stations. In 1905, one was opened at Kiva-

lena, one hundred miles northwest of Kotzebue. This was the most northern Friends Mission Station in the world, and from accounts of the hardships which the missionaries faced, the most difficult. Another station was opened up the Kobuck River the same year.

The following years saw rapid development of the work. 

A number of Friends went to Alaska as teachers in the Government schools in much the same spirit as those who went to the little schools in the Tennessee Mountains to take part in all community activities.

By 1912, Friends had six churches with more than six hundred members and property valued at $14,000. A large number of trained native pastors lived in little huts in outlying villages and cared for small congregations. One of the big problems they faced was the difficulty of building a consistent program because of the rather unsettled life the members lived part of the year. 

Fifteen years later there were eight mission stations. 

Church membership had increased to 774. There were Sunday schools in every center and there were four monthly meetings. 

Friends Alaska Mission was forty years old in 1937. Robert and Carrie Samms were living at the Noatak Mission then. They had had the privilege and the blessing of helping bring about many of the changes that made life happier for their friends in their Alaskan field. The population had grown to two thousand and of these fifteen hundred were associated with the church. The membership was faithful in attendance at Bible classes and all church services. Two missionary families and five or six native families and one single missionary were at work. There were nine church buildings in as many villages so that the field was well cared for.

The establishment of a quarterly meeting was an important development in 1939. This made the native people feel responsible for their churches and they accepted the new responsibility cheerfully.

The war will certainly bring about many changes in the life of Alaska. The great highway that has been built, the impact of the army, and the drawing of Indians and Eskimos into the army of the United States cannot but make some great difference. Ira and Parolee Downs are working at Kotzebue and Robert and Carrie Samms at Kivalena, today. A large share of the work there is being done by the Eskimos. 

God has given California Yearly Meeting a great opportunity in helping prepare the Eskimos spiritually for the crisis they now face. Guatemala 

When the missionary superintendent of California gave his report in 1902 he made a reference to two of the members of California Yearly Meeting who were in "neglected Guatemala." The two young men were Thomas J. Kelley and 

Clark Buckley who had been students at the Training School at Huntington Park and had gone out as independent missionaries. Soon after this report was made, news came of the death of Clark Buckley. He was found dead by the roadside with his pack of Bibles and tracts. Thomas Kelley was in California at the time presenting the claims of the work and the news of Clark Buckley's death was a challenge to the members of yearly meeting. A small band of people, five adults and two children, went out in 1903 without any organization behind them but with the sympathy and pecuniary support of large numbers of Friends. 

California Yearly Meeting's Missionary Board approved and encouraged this work but did not adopt it until 1906. 

The Christian Endeavor Societies of the Yearly Meeting had felt the burden of the work and supported Esther Bond and

Alice Zimmer, the two of the original band who had remained on the field. They sent the following communication to the yearly meeting in 1905, and the next year the Guatemala field was taken over by the yearly meeting on behalf of the Christian Endeavor Society: 

"For the past few years some of our young people of California Yearly Meeting have been looking for a field in which the Endeavorers could work as a Union. As we followed the 

Master's leadings the open door presented was Guatemala, with our two spirit-filled workers, Esther Bond and Alice 

Zimmer. At the Christian Endeavor session at our last Year ly Meeting our missionary superintendent was instructed to take pledges for Guatemala, and when the amount reached $250 to ask the Yearly Meeting Board of Foreign Missions to adopt the field and let us be responsible for raising the required amount. The Lord has now answered our prayers and has given us more than we asked. We have pledges for $276.40 and two societies to hear from. Yours for the millior and a half in dark Guatemala.”

It was freely discussed and the following resolution was unanimously passed: "Resolved, that we congratulate the 

Christian Endeavor Society and thank God that they have succeeded in securing liberal pledges for the support of the Guatemala field, and that this board approves the adoption of this field, and recommends that California Yearly Meeting of Friends Church assume that field on behalf of the Christian Endeavor Society of the Yearly Meeting, they becoming responsible for its support without dropping their present engagements." 

Guatemala is one of the republics of Central America, bordering Mexico on the south. It was under Spain for almost three hundred years, 1524-1821, so that there is a mixture of Spanish stock in the racial group. Guatemala was for a brief time part of the Mexican Empire, then a part of the union of

Central American Republics, but in 1839 it seceded from the union and assumed the name Republic of Guatemala on March 21, 1847. 

It is the second largest of the Central American republics and the most populous. It has a population of over two million people, one-half of whom are of pure Indian descent and the rest mixed. The chief export of Guatemala is coffee which is grown on the rich, volcanic soil of the plateau and the western slope. Other crops in different parts of the country are wheat, corn, cocoa, sugar-cane, bananas, and rubber. 

As in all Spanish-speaking countries the religion of Guatemala is predominantly Catholic, and as in so many churches which have a large Indian membership Catholicism and Indian superstition have combined to form a strange blending of the two. The result has been to retard the development of the country because little was done in the three hundred years of Spanish rule for the education or spiritual welfare of the people.

Protestant missions are comparatively recent in Guatemala and the first missionary was sent at the request of Justo 

Rufino Barcios, then President of the Republic. He presented his request to the Presbyterian Board in 1873 and offered to pay the expenses of the first missionary to the field. 

But that first missionary, even with such authoritative sponsorship, was not to be received by the populace very easily. As late as 1882, the president had to provide a bodyguard for him to protect him from fanaticism in the streets of the national capital. That day is long past, thanks to the brave men and women who went in the name of Jesus to these people, and Protestant missions are quite extensive now.

Friends established their mission center at Chiquimula, in the southwest corner of Guatemala. A number of workers

were sent there who started schools and churches, trained native evangelists and printed large numbers of tracts and magazines. 

Charles S. White visited the mission in 1925 and took back to California a good account of twenty years of labor in that field. He found thirty-eight centers in five districts. There were thirty-five chapels built and in use and others under construction. Five quarterly meetings had been set up.

The Chiquimula Church had sent Juan and Tomasa Allyon to Bolivia as its missionaries, already told about in the work of Oregon Yearly Meeting. The native church was coming to self-government and self-support and was carrying on a program of evangelization. There were 1,874 believers and fifty-five leaders in the native church. 

In the field of education, the mission was conducting three schools, one co-educational Bible School of nineteen pupils, a Girls School enrolling 104 pupils, and a Boys School with fifty-two. There was in addition, a kindergarten, a carpenter shop, a book store, and a printing-press. A dispensary was being operated by a native nurse but the mission was looking forward to a bigger program of health work. 

It was evident that the missionaries worked tremendously hard and were building well.

While the work was growing in Chiquimula, the way opened for a mission in the adjoining republic of Honduras. 

This station was opened in 1914. There were two centers, one at Tegucigalpa, the capital, and one at La Esperanza. Because of the unsettled state of the country, the work here proceeded more slowly.

Honduras has a history much like that of Guatemala but it has had less security. Although it is one of the large republics of Central America, its population in 1930 was under one million.

The two missions and Salvador are very closely united, the stronger one at Chiquimula, particularly in the early days, helping in many ways in Honduras.

The 1943 report of the missions in Central America shows that in Chiquimula evangelism, still the center of the work, is being carried on extensively. Many conferences are held for evangelistic, pastoral, and teaching services. Work is carried on in twelve districts in which there are regularly established monthly and quarterly meetings. These form a yearly meeting. In this field there are fifty organized churches which support thirty-four workers. 

The Bible Training School, Berea, has about forty pupils, and a grammar school, for boarding and day pupils has an enrollment of about one hundred. The clinic, the book store, and the print shop are great assets to the mission, the latter employing four young men and an apprentice and turning out a great variety of literature. The reports estimate between four and five thousand believers.

The Honduras Mission now has work in six districts and a 

Bible Training School of seven students. 

California Yearly Meeting now supports approximately thirty missionaries on the field. That is an ambitious program for one yearly meeting, but the challenge of Central America is great. The winning of the people of these countries and Alaska has been laid on the hearts of California Friends in the faith that they can meet the challenges. 

Spanish Mission 

The California which was added to the Union in 1850 was a Spanish country and most of the inhabitants were Catholics. When the migration from the East began, the new settlers were suddenly aware of the fact that they were coming in to possess a land already occupied. Protestants began almost at once to start missions among the people.

For a long time, under the Jesuits, there had been flourishing missions among the Indians of California. A series of famous missions was established in which the Jesuits actually took the place of fathers, gathering their families of believers about them and giving them a paternalistic rule which made life happy and beneficent but did not prepare them for independence. When that great missionary period ended and mission lands were confiscated, the Indians suffered greatly. The Government of the United States belatedly tried to save the situation when they inaugurated their new policy, but many had perished before that time. 

When Friends began to migrate to the far West they joined other Protestants in work with the Mexicans who had been ceded with the land in 1849. The W.F.M.S. which was organized in 1895 at the same time as the yearly meeting was made responsible for the mission work of Friends. They decided to unite with the Interdenominational Spanish Society active in Southern California. When that organization disbanded in 1902, the W.F.M.S. hearing the call of work right at home, employed Ervin and Margaret Taber to give full time to the field. They were in San Diego then but moved to Whittier where a successful mission was established. In 

1909 the W.F.M.S. also undertook the responsibility for a 

Spanish Mission and Industrial Home which was set up at the home of Philena B. Hadley at Whittier. It started with three children. Soon there were eighteen little girls enjoying the advantages of a Christian home. The beginnings of a church program were made in Whittier, then in other towns near by.

Within a few years the work showed signs of growth and increasingly Mexican pastors were enlisted. The home at 

Whittier continued for some time with eighteen girls and the membership of the yearly meeting kept up its interest. 

There are Mexican churches in Whittier and Bell today.

The congregations are not large in these places but they con tinue to meet a need. Members of Whittier and Bell Meet ings help with Sunday schools and other programs. The home was given up some years ago.

Japanese Work 

The last missionary work to come under California was the work among the Japanese. This, interestingly enough, was begun by Mr. and Mrs. Takahashi, the latter a graduate of Friends Girls School in Tokyo. They lived in Oakland at the time.

The Japanese had been encouraged to come to California at the time the State was growing as a result of the gold rush. 

Their industry and efficiency were appreciated by the Americans and in a little while the rich soil of California was made to bear much fruit because of their labors. But they were lonely in the new country. Language difficulties cut them off from social contacts with the Americans. Mrs. 

Takahashi saw that loneliness was causing them to fall into temptations and she undertook to open her home to them. 

She also invited Americans to meet with them, and as friendship developed, and they came to understand Christianity, the beginnings of a Japanese Church were made. 

The W.F.M.S. and the Christian Endeavor Society of the 

Berkeley Friends Church were enlisted too, and as a result, an evening school and a Sunday afternoon service were started in the church.

Pasadena Monthly Meeting started work for the Japanese in 1908 and it became a sort of center from which other groups were started. Classes in English were offered, and a 

Home was opened there.

Whittier Meeting also started a night school under the W.F.M.S. They enlisted Japanese students, and later Japanese pastors took charge of church meetings.

One able worker came rather unexpectedly. Bunji Kida, a

Japanese Christian, came to the United States to study evangelism in Friends churches. He visited a number of yearly meetings and as he continued in America he came to feel a concern to remain with the Japanese here. His work among his Buddhist compatriots was singularly blessed as he served as an itinerant evangelist.

Many beautiful friendships grew out of this work which were a great blessing and helped when any crisis arose between Japan and the United States. 

The big test came, of course, in 1941 with the outbreak of the war. Brief passages from the reports of California Yearly Meeting for 1942 and 1943 help us to see how deep the friendships were that had been growing through the years. 

These tell about the Norwalk and Whittier work. "All went happily until December 7, when the incident of Pearl Harbor broke upon us like a bomb from the Infernal 

Region. Americans said: 'I do not believe it;' Japanese said the same; but the truth became apparent and brought consternation, embarrassment, disappointment, grief, and war.

Pastor Folger invited the Norwalk Japanese Church to worship with Whittier the next Sunday. Pastor Ishikawa said, 'Our hearts wept in the warm atmosphere.' "Our services have gone on and the Sunday School has been held until the last session, April 12. Easter 1942 came amid confusion of moving but some angelic spirits decorated beautifully; a class from Whittier Church prepared and gave a program. Some of the Japanese girls with their teacher made the Easter baskets. Isamu Ishikawa made a speech of thanks and said, 'We were made to realize that Easter would always be with us, it could not be blacked out.' The Sunday after, the few and scattered members reassembled and had a sad but fond farewell service with tears yet praises to God.

"Many of Whittier members and missionaries followed these people to Downey where busses and cars formed a great procession for Santa Anita, like a funeral. "The first Sunday at Santa Anita a religious service attended by over 1,500 was held. One of our members sent word to Ishikawa to bring all our Sunday School supplies. "Goods are stored in the church building. The parsonage is rented to reliable people. Finances from church and Sunday School supplies are used for Sunday School supplies and for new assemblage in Santa Anita.-A letter from the Ishikawas, housed in a horse stall in Santa Anita says: 'All well and happy, and thanks to all helpers in evacuation.' "Since Easter of 1942, our Japanese Friends have endured a long chapter of experiences in concentration camps, behind barbed wire fences, and in horse stalls on the Santa Anita race track; and in inadequate rooms, fed and protected by armed soldiers. "The friends on the outside, including the American 

Friends Service Committee, your Japanese Committee and thousands of other friends have done what they could in supplying needed books and articles of comfort, words of cheer, visits and letters. "Thousands of letters were exchanged and Christian fellowship thus fostered. Our returned missionaries, the Binfords, Alice Lewis Pearson, Katherine E. Fanning, Herbert and Madeline Nicholson, with American Friends Service

Committee members and local pastors and school teachers, have been faithful in service and influence for fair treatment of Japanese. "When it became evident that mass evacuation of Japanese was inevitable, Pasadena Federated Missions, which holds title to the church buildings and parsonage of the Japanese Union Church, made plans in co-operation with the

church members, to store their household goods and that of their friends in the church buildings. The city fire chief and a neighboring storage company gave helpful instruction on how to store. For weeks Japanese and some Christian friends worked together to be ready for the fateful days. 

When they came, groups from the churches of the city were at the stations to give a farewell lunch and a friendly handclasp to these exiles from their homes." 

This all too brief outline cannot convey what service in 

Alaska, Guatemala, Honduras, Salvador, and among the

Mexicans and Japanese in California has meant in human and spiritual terms. The isolation of the Alaskan missionaries and the great discouragements as well as successes of the work; the difficulties of working in Catholic countries where so much superstition and opposition had to be overcome; the sensitiveness for fellow Americans of other tongues and races cannot be put into words or statistics. 

The record is one that shows obedience to the Master's command: "And ye shall be witnesses unto me both in Jerusalem, and in all Judea, and in Samaria, and unto the uttermost part of the earth." 

Chengtu, China

English Friends have had mission work in Chengtu, Szechwan, China since 1895. American Friends have had a close tie with it through Robert and Margaret Simkin and Arnold and Lois Vaught. Alumni and students of Haverford College, Haverford, Pennsylvania, have contributed to the support of the Simkins for many years. The Friends Service 

Council of London Yearly Meeting has supported the 

Vaughts. The American Friends Board of Missions includes an account of the work of Robert and Margaret Simkin each year in its annual report, but actually has no administrative responsibility for it. The board functions as an agency

through which funds are transmitted to them and through which Friends in America can keep in touch with their work. 

Robert Davidson selected Chengtu as a place of work because it was a large and important intellectual and cultural center of a great province. Chungking, the capital of Free 

China, is in Szechwan Province. This province is rich in mineral deposits and coal, and is a fertile agricultural area. 

When the Davidsons went there, they had many hardships and much discouragement. However, they were able to open a school for girls, a dispensary, and hold preaching services. The Boxer Rebellion interrupted their work but following it there was rapid growth. By 1916 English Friends had twenty-nine workers on the field. There were six schools, two hospitals, and a dispensary under their care. 

One splendid example of mission comity has taken place in Chengtu in which Friends have played a part. They have united with American Baptists, and American and Canadian 

Methodists in establishing West China Union University.

Dr. Henry T. Hodgkin, who was well-known to American 

Friends, had a big share in planning for this. Since the great migration of university students from Occupied to Free China, this university has received large numbers of them. 

One of the last reports stated that there were fifteen hundred regular students and as many special. Robert Simkin taught 

Church History for many years at the university but more recently he and Margaret Simkin have been helping at the Friends Center in Shanghai, a hostel established in 1939 as a war emergency service which does many kinds of relief.

CHAPTER XIII A FORWARD LOOK 

The story of Friends Missions is not ended. We have surveyed it through only two phases of its service. The first was the almost lightning-like period when in a few years the message of George Fox flashed throughout the British Isles and was carried to Europe and the continent by men and women on fire with the truths that had come to them. The second period of Friends missionary work was marked by the slower process of founding missions. If this period seems less spectacular than the first, it has in it a more permanent element. In those first joyous days, ministers remained but a few days or weeks in one place, but the second band of missionaries gave years, and in many instances, a life-time of constructive, inspired service.

In such a study as we have made, we have sought to trace the steps which led to the founding of missions and to see how "Friendly Concerns" have the stamp of Divine inspiration. We have also tried to see what has been accomplished in each field. Because the scope of our study was so vast we have had to leave out many valuable and interesting details such as the warm, human stories of changed lives and triumphant living which fill the annals of missionary literature. 

It is as though we have been sitting on the hill above the town looking down on the scene below. We see the homes, the town-hall, the schools, and the church-spires in which much of the life of the town is carried on, but it is of our neighbors, our friends, and our families that we are thinking, without whom the buildings would have no meaning. So in the story we may seem to have pointed out the landmarks

A Forward Look 

257 but the human and spiritual values are what have really counted.

Although each field under the American Friends Board of Missions differs as brothers and sisters in a family differ from one another, there are certain clearly defined characteristics in the work in all of them that are almost startling in their similarity.

The first of these is that in practically every instance individual concerns led to the opening of the mission. One needs to think only of Samuel Purdie, Evi Sharpless, Sybil Jones, and Esther Baird in this connection. They and others in their faithfulness to the leading of the Spirit were able to carry large numbers in the Society of Friends into great service.

A second family resemblance is the educational work that has been done in every field. Schools were opened almost simultaneously with the establishment of meetings or in connection with them. Educational work continues to have an important place in the foreign fields. 

A third aspect of Friends is devolution, which means placing the work in the hands of nationals as quickly as they are ready to undertake it. From the very first, large numbers of nationals have been enlisted as pastors and teachers in the development of each mission. In three of the foreign fields under the Board yearly meetings have been established. They are Cuba, Jamaica, and Palestine. Friends in 

Africa anticipate setting-up their own yearly meeting in the near future. Mission work in Mexico is done by nationals entirely, the Mission Board making only a moderate financial contribution to it. The majority of the teachers in Friends 

Mission Schools are nationals; in Cuba there are no American teachers at all. The number of missionaries in each field has decreased accordingly. The development of native lead-

ership is the goal of missions and has been achieved to a marked degree by Friends. This, however, places greater demands on the missionaries who go to the field as they must be highly-trained if they are to serve comparably. 

Christian Endeavor societies made a great contribution to every field in the early days of missionary enterprise. Missions appealed to young people of that period and they made many sacrifices to support them. They sent missionaries to 

Jamaica, Mexico, and Cuba, and were responsible for the opening of the Boys School in Ramallah, Palestine. The church, in its young people's organizations, presented a challenging appeal to the world. One can hardly overestimate the influence of this youth group consecrated to the service of Jesus Christ.

We look at the past of Christian missions and wonder what the future holds in the light of the past. Mission Boards are faced with the fact that there is a decided slump in missionary giving, which indicates a comparable decline in missionary interest. No doubt many factors are responsible for this. 

The generous impulses and vision of the first quarter of the century suffered from the economic, social, and spiritual poverty which followed in the wake of the first world war. 

The large budgets of the early 1920's have never been approached since then. The strength of the men's movements in missions growing out of the Ecumenical Conference of 

1900 seems, at the moment, almost spent. An awareness of great areas of human need at home has made many people question the wisdom of sending missionaries abroad. And many people think of missions as they were carried on fifty years ago! They fail to realize that some of the most successful ventures in Christian comity and cooperation have been born on the mission field, and that mission churches and hospitals and schools, for the most part, can compare favorably with similar institutions in the homeland.

A Forward Look 259 Perhaps the chief factor in the decline of missionary interest, however, seems to be the crisis within the Christian Church itself. Great missionary movements do not depend on economic prosperity. The early Christians never stopped to count the cost. An utter disregard of self or property was characteristic of the first Quakers. The Friends of the Middle West who gave so generously to missions in the 1870's were largely hard-working pioneers. 

We must probe deeper if we would find the core. One of the chief criticisms of the church, today, is that it has lost its sense of mission; and it is only when a church is conscious of its mission that it is living and vital. If this is true, then the hope of the world is for the Church to regain its strength by awakening to the challenge of the present age.

The war has caused our world to shrink into a small neighborhood, a community of many peoples. It is obvious that economic and political alliances are inadequate, insecure, and temporary. Spiritual ties are the only ones on which mankind can hope to build an enduring peace and a community in which all men can live as brothers. Jesus, going about Palestine trying to bring men to God, making God known to them, and helping them to understand the Kingdom of Heaven, left his teachings to his followers, laying it upon them to carry them to all the world. His word is known in almost every nation on earth today, but the nations have failed to apply it to life. We need the teachings of Jesus today as much as we have ever needed them, and to share them and live them is the mission of the Church universal. 

There is hope in the world, today, for the Christian Church is concerned with its world task. The Society of Friends faces a new era in missions at a time when the need for its testimonies is very great. In a day when so much harshness and hate exist, there is need of "answering to that of God in every man; in a day when the tumult of war fills the land,

there is need of living in the virtue of that life and power that takes away the occasion of all wars;" in a day when there is much spiritual hunger, there is need of the message, "There is one, even Christ Jesus, who can speak to thy condition."

Friends have continuing responsibility for their special work at home and abroad. They have an ever greater one to meet the crisis of the age. We have recorded the story of how Friends in the past met great challenges and were faithful to tasks laid upon them. The future rests with those who follow in their train.

BOARD MEMBERS AND MISSIONARIES These lists contain: 

1. All the members who have served on the American 

Friends Board of Missions, and its predecessors, the American Friends Board of Foreign Missions and the Home Mis sion Board of the Five Years Meeting. 

2. A list of all the missionaries who have been employed by these boards and those who served on the seven fields now associated with the board previous to the organization of these boards, while the work on these fields was under the direction of certain yearly meetings and other organizations, as the Associated Executive Committee of Friends on Indian Affairs.

The records of some of these people are incomplete. The asterisk (*) means deceased.

The following principles have been followed in making up the list: 

1. Concerning board members: 

a. It includes all and only those who have been officially appointed to the American Friends Board of 

Missions, the American Friends Board of Foreign

Missions, and the Home Mission Board of the Five 

Years Meeting. 

b. The name used is that as of the time of appointment. c. The appointing agent is the yearly meeting or other body which first appointed the person on the board. 

2. Concerning the missionaries: 

a. The list includes all persons who have been officially appointed as missionaries on the seven fields now associated with the board, including all those who were appointed to these fields before the organization of the board. Distinction has been made between those who were officially employed by the Board and those who were employed as teachers by

schools under the general supervision of the board but not by the board itself. Southland Institute is an example. b. The names are as of the date of first appointment.

In case of subsequent change of name, the new one appears in parentheses. c. The dates of service state the year that the individual first arrived on the field and the year of final retirement from that field.
